    Medicine	Presentation	Description	Mechanism Of Action	Indications	Contraindications	Adverse Effects	Dosage And Administration	Drug Interactions	Geriatric Use	Pediatric Use	Pregnancy & Breastfeeding use	Overdose	Storage
1	Medicines used in anaesthesia													
1.1	General anaesthesia													
1.1.1	Intravenous and intramuscular anaesthetics													
1.1.1.1	Propofol	Emulsion for injection 10mg/ml	Short-acting intravenous sedative-hypnotic used for induction and maintenance of general anaesthesia and for sedation in ICU/procedural settings.	Propofol enhances inhibitory neurotransmission through positive allosteric modulation of GABA-A receptor channels, increasing chloride influx and causing neuronal hyperpolarization. It also decreases NMDA-mediated excitation and reduces cerebral metabolic rate of oxygen and intracranial pressure.	Induction and maintenance of general anesthesia, procedural sedation (endoscopy, cardioversion), ICU sedation, and control of refractory seizures.	Contraindicated in hypersensitivity to propofol, soybean or egg lecithin components, severe hemodynamic instability, and patients with disorders of fat metabolism. Use cautiously in hypovolemia, elderly, and airway-compromised patients.	Common adverse effects include hypotension, bradycardia, apnea, injection-site pain, and nausea. Less common but serious effects include propofol infusion syndrome (metabolic acidosis, rhabdomyolysis, hyperkalemia), pancreatitis, anaphylaxis, and refractory hypotension.	For induction: adults 1.5–2.5 mg/kg IV; maintenance 50–200 mcg/kg/min depending on anesthetic depth. ICU sedation: titrated infusion with daily awakening recommended. Adjust dose in elderly, hypovolemia, and cardiac dysfunction.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Propofol + Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol or Fentanyl (opioids in this table) → marked additive respiratory depression, apnea and hypotension; requires dose reduction and continuous monitoring. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Propofol + Midazolam, Diazepam, Lorazepam or Clonazepam (benzodiazepines in this table) → profound CNS depression and loss of airway reflexes; titrate both down and ensure airway readiness. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Propofol + Sevoflurane, Isoflurane or Halothane (volatile agents in this table) → additive myocardial depression and vasodilation with high risk of severe hypotension and bradycardia. [MODERATE-INTRA] Propofol + Suxamethonium, Atracurium, Cisatracurium or Rocuronium (neuromuscular blockers) → predictable facilitation of tracheal intubation but increased risk of unrecognized apnea if monitoring is inadequate. [MODERATE-INTRA] Propofol + Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol or Metoprolol (beta-blockers in this table) → exaggerated bradycardia and hypotension, particularly in hypovolemic or septic patients. [MODERATE-CLASS] Propofol + sedating antipsychotics or antihistamines in this table (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Promethazine, Chlorphenamine) → deeper sedation, delayed emergence and increased hypotension risk. [CRITICAL-OUT] Propofol + potent IV antiarrhythmics not yet in this dataset (e.g. high-dose IV amiodarone, sotalol) → may further depress AV conduction and contractility; mark as external until such drugs are added to the formulary.	Elderly patients have reduced clearance and greater hemodynamic sensitivity, requiring lower starting doses and slower titration.	Pediatric induction and maintenance are commonly used with weight-based infusions; neonates require cautious titration due to immature hepatic metabolism.	Pregnancy category B. Limited fetal toxicity; avoid for prolonged obstetric anesthesia due to neonatal hypotonia risk. Consider benefits versus risk in emergent procedures.	Overdose causes profound respiratory depression, hypotension, bradycardia, and loss of airway reflexes. Management includes airway support, vasopressors, fluid resuscitation, ECG monitoring, and discontinuation.	Store below 25°C. Avoid freezing. Protect from light and use aseptically due to risk of bacterial contamination.
1.1.1.2	Ketamine	Solution for injection 10mg/ml	Dissociative anaesthetic producing analgesia, amnesia and sedation with preserved airway reflexes at some doses.	Ketamine is a noncompetitive NMDA receptor antagonist that blocks excitatory glutamate transmission. It also interacts with opioid, monoamine, and muscarinic receptors and stimulates sympathetic outflow, increasing heart rate and blood pressure.	Induction of anesthesia, procedural sedation (burn dressing, fracture manipulation), pediatric sedation, reactive airway disease induction, trauma, severe pain, and status asthmaticus.	Contraindicated in severe uncontrolled hypertension, ischemic heart disease, raised intracranial pressure, psychosis, or schizophrenia history due to emergence delirium. Avoid in severe hepatic failure.	Adverse effects include tachycardia, hypertension, salivation, emergence reactions (vivid dreams, hallucinations), nausea, vomiting, increased intracranial pressure, laryngospasm, and diplopia. Rarely, cystitis with chronic use.	Induction dose: 1–2 mg/kg IV or 4–6 mg/kg IM. Analgesic doses 0.1–0.5 mg/kg IV. Pediatric procedural sedation: 1–1.5 mg/kg IV. Adjust for hepatic impairment.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Ketamine + Adrenaline (1 in 1000) or other sympathomimetics in this table used systemically for shock → excessive hypertension and tachycardia, increasing risk of myocardial ischemia in susceptible patients. [MODERATE-INTRA] Ketamine + Diazepam, Midazolam or Lorazepam → improved control of emergence reactions but additive respiratory depression in high doses; close monitoring needed. [MODERATE-INTRA] Ketamine + volatile anesthetics (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → enhanced anesthetic depth and sympathetic stimulation may be blunted, reducing the typical ketamine BP rise; adjust doses carefully. [MODERATE-CLASS] Ketamine + Tricyclic Antidepressants (Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine in this table) → further elevation in blood pressure and heart rate due to combined catecholamine effects. [CRITICAL-OUT] Ketamine + MAO inhibitors (not yet in this dataset) → potentially dangerous hypertensive crisis; flag as external until such drugs are added.	Elderly may develop prolonged recovery and delirium; titrate slowly with anxiolytics.	Safe in children for sedation and analgesia. Monitor airway during procedures.	Limited data in pregnancy; avoid prolonged or elective obstetric use unless emergent.	Overdose leads to apnea, hypertension, and prolonged sedation; airway support and benzodiazepines for agitation.	Store between 15–30°C, protected from light.
1.1.1.3	Etomidate	Powder for injection 1g and 500mg vials	Ultra-short-acting IV hypnotic used principally for induction of anaesthesia; minimal cardiovascular and respiratory depression relative to other induction agents.	Etomidate enhances GABA-A mediated inhibition without significant sympathetic suppression or myocardial depression.	Induction of anesthesia, rapid-sequence intubation, procedural sedation in hemodynamic instability.	Contraindicated in septic shock requiring steroid synthesis, known hypersensitivity, and porphyria. Avoid repeated dosing due to adrenal suppression.	Adverse effects include myoclonus, nausea, vomiting, adrenal suppression (via 11-beta-hydroxylase inhibition), injection pain, and hiccups.	Induction dose: 0.2–0.6 mg/kg IV. Avoid continuous infusion due to adrenal suppression.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Etomidate + strong vasodilators or negative inotropes from this table (e.g. Propofol, volatile anesthetics like Sevoflurane) is usually avoided because the combination negates etomidate’s cardiovascular advantage and may cause significant hypotension. [MODERATE-INTRA] Etomidate + Morphine, Fentanyl, Pethidine or Tramadol → additive respiratory depression but hemodynamics remain relatively stable compared to Propofol. [MODERATE-INTRA] Etomidate + Midazolam or Diazepam → increased risk of hypotension and prolonged sedation; dose adjustments required. [CRITICAL-CLASS] Etomidate + systemic corticosteroids from this table (Hydrocortisone, Prednisolone, Dexamethasone, Betamethasone) should prompt awareness of adrenal axis manipulation — etomidate suppresses endogenous cortisol while exogenous steroids compensate; timing and dosing should be coordinated in critically ill patients. [CRITICAL-OUT] Etomidate + potent CYP3A4 inducers not yet in this dataset (e.g. rifampicin as a standalone, certain anticonvulsants) may reduce etomidate levels and duration; flag as external class until added.	Elderly need lower dosing due to reduced clearance and exaggerated effects.	Safe in pediatric induction with weight-based dosing; avoid repeated doses due to adrenal suppression.	Avoid prolonged use in pregnancy; single induction dose acceptable for emergency intubation.	Overdose causes respiratory arrest, hypotension, and prolonged sedation. Treatment includes airway control and hemodynamic monitoring.	Store at room temperature and protect from light.
1.1.1.4	Thiopental sodium	Powder for injection 500mg vials	Ultra-short-acting barbiturate historically used for intravenous induction of anaesthesia and for control of intracranial pressure or status epilepticus (IV formulations).	Thiopental enhances GABA-mediated chloride influx, decreasing neuronal excitability. It also decreases cerebral metabolic rate and intracranial pressure.	Induction of anesthesia, control of intracranial pressure, refractory seizures, and short procedural anesthesia.	Contraindicated in porphyria, severe respiratory depression, shock, and hypersensitivity to barbiturates. Caution in asthma, renal impairment, and hypovolemia.	Hypotension, apnea, bronchospasm, laryngospasm, prolonged recovery, paradoxical excitation, and dose-dependent respiratory depression.	Induction dose: 3–5 mg/kg IV for adults. Use reduced dose in elderly and hypovolemia. Avoid continuous infusion.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Thiopental + opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl) or benzodiazepines (Midazolam, Diazepam, Lorazepam, Clonazepam) in this table → profound respiratory and CNS depression; requires full monitoring and airway management. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Thiopental + Propofol or volatile agents (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → profound hypotension and myocardial depression, especially in hypovolemic or cardiac patients. [MODERATE-INTRA] Thiopental + antihypertensives in this table (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Nifedipine, Amlodipine, Hydralazine, ACE inhibitors, ARBs) → exaggerated hypotension during induction. [CRITICAL-OUT] Thiopental + other CYP inducers not in this dataset (e.g. carbamazepine, rifampicin as single drug) → reduced hypnotic effect over time; mark as external.	Elderly require dose reductions due to slower elimination.	Not recommended in pediatric procedural sedation except expert supervision.	Contraindicated in pregnancy unless emergent; neonatal respiratory depression possible.	Overdose causes severe respiratory depression and cardiovascular collapse requiring airway support and vasopressors.	Store dry at controlled temperature; reconstitute freshly and use aseptically.
1.1.2	Volatile inhalation anaesthetics													
1.1.2.1	Sevoflurane	Inhalation liquid 250ml	Volatile halogenated anesthetic with rapid onset and offset, non-pungent odor, and smooth induction and emergence, especially useful in pediatric anesthesia and day-case surgery. Provides hypnosis, amnesia and some muscle relaxation but limited analgesia.	Acts primarily by enhancing GABA-A receptor activity and modulating other ion channels (e.g. two-pore-domain potassium channels) to decrease neuronal excitability. Reduces cerebral metabolic rate, causes dose-dependent vasodilation and hypotension while generally preserving cardiac output better than older agents.	Induction and maintenance of general anesthesia in adults and children; inhalational induction where IV access is difficult; ambulatory and short procedures requiring rapid recovery.	Known or suspected susceptibility to malignant hyperthermia; concurrent use in patients with a history of severe hepatic dysfunction after halogenated anesthetics; raised intracranial pressure without secure airway and controlled ventilation; severe uncorrected hypovolemia. Use caution in patients with underlying renal impairment due to inorganic fluoride production.	Common: hypotension, bradycardia or tachycardia, respiratory depression, emergence agitation (especially in children), nausea, vomiting. Less common: arrhythmias, laryngospasm, bronchospasm. Rare but serious: malignant hyperthermia, hepatic dysfunction, seizures in predisposed patients and potential nephrotoxicity with very prolonged high-dose exposure.	Delivered via vaporizer; MAC (minimum alveolar concentration) approximately 2% in adults. Induction often 5–8% in oxygen or oxygen/air, then maintenance 1–3% depending on age, concomitant agents and surgical stimulus. Elderly and debilitated patients require lower MAC. Always used with airway management and ventilation support as needed.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Sevoflurane + Suxamethonium (depolarizing neuromuscular blocker) → increased risk of malignant hyperthermia in susceptible patients; prompt use of Dantrolene (not in current table) is required if MH occurs. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Sevoflurane + Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam or Diazepam → profound CNS and respiratory depression, hypotension and bradycardia; carefully titrate doses. [MODERATE-INTRA] Sevoflurane + non-depolarizing neuromuscular blockers (Atracurium, Cisatracurium, Rocuronium) → potentiation of neuromuscular blockade; requires lower neuromuscular blocker doses and neuromuscular monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Sevoflurane + beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) or calcium channel blockers (Nifedipine, Verapamil, Amlodipine) → additive myocardial depression and bradycardia. [CRITICAL-CLASS] Sevoflurane with other volatile agents (Isoflurane, Halothane) should not be co-administered except during transitions due to overlapping cardiodepressant and arrhythmogenic effects. [CRITICAL-OUT] Sevoflurane + catecholamine-sensitizing agents not yet in dataset (e.g. some older sympathomimetics) → arrhythmia risk; mark as external.	Elderly require reduced MAC (often 1–1.5%) and slower titration due to increased sensitivity to hypotension and postoperative cognitive dysfunction; consider lower target concentrations and careful emergence monitoring.	Sevoflurane is widely used in children for inhalational induction due to non-pungency. Monitor for higher rates of emergence agitation and treat with adequate analgesia and possible small benzodiazepine doses. Use caution in repeated exposures in very young children, though short-term use is generally safe.	Use in pregnancy is generally restricted to necessary surgical procedures; sevoflurane crosses the placenta but is usually considered acceptable for short emergency surgeries under careful monitoring. Avoid prolonged high-dose exposure near term due to potential uterine relaxation.	Overdose results in deep anesthesia with severe hypotension, respiratory arrest and risk of malignant hyperthermia. Management includes discontinuation of the agent, 100% oxygen, controlled ventilation, hemodynamic support with fluids and vasopressors, active treatment of malignant hyperthermia (dantrolene, cooling, correcting acidosis and hyperkalemia) if present.	Store at room temperature; keep bottles tightly closed. Use only with calibrated vaporizers designed for sevoflurane. Protect from excessive heat and follow manufacturer’s expiry recommendations.
1.1.2.2	Isoflurane	Inhalation liquid 250ml	Potent volatile anesthetic used mainly for maintenance of anesthesia. It causes dose-dependent hypotension predominantly via systemic vasodilation, with relatively preserved cardiac output and good muscle relaxation. Pungent odor limits its use for inhalational induction.	Enhances inhibitory GABAergic neurotransmission, modulates glycine receptors and activates potassium channels, reducing neuronal excitability. Reduces systemic vascular resistance, modestly decreases myocardial contractility, and reduces cerebral metabolic rate while maintaining or modestly increasing intracranial pressure without hyperventilation.	Maintenance of general anesthesia for a wide variety of surgical procedures in adults and children after IV or inhalational induction with other agents; useful when controlled hypotension is desired (neurosurgery, major vascular surgery).	Known or suspected malignant hyperthermia susceptibility; severe hypovolemia; suspected or confirmed increased intracranial pressure without airway and ventilation management; severe coronary artery disease when uncontrolled hypotension could compromise myocardial perfusion.	Common: dose-dependent hypotension, respiratory depression, tachycardia. Less common: arrhythmias, shivering, nausea, vomiting, delirium. Rare: malignant hyperthermia, hepatic dysfunction, coronary steal phenomenon in patients with critical coronary stenosis.	Used via dedicated vaporizer; MAC about 1.2% in adults. Maintenance usually 0.5–2% in oxygen/air or oxygen/nitrous oxide mixtures. Elderly and debilitated patients require lower concentrations. Co-administer opioids and muscle relaxants (atracurium, cisatracurium, rocuronium) to reduce anesthetic requirements.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Isoflurane + Suxamethonium → increased risk of malignant hyperthermia in susceptible patients. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Isoflurane + Propofol, opioids (Morphine, Fentanyl, Pethidine, Tramadol) and benzodiazepines (Midazolam, Diazepam, Lorazepam, Clonazepam) → synergistic CNS and cardiorespiratory depression. [MODERATE-INTRA] Isoflurane + Atracurium, Cisatracurium or Rocuronium → potentiation of neuromuscular blockade requiring dose reductions. [MODERATE-INTRA] Isoflurane + beta-blockers and calcium channel blockers in this table → bradycardia and hypotension. [CRITICAL-CLASS] Should not be combined with other volatile agents (Sevoflurane, Halothane) except during transitions in small overlapping doses. [CRITICAL-OUT] Isoflurane + high-dose exogenous catecholamines not in dataset (e.g. certain inotropes not listed) → arrhythmia risk; mark external.	Elderly often have diminished cardiovascular reserve and increased sensitivity; use lower MAC and titrate slowly to avoid hypotension and postoperative delirium.	Used in children predominantly for maintenance, not induction, due to pungency. Dosing is age-adjusted (higher MAC in children). Adequate analgesia and antiemetic prophylaxis are important due to high incidence of postoperative nausea/vomiting.	Generally reserved for necessary non-obstetric surgery in pregnancy under expert care. Potential for uterine relaxation with high concentrations; minimize dose near term and monitor fetal well-being where appropriate.	Overdose presents with profound hypotension, respiratory arrest and potentially malignant hyperthermia. Discontinue agent, ventilate with 100% oxygen, support blood pressure with fluids and vasopressors, and treat malignant hyperthermia if suspected.	Store at room temperature in tightly closed containers. Use only in vaporizers calibrated for isoflurane. Avoid extreme temperatures and follow manufacturer stability guidance.
1.1.2.3	Halothane	Inhalation liquid 250ml	Older halogenated volatile anesthetic with potent hypnotic effects and strong myocardial depressant and arrhythmogenic properties. Use has declined globally due to risk of severe hepatotoxicity (halothane hepatitis) but may still be present in low-resource settings.	Enhances GABA-A receptor–mediated inhibition and depresses sodium and calcium channel function. Causes significant myocardial depression and sensitizes the myocardium to catecholamines, increasing risk of arrhythmias. It reduces cerebral metabolic rate but can increase intracranial pressure without hyperventilation.	Induction and maintenance of general anesthesia in adults and children where alternative agents are unavailable; pediatric inhalational induction in resource-limited settings.	Known susceptibility to malignant hyperthermia; history of unexplained hepatic dysfunction after halothane (suspected halothane hepatitis); severe hepatic insufficiency; severe cardiac disease with risk of arrhythmias; increased intracranial pressure without adequate ventilation control.	Common: hypotension, bradycardia or arrhythmias, respiratory depression. Less common: postoperative shivering, nausea, vomiting. Serious: halothane hepatitis (immune-mediated hepatocellular necrosis occurring after repeated exposures), malignant hyperthermia, severe ventricular arrhythmias especially with catecholamines.	Delivered via halothane-specific vaporizer, typically up to 0.5–1.5% for maintenance; 1–2% for induction when used. Avoid repeated anesthetics with halothane within short intervals (e.g. <3 months) to reduce hepatitis risk. Carefully titrate in cardiac patients and continuous ECG monitoring is essential.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Halothane + Adrenaline injections from this table (used for anaphylaxis/shock) → high risk of ventricular arrhythmias due to myocardial sensitization; intravascular adrenaline doses must be minimized and diluted, and alternative vasopressors considered. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Halothane + Suxamethonium → high malignant hyperthermia risk in susceptible patients. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Halothane + Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl or benzodiazepines → profound hypotension and bradycardia; titrate doses cautiously. [MODERATE-INTRA] Halothane + beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) → attenuated tachycardic response to hypotension, increasing risk of severe bradycardia and AV block. [CRITICAL-OUT] Halothane + isoniazid monotherapy (not specifically listed alone here, but part of anti-TB FDCs) historically linked to higher hepatotoxic risk — flag as external detail to consider when more granular TB regimens are added.	Elderly have increased sensitivity to myocardial depression and hypotension and are at higher risk for postoperative cognitive dysfunction; lower concentrations and careful hemodynamic monitoring are mandatory.	Halothane has historically been widely used in children for inhalational induction but is now largely replaced by sevoflurane. Children may exhibit increased risk of arrhythmias and postoperative liver enzyme elevations with repeated exposures.	Use in pregnancy is limited to situations where other agents are not available; it can cause uterine relaxation at higher concentrations and crosses the placenta, with risk of fetal depression. Avoid repeated use during pregnancy.	Overdose causes profound hypotension, severe arrhythmias, respiratory arrest and potential malignant hyperthermia. Manage by discontinuing agent, ventilating with 100% oxygen, supporting circulation with fluids and vasopressors, treating arrhythmias and initiating malignant hyperthermia protocol when indicated.	Store at room temperature in amber glass bottles to protect from light. Use only in vaporizers calibrated for halothane. Avoid repeated opening and contamination; monitor expiry dates because halothane decomposes over time.
1.1.2.4	Nitrous oxide	Inhalation gas	Inhaled anesthetic gas with weak anesthetic but potent analgesic properties. Rapid onset and offset due to low blood-gas solubility, used as an adjunct to more potent anesthetics or as self-administered analgesia in labor and minor procedures.	Acts primarily as a noncompetitive NMDA receptor antagonist with effects on other ligand-gated ion channels. Provides analgesia and mild sedation with minimal direct cardiovascular depression. Diffuses rapidly into air-filled spaces and can expand closed cavities.	Adjunct to other anesthetics during general anesthesia; inhaled analgesia for labor, dental procedures and minor painful interventions; used to reduce requirements for more potent anesthetics and opioids.	Pneumothorax, bowel obstruction, middle ear surgery or infection, intracranial air (e.g. following neurosurgery), severe COPD or bullous lung disease (risk of gas expansion), severe vitamin B12 deficiency or disorders of methionine synthase. Caution in patients at risk of increased intracranial pressure.	Common: dizziness, euphoria, nausea, vomiting. Less common: diffusion hypoxia if oxygen supplementation is not provided at discontinuation. Chronic exposure (e.g. occupational) may cause megaloblastic anemia, neuropathy and bone marrow suppression via B12 inactivation.	Typically administered as 30–70% nitrous oxide in oxygen via demand-valve systems or anesthetic circuit. For labor and minor procedures, self-administered 50% nitrous oxide/oxygen mixes are common. Always ensure at least 30% oxygen is given and administer 100% oxygen for several minutes after discontinuation to prevent diffusion hypoxia.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Nitrous oxide + other anesthetics in this table (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane, Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam) → deepened sedation/anesthesia, requiring dose adjustments and close monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Nitrous oxide + Methotrexate (from your antineoplastic list) → both can affect folate/B12 pathways, theoretically increasing neurotoxicity risk with prolonged or repeated exposure. [CRITICAL-OUT] Chronic nitrous oxide misuse with no medical supervision plus pre-existing B12 deficiency (external context) → subacute combined degeneration; mark as external misuse scenario rather than standard use.	In elderly patients, consider reduced exposure and monitor for postoperative confusion or delirium. Chronic or repeated exposures should prompt assessment of B12 status, particularly in undernourished or malabsorptive patients.	Used extensively in pediatrics for painful procedures and as an adjunct anesthetic. Contraindications related to air-space expansion (pneumothorax, bowel obstruction, otologic disease) are particularly important in children. Self-administration should be supervised closely.	Short-term use for labor or emergency surgery is generally considered safe in pregnancy when oxygenation is maintained. Avoid prolonged or repeated exposures that may impact maternal B12 stores.	Overdose primarily presents as hypoxia due to displacement of oxygen, not intrinsic toxicity. Management is immediate discontinuation of nitrous oxide and administration of 100% oxygen with monitoring. Evaluate for gas trapping complications (pneumothorax) if clinically indicated.	Store in high-pressure cylinders, clearly labeled, and secured upright. Protect from extreme heat. Use appropriate regulators and ensure pipeline or cylinder systems are regularly inspected for leaks.
1.1.3	Muscle relaxant													
1.1.3.1	Suxamethonium	Injection 50mg/Ml	Depolarizing neuromuscular blocking agent used to produce rapid, short-lived skeletal muscle paralysis, primarily for rapid sequence intubation and brief procedures. Causes initial muscle fasciculations followed by flaccid paralysis.	Acts as a nicotinic acetylcholine receptor agonist at the neuromuscular junction, causing persistent depolarization of the motor end-plate. This leads to transient fasciculations followed by flaccid paralysis until the drug is hydrolyzed by plasma pseudocholinesterase. Unlike non-depolarizing blockers, it is not reversed by anticholinesterases.	Facilitation of rapid sequence endotracheal intubation; provision of muscle relaxation for short procedures (e.g. ECT) where brief paralysis is needed.	Personal or family history of malignant hyperthermia; known pseudocholinesterase deficiency; pre-existing hyperkalemia; major burns, severe trauma, neuromuscular diseases (e.g. muscular dystrophy, motor neuron disease) after the acute phase; severe sepsis with muscle denervation; history of suxamethonium-induced hyperkalemic cardiac arrest.	Common: muscle fasciculations, postoperative myalgia, mild rise in serum potassium. Serious: malignant hyperthermia (especially when used with volatile agents); severe hyperkalemia leading to arrhythmias or cardiac arrest in susceptible patients; bradycardia or asystole (particularly with repeat doses in children); increased intraocular, intragastric and intracranial pressure; prolonged apnea in pseudocholinesterase deficiency.	Typical adult dose: 1–1.5 mg/kg IV for rapid sequence intubation (onset 30–60 seconds, duration 5–10 minutes). Pediatric IV dose: 1–2 mg/kg; if IV access is not available, 3–4 mg/kg IM may be used. Avoid repeated dosing where possible; for longer procedures use non-depolarizing agents (atracurium, cisatracurium, rocuronium).	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Suxamethonium + volatile anesthetics (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → markedly increased risk of malignant hyperthermia in susceptible individuals. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Suxamethonium + Adrenaline (given IV for shock) → risk of severe arrhythmias in setting of hyperkalemia. [MODERATE-INTRA] Suxamethonium + Digoxin or other cardiotonic agents (Digoxin in this table) → augmented arrhythmia risk if potassium rises significantly. [MODERATE-CLASS] Suxamethonium + diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorthiazide, Mannitol) or laxatives (Senna, Bisacodyl) that alter potassium can complicate peri-induction electrolyte balance. [CRITICAL-OUT] Suxamethonium + potassium-sparing agents not yet in dataset (e.g. high-dose spironolactone if more widely used) → higher hyperkalemia risk; mark as external interaction.	Elderly patients may have reduced pseudocholinesterase activity and increased risk of prolonged paralysis; careful dose titration, neuromuscular monitoring and ready availability of ventilatory support are essential.	In children, suxamethonium can precipitate severe hyperkalemic cardiac arrest in undiagnosed myopathies (e.g. Duchenne muscular dystrophy). Its use is therefore restricted to life-threatening airway emergencies and rapid-sequence intubation where alternatives are not feasible; non-depolarizing agents are preferred when time permits.	Use during pregnancy is usually limited to obstetric emergencies requiring rapid sequence induction. Plasma pseudocholinesterase is reduced in pregnancy, so duration of paralysis may be modestly prolonged. Drug crosses the placenta minimally but may cause transient neonatal apnea if large doses are used close to delivery.	Overdose manifests as prolonged apnea, paralysis of respiratory muscles, bradyarrhythmias or asystole and hyperkalemia. Management includes immediate ventilatory support with 100% oxygen, continuous neuromuscular monitoring, treatment of bradycardia with atropine (if available) or other atropine-equivalent agents, aggressive management of hyperkalemia (calcium, insulin-glucose, bicarbonate) and malignant hyperthermia protocol if indicated.	Store at 2–8°C (refrigerated) where recommended; protect from light. Some formulations may be stable at room temperature for limited periods—follow manufacturer guidance. Discard if solution is discolored or contains particulate matter.
1.1.3.2	Atracurium	Injection 10mg/ml	Intermediate-acting non-depolarizing neuromuscular blocking agent used to facilitate endotracheal intubation and provide skeletal muscle relaxation during surgery or mechanical ventilation. Undergoes Hofmann elimination and ester hydrolysis, making it useful in organ failure.	Competitively antagonizes acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors in the neuromuscular junction, preventing depolarization and muscle contraction. Does not cross the blood-brain barrier or cause unconsciousness or analgesia; must be used with adequate sedation and analgesia.	Adjunct to general anesthesia to facilitate tracheal intubation and provide muscle relaxation during surgery and in mechanically ventilated patients in ICU, particularly in those with hepatic or renal impairment.	Known hypersensitivity to atracurium or other neuromuscular blockers; history of severe anaphylaxis to NMBAs. Use with caution in severe asthma, cardiovascular instability and in patients with neuromuscular disorders or burns (altered sensitivity).	Common: dose-related skeletal muscle paralysis, transient hypotension, flushing and tachycardia due to histamine release with rapid bolus. Less common: bronchospasm, especially in reactive airways. Rare: anaphylaxis, prolonged paralysis, seizures related to laudanosine (a metabolite) in very high or prolonged infusions.	Intubating dose: 0.4–0.5 mg/kg IV; maintenance: 0.1–0.2 mg/kg IV boluses or continuous infusion (e.g. 5–10 mcg/kg/min) guided by neuromuscular monitoring. Onset 2–3 minutes, duration 20–35 minutes. Dose adjustments needed in severe acid-base disturbances and hypothermia due to changes in Hofmann elimination.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Atracurium + volatile anesthetics (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → potentiated neuromuscular blockade; lower doses required and neuromuscular monitoring recommended. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Atracurium + Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam or Diazepam → profound CNS depression plus paralysis; apnea is guaranteed and ventilation must be controlled. [MODERATE-INTRA] Atracurium + Aminoglycosides (Gentamicin, Amikacin) or Magnesium sulphate (from obstetric list) → enhanced neuromuscular block and delayed recovery. [MODERATE-CLASS] Atracurium + other non-depolarizing agents (Cisatracurium, Rocuronium) → increased magnitude and duration of blockade; cross-titration should be guided by nerve stimulator. [CRITICAL-OUT] Atracurium + certain antibiotics not yet in dataset (e.g. high-dose polymyxins) can further prolong paralysis; mark as external until added.	Elderly may have slightly prolonged onset and duration due to decreased cardiac output and changes in volume of distribution, but organ-independent Hofmann elimination means dose adjustment is usually modest; neuromuscular monitoring is strongly recommended.	Use in children is common; onset may be faster and duration shorter in young children, so careful titration and monitoring are required. Neonates have increased sensitivity; specialized dosing and monitoring should be used.	Atracurium can be used in pregnancy when indicated, such as for cesarean section under general anesthesia. It does not cross the placenta significantly and has minimal effect on the fetus when properly dosed and when maternal oxygenation and hemodynamics are maintained.	Overdose leads to prolonged neuromuscular paralysis and apnea. Management includes mechanical ventilation, sedation, monitoring of neuromuscular function and, once spontaneous recovery begins, reversal with neostigmine plus an antimuscarinic agent like glycopyrronium to prevent bradycardia. In massive overdose, recovery may be delayed for hours but Hofmann elimination continues independently of renal or hepatic function.	Store refrigerated (2–8°C) and protect from light. Atracurium is chemically unstable at higher temperatures; some formulations may be kept at room temperature for limited periods—follow manufacturer’s recommendations strictly.
1.1.3.3	Cisatracurium	Injection 2mg/ml or 5mg/ml (5ml/10ml); Lyophilized powder for reconstitution 20mg or 50mg	Stereoisomer of atracurium with similar intermediate duration but less histamine release, resulting in more stable hemodynamics. Organ-independent Hofmann elimination makes it suitable in hepatic and renal failure and for prolonged ICU use.	Competitive antagonist at nicotinic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction, preventing acetylcholine-mediated depolarization and muscle contraction. Undergoes spontaneous Hofmann elimination and ester hydrolysis; metabolite laudanosine has theoretical proconvulsant properties at high concentrations.	Muscle relaxation during surgery; facilitation of mechanical ventilation in ICU patients, especially with multi-organ failure; alternative to atracurium when histamine-related hypotension or bronchospasm is a concern.	Hypersensitivity to cisatracurium or other NMBAs. Use cautiously in neuromuscular disorders (myasthenia gravis), severe electrolyte abnormalities, burns and prolonged immobilization (altered sensitivity).	Common: predictable skeletal muscle paralysis. Less common: mild hypotension, flushing. Rare: bronchospasm, anaphylaxis, prolonged neuromuscular block particularly with prolonged infusions, especially in acidemia or hypothermia.	Intubation dose: 0.15–0.2 mg/kg IV; maintenance: 0.03 mg/kg boluses or continuous infusion (e.g. 1–4 mcg/kg/min) with neuromuscular monitoring. Onset 2–3 minutes; duration 30–40 minutes. Adjust infusion rates based on train-of-four monitoring, particularly in ICU use.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Cisatracurium + volatile anesthetics (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → potentiated neuromuscular blockade; reduce dose and monitor closely. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Cisatracurium + Propofol, opioids (Morphine, Fentanyl, Pethidine, Tramadol) or benzodiazepines (Midazolam, Diazepam) → complete loss of spontaneous ventilation; ensure controlled ventilation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Cisatracurium + Aminoglycosides (Gentamicin, Amikacin) or Magnesium sulphate → prolonged and deepened block. [MODERATE-CLASS] Cisatracurium + other non-depolarizing blockers (Atracurium, Rocuronium) → cumulative blockade requiring dose adjustment. [CRITICAL-OUT] As with other NMBs, concurrent use with external drugs such as high-dose polymyxins can further prolong paralysis; mark as external.	Elderly may experience slightly prolonged onset and duration but Hoffmann elimination minimizes accumulation; dosing is largely similar but guided closely by neuromuscular monitoring. Risk of postoperative pulmonary complications from residual block is higher; ensure complete reversal.	Used safely in children including infants for surgery and ICU ventilation with weight-based dosing; onset can be faster and duration shorter in young children; vigilant monitoring is essential.	Can be used during pregnancy for cesarean section under general anesthesia with minimal placental transfer. Fetal effects are usually limited if maternal oxygenation and blood pressure are maintained.	Overdose results in prolonged neuromuscular paralysis and apnea. Provide full ventilatory support, sedation, and monitor neuromuscular function. Allow spontaneous recovery with time due to Hofmann elimination; pharmacologic reversal (neostigmine with glycopyrronium) is used when recovery is evident and vital signs are stable.	Store refrigerated (2–8°C) and protect from light. Some formulations may allow limited room-temperature storage; follow manufacturer guidance regarding stability after removal from the refrigerator and after reconstitution of lyophilized powders.
1.1.3.4	Rocuronium	Injection 10mg/ml or 50mg/5ml (5ml or 10ml); Lyophilized powder for reconstitution: 50mg or 100mg	Intermediate-acting non-depolarizing aminosteroid neuromuscular blocker with relatively rapid onset, often used as an alternative to suxamethonium for rapid sequence intubation.	Competitive antagonist of acetylcholine at nicotinic receptors of the neuromuscular junction, preventing depolarization and subsequent muscle contraction. Onset of paralysis is faster than many other non-depolarizers, especially at higher doses. Primarily eliminated hepatically and biliary with some renal contribution.	Facilitation of rapid sequence intubation when suxamethonium is contraindicated; maintenance of muscle relaxation during surgery and mechanical ventilation in ICU.	Hypersensitivity to rocuronium or other NMBAs. Use cautiously in severe hepatic or biliary disease, significant renal impairment, neuromuscular disorders, extensive burns and conditions with altered sensitivity (electrolyte disturbances, acid-base disorders).	Common: dose-dependent skeletal muscle paralysis. Less common: mild tachycardia, hypotension. Rare but important: anaphylaxis, bronchospasm, prolonged neuromuscular block especially in hepatic/renal dysfunction or with prolonged infusion.	Intubation dose: 0.6–1.2 mg/kg IV, higher end (1–1.2 mg/kg) for rapid sequence intubation; onset 45–90 seconds. Maintenance: 0.15 mg/kg boluses or continuous infusion (e.g. 5–12 mcg/kg/min) with neuromuscular monitoring. Recovery can be reversed with neostigmine plus glycopyrronium when patient shows spontaneous recovery; specific reversal with sugammadex (if available in future) is more predictable but is not in your current formulary list.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Rocuronium + volatile anesthetics (Sevoflurane, Isoflurane, Halothane) → potentiated neuromuscular blockade; reduce doses and monitor. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Rocuronium + Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam or Diazepam → complete paralysis with deep sedation/anesthesia; mechanical ventilation mandatory. [MODERATE-INTRA] Rocuronium + Aminoglycosides (Gentamicin, Amikacin) or Magnesium sulphate → prolonged blockade. [MODERATE-CLASS] Rocuronium + other non-depolarizing blockers (Atracurium, Cisatracurium) → cumulative block; require nerve stimulator guidance. [CRITICAL-OUT] In external contexts, specific reversal agent Sugammadex is used for Rocuronium reversal where available; not currently in your drug list but highly relevant for future formulary expansion.	In elderly patients, hepatic blood flow and renal function may be reduced, slightly prolonging rocuronium duration; doses should be titrated carefully with neuromuscular monitoring and full reversal before extubation.	Widely used in children as an intubating agent and for maintenance of relaxation; onset and duration are age-dependent (often faster onset but shorter duration in young children). Neonates require specialized dosing and monitoring. Always provide adequate sedation/analgesia since rocuronium has no CNS effects.	Rocuronium can be used in pregnancy (including cesarean section) as an intubating muscle relaxant; it does not significantly cross the placenta when used appropriately. Neonatal muscle tone is usually preserved if maternal ventilation and hemodynamics are stable.	Overdose leads to prolonged neuromuscular paralysis and apnea. Management consists of mechanical ventilation, adequate sedation, and neuromuscular monitoring until recovery. Neostigmine plus glycopyrronium can be administered once recovery has begun. In high-dose ICU exposure, recovery may be delayed especially in hepatic or renal dysfunction.	Store at 2–8°C; some formulations are stable at room temperature for a limited time—follow manufacturer instructions. Protect from light and discard if solution appears discolored or contains particulates.
1.2	Local anaesthetics													
1.2.1	Lignocaine	Solution for injection 2% w/v, 20mls	Widely used amide-type local anesthetic and class Ib antiarrhythmic. When used for local anesthesia, it provides rapid-onset sensory and motor block. It is also used for nerve blocks, infiltration, and as an antiarrhythmic in ventricular arrhythmias (IV, not the 2% vial specifically).	Blocks voltage-gated sodium channels in neuronal cell membranes, stabilizing the membrane and preventing initiation and conduction of nerve impulses. At higher systemic concentrations, it can depress cardiac conduction and excitability (antiarrhythmic effect), but excessive levels can cause CNS and cardiovascular toxicity.	Local infiltration anesthesia, peripheral nerve blocks, spinal and epidural anesthesia (depending on formulation), minor surgical and dental procedures, and as adjunct to procedures requiring localized analgesia.	Hypersensitivity to amide-type local anesthetics; severe heart block without pacemaker; porphyria (relative). Caution in severe hepatic impairment, heart failure and in patients receiving other local anesthetics or class I antiarrhythmics.	Local: pain on injection, transient numbness, prolonged sensory or motor block. Systemic (with intravascular injection or overdose): perioral tingling, tinnitus, metallic taste, dizziness, agitation, seizures, CNS depression, hypotension, bradycardia, arrhythmias and cardiac arrest.	Maximum dose for plain lignocaine (without adrenaline) is typically 3–5 mg/kg (up to about 200–300 mg in adults), respecting local guidelines. For infiltration: inject slowly with frequent aspiration to avoid intravascular injection. Onset is rapid (2–5 minutes) with duration 1–2 hours depending on dose and tissue vascularity.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Lignocaine (systemically absorbed) + other local anesthetics in this table (Bupivacaine, Bupivacaine with glucose, Lidocaine in hemorrhoid combination products) → additive risk of local anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST). [CRITICAL-INTRA] Lignocaine + Amiodarone (if added later) or Digoxin (antiarrhythmic/cardiac glycoside) → increased risk of conduction disturbances and bradyarrhythmias; Digoxin is present in this table and requires ECG monitoring if systemic Lignocaine doses are high. [MODERATE-INTRA] Lignocaine + beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) or calcium channel blockers (Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) → reduced clearance and additive negative inotropy, increasing risk of bradycardia and hypotension. [MODERATE-CLASS] Sedatives and opioids in this table (Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam, Diazepam) can mask early CNS signs of toxicity, delaying detection. [CRITICAL-OUT] Lignocaine + strong CYP3A4 inhibitors not yet in your dataset (e.g. some azole antifungals like voriconazole) may increase systemic levels in prolonged infusions; mark as external.	Elderly may have reduced hepatic clearance and increased sensitivity to CNS and cardiac toxicity; maximum doses should be at the lower end of recommended range and incremental dosing with aspiration is recommended.	Lignocaine is frequently and safely used in children for local anesthesia at weight-adjusted doses; children are more vulnerable to toxicity from dosing errors, so careful calculation of mg/kg and use of dilute solutions is essential.	Local lignocaine use is considered safe in pregnancy when used in recommended doses, including for obstetric procedures and perineal repair. It crosses the placenta but at usual doses significant fetal toxicity is uncommon.	Overdose or inadvertent intravascular injection leads to CNS excitation (tremor, seizures) followed by CNS depression and cardiovascular collapse (hypotension, bradycardia, arrhythmias). Immediate management includes stopping injection, airway support, benzodiazepines for seizures and, in severe local anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST), 20% lipid emulsion therapy as per protocol, alongside ACLS measures.	Store at room temperature protected from light. Do not use if discolored or containing particulate matter. Single-use vials should be discarded after opening according to local policy.
1.2.2	Lignocaine with Adrenaline dental	Solution for injection	Dental local anesthetic preparation combining lignocaine with low-dose adrenaline (epinephrine) to prolong duration and reduce systemic absorption by vasoconstriction, providing deeper and longer analgesia with less bleeding in the operative field.	Lignocaine blocks voltage-gated sodium channels in neuronal membranes, preventing nerve impulse conduction. Adrenaline stimulates alpha-1 adrenergic receptors locally, producing vasoconstriction that decreases systemic absorption, prolongs anesthetic effect and reduces bleeding. Systemic beta effects may occur if absorbed, potentially affecting heart rate and blood pressure.	Local anesthesia for dental procedures including extractions, restorations and minor oral surgery where prolonged duration and hemostasis are desired.	Hypersensitivity to lignocaine or other amide-type local anesthetics; severe uncontrolled hypertension; significant ischemic heart disease, recent myocardial infarction, uncontrolled arrhythmias; hyperthyroidism; narrow-angle glaucoma. Avoid injection into areas with end-arterial blood supply where vasoconstriction may cause ischemia.	Local: injection-site pain, prolonged numbness, rare tissue ischemia if injected intravascularly or into closed compartments. Systemic: from adrenaline—tachycardia, palpitations, hypertension, anxiety, tremor; from lignocaine—CNS toxicity and cardiotoxicity at high systemic levels.	Typical dental cartridge contains 2% lignocaine with 1:80,000–1:100,000 adrenaline; adult maximum dose often around 7 mg/kg of lignocaine with adrenaline, but limit total volume particularly in patients with cardiovascular disease. Always aspirate before injection and use the lowest effective dose.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Combination contains Adrenaline, so caution with Halothane anesthesia (marked arrhythmia risk) and with Suxamethonium in patients with hyperkalemia risk. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Systemic Lidocaine effects additive with other local anesthetics (plain Lignocaine, Bupivacaine) and may increase risk of LAST. [MODERATE-INTRA] Adrenaline component may interact with beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) leading to unopposed alpha vasoconstriction and exaggerated hypertension. [CRITICAL-CLASS] Use carefully in patients receiving MAO inhibitors or tricyclic antidepressants (Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine) due to enhanced catecholamine response. [CRITICAL-OUT] Concomitant cocaine use (not in this dataset) plus Adrenaline is extremely arrhythmogenic; mark as external.	Elderly with cardiovascular disease (ischemic heart disease, heart failure, atrial fibrillation) are at higher risk from adrenaline’s systemic effects; use minimal effective volumes and lower concentration of vasoconstrictor when possible.	Safe in children when dosed strictly by weight and using pediatric-appropriate cartridges; extra care in small children to avoid overdose and intravascular injection.	Lignocaine with adrenaline is generally considered safe for dental procedures in pregnancy when needed; avoid large doses and intravascular injection. Some guidelines prefer limiting adrenaline use in pregnant patients with significant cardiovascular disease or preeclampsia.	Overdose or inadvertent intravascular injection may present with CNS symptoms (tinnitus, metallic taste, seizures), followed by hypotension or hypertension, arrhythmias and cardiac arrest. Management is as for lignocaine toxicity, including airway support, seizure control and lipid emulsion therapy in severe cases, plus management of adrenaline-induced hypertension and arrhythmias (e.g. with beta-blockers where appropriate).	Store at controlled room temperature, protected from light and excessive heat. Do not use solutions that are discolored or have expired; adrenaline is subject to oxidation and loss of potency over time.
1.2.3	Bupivacaine without preservatives	Solution for injection 50mg/ml, (2ml)	Long-acting amide local anesthetic providing prolonged sensory and motor block, commonly used for regional blocks, spinal/epidural anesthesia and postoperative analgesia. The preservative-free formulation is indicated for neuraxial use (epidural, spinal).	Blocks voltage-gated sodium channels in neuronal cell membranes, stabilizing the membrane and preventing initiation and conduction of nerve impulses. More potent and longer-acting than lignocaine but with a narrower safety margin, particularly for cardiotoxicity.	Regional nerve blocks (e.g. brachial plexus, femoral, sciatic), epidural anesthesia and analgesia for surgery and labor, spinal anesthesia (with appropriate formulation), postoperative analgesia via epidural infusions or peripheral nerve catheters.	Hypersensitivity to bupivacaine or other amide-type local anesthetics. Contraindicated for IV regional (Bier) blocks due to high risk of cardiotoxicity on systemic release. Caution in severe cardiac conduction disturbances, heart failure, advanced hepatic disease and in patients receiving other local anesthetics or class I antiarrhythmics.	Local: prolonged sensory and motor block, potential nerve injury from needle trauma or high concentration. Systemic toxicity (usually from inadvertent intravascular injection or overdose): early CNS symptoms (perioral numbness, tinnitus, metallic taste, agitation, confusion), progressing to seizures, CNS depression, severe hypotension, ventricular arrhythmias and cardiac arrest. Bupivacaine is particularly notorious for difficult-to-resuscitate cardiotoxicity.	Doses depend on block type and concentration. For peripheral nerve block, typical maximum dose is 2–3 mg/kg (longer-acting and more toxic than lignocaine). For epidural bolus in adults: 0.25–0.5% solutions in volumes of 10–20 ml depending on level and purpose. Onset is slower than lignocaine (10–20 minutes) but duration is 3–8 hours or more. Always aspirate before injection, use incremental dosing and monitor continuously.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Bupivacaine (systemic absorption) + other local anesthetics (Lignocaine, Bupivacaine with glucose, Lidocaine-containing hemorrhoid or skin preparations) → additive risk of LAST; total combined dose must stay below safe mg/kg thresholds. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Bupivacaine + beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) or calcium channel blockers (Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) → worsened negative inotropy and bradycardia in a LAST event, making resuscitation more difficult. [MODERATE-INTRA] Bupivacaine neuraxial block + systemic vasodilators/antihypertensives (Hydralazine, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) → additive hypotension especially in obstetric patients. [MODERATE-CLASS] Sedatives and opioids (Propofol, Morphine, Fentanyl, Midazolam, Diazepam) may obscure early CNS toxicity signs; monitor systematically rather than relying on patient reporting. [CRITICAL-OUT] Bupivacaine + class I antiarrhythmics not currently in this dataset (e.g. flecainide) → highly dangerous cardiotoxic synergy; flag as external until such agents are added.	Elderly patients often have reduced cardiac reserve and hepatic clearance; lower maximum total doses and more cautious incremental dosing are recommended, with strict hemodynamic and ECG monitoring during major regional anesthesia.	Used in children for caudal and peripheral nerve blocks with strictly weight-based dosing; toxicity margin is narrow, so limit total mg/kg, avoid multiple local anesthetics concurrently, and use ultrasound guidance to minimize intravascular injection risk.	Bupivacaine is frequently used in obstetric epidural analgesia and cesarean section anesthesia. When used at appropriate concentrations and dosing, it is generally safe for mother and fetus, though high spinal or total spinal anesthesia may cause maternal hypotension and fetal distress; careful dosing and monitoring are essential.	Systemic toxicity (LAST) presents with CNS excitation followed by depression and severe cardiovascular collapse, often refractory to standard resuscitation. Management includes immediate cessation of injection, airway support, seizure control with benzodiazepines, early administration of 20% lipid emulsion (as per LAST protocols) and aggressive ACLS with particular attention to prolonged resuscitation and avoidance of large doses of vasopressors such as adrenaline where possible.	Store at room temperature and protect from light. Use only clear, colorless solutions in intact containers. Preservative-free formulations intended for neuraxial use must be maintained sterile; discard any unused portion after opening, according to aseptic practice and institutional policy.
1.2.4	Bupivacaine	Solution for injection 0.5% w/v	Long-acting amide local anesthetic used for peripheral nerve blocks, local infiltration, epidural analgesia and spinal anesthesia. Its long duration makes it valuable in postoperative analgesia and labor analgesia.	Blocks voltage-dependent sodium channels, stabilizing neuronal membranes and preventing nerve impulse initiation and propagation. Highly lipophilic, leading to prolonged duration but increased affinity for myocardial sodium channels, increasing cardiotoxic potential.	Peripheral nerve blocks, epidural anesthesia and analgesia for labor and postoperative pain control, spinal anesthesia for lower abdominal and lower limb surgery.	Hypersensitivity to bupivacaine or other amide-type local anesthetics; contraindicated for IV regional anesthesia due to high risk of cardiotoxicity; caution in cardiovascular disease, conduction abnormalities, severe hepatic impairment and with concomitant Class I antiarrhythmics or other local anesthetics.	Local: prolonged numbness, motor block. Systemic toxicity: CNS excitation (perioral numbness, tinnitus, dizziness, tremors, seizures) followed by CNS depression and profound cardiovascular collapse, arrhythmias and cardiac arrest. Bupivacaine’s cardiotoxicity is more difficult to reverse than lignocaine.	Peripheral nerve block: typical maximum 2–3 mg/kg total dose depending on block type and patient status. Epidural bolus: 0.25–0.5% 10–20 ml depending on segmental level; incremental dosing and frequent aspiration strongly recommended.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Combined local anesthetic totals with Lignocaine plain and Lidocaine-containing hemorrhoid preparations → additive systemic toxicity (LAST). Total mg/kg must be tracked cumulatively. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Epidural bupivacaine + Suxamethonium or Rocuronium during cesarean anesthesia → masked apnea and deeper respiratory depression; airway monitoring mandatory. [MODERATE-INTRA] Epidural sympathetic block + Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol or Metoprolol (beta-blockers) → profound hypotension and bradycardia. [MODERATE-INTRA] Epidural anesthesia + Hydralazine or nifedipine (intra-table vasodilators) → severe hypotension requiring vasopressor support. [CLASS] Sedation from Propofol, Midazolam, Diazepam or opioids masks early CNS toxicity signs. [CRITICAL-OUT] Cardiac arrhythmic synergy with class I antiarrhythmics (flecainide, procainamide) – not in dataset, mark external.	In elderly, use the lowest effective dose and increment slowly due to higher sensitivity and decreased hepatic clearance; monitor ECG and mental status during major blocks.	Safe in children with weight-based dosing; children have lower toxicity thresholds — accurate mg/kg calculation, avoidance of multiple local anesthetics and ultrasound guidance are key safety measures.	Used extensively in obstetric epidural analgesia when properly dosed; maternal hypotension and fetal compromise possible if neuraxial block is extensive, requiring left uterine displacement, fluid preload and vasopressor readiness.	Systemic toxicity (LAST) causes seizures and profound arrhythmias resistant to standard CPR. Treat with airway support, seizure control, 20% lipid emulsion therapy, slow epinephrine dosing, and prolonged resuscitation if needed.	Store at room temperature away from light; discard remaining solution after single-use when sterility cannot be maintained.
1.2.5	Bupivacaine with glucose	Solution for injection 0.5% + 8% w/v	Hyperbaric spinal anesthetic solution where glucose increases density relative to CSF, resulting in predictable spread for spinal anesthesia. Provides deep sensory and motor block for lower abdominal, pelvic and orthopedic procedures.	Blocks neuronal sodium channels, preventing nerve impulse propagation. Hyperbaric formulation facilitates gravitational distribution within the subarachnoid space, enhancing control over anesthetic spread.	Spinal anesthesia for cesarean section, lower abdominal, pelvic, urologic and lower limb surgeries.	Contraindications: hypersensitivity to amide anesthetics; coagulopathy; infection at injection site; septicemia; severe hypovolemia; raised intracranial pressure without CSF drainage; relative contraindication in patients with fixed cardiac output states. Avoid traumatic spinal attempts in anticoagulated patients.	Common: hypotension, bradycardia, prolonged sensory and motor block, urinary retention. Rare: total spinal block with apnea and severe hypotension; post-dural puncture headache; transient neurologic symptoms. Severe systemic toxicity possible with accidental intravascular injection.	Dose for spinal anesthesia varies (1.5–3 ml of 0.5% hyperbaric bupivacaine) depending on height, surgical level and duration. Onset is rapid; duration is 2–4 hours or longer. Positioning determines spread — meticulous incremental dosing is important.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Combined systemic absorption with other local anesthetics (Lignocaine, Bupivacaine plain, Lidocaine-containing hemorrhoid preparations) → additive LAST risk; mg/kg totals must remain below maximum safe limits. [MODERATE-INTRA] Neuraxial sympathetic block + antihypertensives (ACE inhibitors, Hydralazine, Calcium channel blockers, Beta-blockers) → profound hypotension due to loss of autonomic tone. [MODERATE-CLASS] Sedatives, opioids, benzodiazepines and volatile anesthetics in this table may obscure early CNS toxicity signs or potentiate hypotension. [CRITICAL-OUT] Concurrent class I antiarrhythmics not in current dataset (flecainide, procainamide) → dangerously increased cardiotoxicity; mark external.	Elderly patients are more susceptible to severe hypotension and prolonged block; preload with fluids, monitor invasive blood pressure if major surgery, and titrate vasopressors early.	Used safely in pediatrics at specialist centers for specific surgical indications; weight-based dosing and close cardiopulmonary monitoring essential.	Spinal anesthesia in cesarean section is extremely common. Requires left uterine displacement and readiness to treat hypotension with vasopressors. Neonatal outcomes generally excellent with appropriate monitoring.	Excessive dosing or systemic absorption causes CNS toxicity and refractory arrhythmias as per LAST protocol. Treat aggressively with lipid emulsion therapy, seizure control and tailored resuscitation.	Store refrigerated or at controlled room temperature depending on formulation; protect from light and discard vials after single use.
1.3	Anticholinesterases													
1.3.1	Neostigmine	Solution for injection 2.5mg/ml, (1ml)	Reversal agent for non-depolarizing neuromuscular blockade and treatment of myasthenia gravis. Always administered with anticholinergic agent to control muscarinic effects.	Inhibits acetylcholinesterase, increasing acetylcholine concentration at neuromuscular junction, reversing competitive neuromuscular blockers. Also increases parasympathetic activity at muscarinic receptors.	Reversal of neuromuscular blockade from Rocuronium, Cisatracurium and Atracurium; myasthenia gravis therapy; ileus and urinary retention (rare use).	Intestinal obstruction, urinary retention without catheter access, bradycardia, asthma, peritonitis; caution in coronary artery disease, peptic ulcer disease.	Bradycardia, salivation, bronchospasm, diarrhea, abdominal cramping, nausea, vomiting; excessive doses may cause cholinergic crisis with muscle weakness and respiratory failure.	0.02–0.07 mg/kg IV for neuromuscular block reversal, administered with Glycopyrronium or Atropine to counter muscarinic effects; titrate based on neuromuscular monitoring.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Neostigmine + non-depolarizing blockers (Rocuronium, Cisatracurium, Atracurium) → intended reversal; inappropriate premature dosing may cause paradoxical/desynchrony in ICU blockade. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Neostigmine + Suxamethonium → potentiation of depolarizing block and prolonged apnea; avoid combination. [MODERATE-INTRA] Neostigmine + beta-blockers (Atenolol, Propranolol, Carvedilol, Labetalol, Metoprolol) → exaggerated bradycardia; requires atropine or glycopyrronium pretreatment. [CRITICAL-OUT] Neostigmine + potent cholinesterase inhibitors not in dataset (physostigmine, organophosphate exposure) → cholinergic crisis; mark external.	Elderly prone to bradycardia and secretions; reversal doses must be conservative and anticholinergic co-administration essential.	Used in pediatrics for neuromuscular reversal with weight-based dosing; atropine co-administration mandatory.	Generally safe if used intrapartum for block reversal; muscarinic side effects may affect mother and fetus; judicious dosing mandatory.	Overdose produces cholinergic crisis with salivation, bronchospasm, diarrhea, bradycardia and respiratory failure; treat with airway support and atropine.	Store at room temperature, protect from light; discard if discolored.
2	Medicines acting on Gastrointestinal System													
2.1	Antacids													
2.1.1	Aluminium hydroxide + Magnesium trisilicate	Gel, Chewable tablets	Complex antacid combination providing acid neutralization, mucosal protection and moderate antispasmodic benefit.	Neutralizes gastric hydrochloric acid and enhances mucosal defense through adsorption and mild protective coating.	Dyspepsia, gastritis, symptomatic relief of peptic ulcer disease, GERD.	Renal failure (due to magnesium accumulation), bowel obstruction, chronic diarrhea, severe fluid restriction.	Constipation (aluminium component), diarrhea (magnesium component), abdominal cramping, nausea; long-term use may reduce phosphate levels.	Chewable tablets or suspension dosed after meals and at bedtime for symptomatic relief.	[MINOR-INTRA] Absorption interactions minimal with your current table; no strong intra-table cardiotoxic or CNS impacts. [CLASS] Antacids reduce absorption of tetracyclines, quinolones and levothyroxine (not yet in dataset); mark as class external interactions. [OUT] Chronic concomitant use with phosphate-binding agents not yet listed may enhance hypophosphatemia risk.	Elderly at risk for electrolyte disturbances; avoid chronic magnesium in renal impairment.	Safe in pediatrics for symptomatic relief under supervision.	Generally safe in pregnancy; avoid chronic high intake due to electrolyte issues.	Overdose causes diarrhea, metabolic alkalosis (rare) or hypophosphatemia; supportive management.	Store tightly closed at room temperature; shake suspensions before use.
2.1.2	Aluminium hydroxide	Gel 4%; Tablets 500mg, Oral suspension 4%	Antacid with strong phosphate-binding capacity and mucosal protective effects.	Neutralizes gastric acid and binds dietary phosphate, reducing aluminum absorption.	Dyspepsia, peptic ulcer symptoms, hyperphosphatemia in renal failure.	Bowel obstruction, hypophosphatemia, chronic constipation.	Constipation, nausea, abdominal distension; long-term use can induce phosphate depletion and osteomalacia.	Administer after meals and at bedtime; dosing individualized for symptoms or phosphate binding.	[MODERATE-CLASS] Reduces absorption of tetracyclines, quinolones and levothyroxine (all not yet in dataset); mark external. [MINOR-INTRA] Minimal interaction with intra-table drugs except possible constipation exaggeration with Codeine, Morphine or Loperamide. [OUT] Co-administration with phosphate binders not in dataset increases risk of electrolyte imbalance.	Elderly more prone to constipation and electrolyte derangements; avoid chronic high-dose use.	Safe for short-term pediatric relief under supervision.	Generally safe in pregnancy for dyspepsia; avoid prolonged use.	Overdose causes severe constipation and phosphate depletion; treat with stool softeners and phosphate repletion.	Store tightly sealed, protect from moisture; shake suspensions well.
2.1.3	Magnesium trisilicate	Chewable tablets 250mg	Antacid with moderate acidity neutralization and adsorbent properties.	Neutralizes gastric acid and absorbs pepsin, reducing irritation.	Dyspepsia, gastritis, symptomatic GERD.	Renal failure (magnesium accumulation), chronic diarrhea.	Diarrhea, abdominal discomfort; rare electrolyte imbalance.	Take after meals and at bedtime based on symptoms.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Magnesium salts + laxatives (Senna, Bisacodyl) or diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide) → electrolyte disturbances with chronic/abusive use. [CLASS] Reduced absorption of antibiotics (tetracyclines, quinolones) not in dataset; mark external.	Elderly with renal impairment risk magnesium accumulation; avoid chronic use.	Safe in pediatrics with parental supervision.	Generally safe in pregnancy; avoid chronic excessive dosing.	Overdose may cause hypermagnesemia with hypotension and lethargy in severe renal impairment.	Store at room temperature; keep tightly closed to avoid moisture.
2.2	Antispasmodics													
2.2.1	Hyoscine butyl bromide	Solution for injection 20mg/ml (1ml), Tablets 10mg	Antispasmodic smooth-muscle relaxant for gastrointestinal and genitourinary colic.	Antagonizes muscarinic receptors in smooth muscle, reducing spasm and pain; minimal CNS penetration.	Abdominal colic, irritable bowel syndrome, renal or biliary colic, adjunct for endoscopic procedures.	Glaucoma, myasthenia gravis, megacolon, tachyarrhythmias, obstructive uropathy.	Dry mouth, tachycardia, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention; rare arrhythmias in high-dose parenteral use.	Tablets: 10–20mg 3–4 times daily; injection for acute colic.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Hyoscine + Loperamide or Codeine → increased risk of ileus and severe constipation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Tachycardia magnified with Adrenaline boluses in shock settings. [CLASS] Anticholinergic cognitive effects additive with antipsychotics or tricyclic antidepressants (Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine). [OUT] Combination with anticholinergic ophthalmics not yet in dataset could cause acute glaucoma; mark external.	Elderly more susceptible to urinary retention, confusion and constipation; dose conservatively.	Safe in pediatrics for abdominal colic under physician guidance.	Avoid in pregnancy unless clinically necessary due to limited safety data.	Overdose produces anticholinergic toxicity with tachycardia, dry mucosa, confusion; treat with supportive care.	Store at room temperature, protect from light; discard if solution discolored.
2.3	Anti-ulcer medicines													
2.3.1	Cimetidine	Tablets 200mg, 400mg, 800mg; Syrup/suspension 200mg/5ml, Solution for injection 100mg/ml	First-generation H2 receptor antagonist reducing basal and nocturnal gastric acid secretion.	Competitive inhibition of histamine H2 receptors on gastric parietal cells, reducing acid output.	Peptic ulcer disease, GERD, stress ulcer prophylaxis.	Hypersensitivity, caution in severe hepatic disease.	Diarrhea, headache, dizziness, confusion in elderly; gynecomastia and impotence with chronic high dosing.	Oral dosing 200–800mg/day depending on indication; IV for acute stress prophylaxis.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Slight sedation interaction with Diazepam or Clonazepam due to hepatic metabolism interference. [CLASS] Reduced absorption of drugs requiring acidic pH, not currently in dataset. [OUT] More potent interactions with theophylline, phenytoin and warfarin not in dataset — mark external.	Elderly prone to confusion and drug interactions; avoid chronic high dosing.	Safe if weight-based in pediatrics for gastritis.	Generally safe in pregnancy when antacids or dietary changes fail.	Overdose causes CNS depression or arrhythmias; supportive care only.	Store at room temperature and protect from moisture.
2.3.2	Ranitidine	Tablets 150mg, 300mg, Solution for injection 25mg/ml	Second-generation H2 blocker with fewer endocrine effects than cimetidine.	Competitive inhibition of H2 receptors on parietal cells, reducing acid secretion.	Peptic ulcer disease, GERD, prevention of stress ulcers.	Hypersensitivity, caution in hepatic impairment.	Diarrhea, headache, dizziness; rare bradycardia with IV administration.	Oral 150–300mg/day; IV for stress prophylaxis or NPO patients.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Minimal interactions inside this table unless sedation masked by benzodiazepines. [CLASS] Similar absorption interference profile to cimetidine with external antibiotics, antifungals or thyroid drugs; mark external.	Elderly tolerate generally well but monitor renal function for dose adjustment.	Safe in pediatrics for symptomatic gastritis and reflux.	Safe for pregnancy when clinically indicated.	Overdose rarely dangerous; treat symptomatically.	Store at room temperature; protect from light for injection forms.
2.3.3	Omeprazole	Capsule 10/20mg	Proton pump inhibitor with potent and sustained acid suppression.	Irreversibly inhibits H+/K+-ATPase proton pump in gastric parietal cells.	GERD, erosive esophagitis, ulcer healing, NSAID prophylaxis.	Hypersensitivity, caution in hepatic impairment and long-term use due to B12 or magnesium deficiency.	Headache, abdominal pain, diarrhea, B12 deficiency, hypomagnesemia with long use.	20mg daily for GERD; individualized.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Minimal internal absorption interactions; small prolongation of sedation possible with Diazepam. [CLASS] pH-dependent absorption interactions for external drugs such as itraconazole, iron, levothyroxine; mark external. [OUT] CYP2C19 genotype and external antiplatelets (clopidogrel) interactions not yet relevant until drugs added.	Elderly prone to magnesium depletion and fractures with prolonged therapy.	Safe weight-based for pediatric GERD.	Safe in pregnancy when indicated.	Benign overdose producing headache, GI upset; symptomatic care.	Store at room temperature away from heat and moisture.
2.3.4	Esomeprazole	Tablet/capsule20mg/40mg, Solution for injection 20mg	S-isomer of omeprazole with more consistent acid suppression and improved bioavailability.	Same mechanism as omeprazole — irreversible proton pump inhibition.	GERD, erosive esophagitis, ulcer therapy, stress prophylaxis.	Hypersensitivity, long-term use risks.	Headache, diarrhea, abdominal discomfort, electrolyte issues; monitor B12 and magnesium.	20–40mg daily orally; IV preparations available.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Mild hepatic metabolism competition with Diazepam or Midazolam but generally safe clinically. [CLASS] pH-dependent absorption changes for external drugs. [OUT] Similar clopidogrel interaction remains external until antiplatelet agents are added.	Elderly require magnesium monitoring for long-term therapy.	Safe pediatric GERD under supervision.	Safe in pregnancy.	Benign overdose with GI upset; symptomatic care.	Store at controlled room temperature; protect from light for vials.
2.4	Antidiarrhoeals													
2.4.1	Codeine phosphate	Tablets 15mg, 30mg, 60mg	Weak opioid analgesic and antidiarrheal metabolized to morphine; used for mild-to-moderate pain and cough suppression.	Opioid receptor agonist after hepatic conversion to morphine; decreases pain transmission and slows GI motility.	Pain relief, cough suppression, diarrhea.	Respiratory depression, severe asthma, head injury, paralytic ileus, ultra-rapid CYP2D6 metabolizers.	Constipation, nausea, sedation, respiratory depression, dependence.	15–60mg every 4–6 hours.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Severe CNS and respiratory depression with Morphine, Tramadol, Fentanyl or Propofol. [MODERATE-INTRA] Additive constipation with Loperamide or Hyoscine. [CLASS] Sedation and hypotension enhanced by external antihistamines or antipsychotics not yet in dataset. [OUT] Potentiation with external MAOIs should be flagged.	Elderly risk delirium and respiratory suppression; start low.	Avoid pediatric use due to unpredictable metabolism.	Avoid late pregnancy due to neonatal withdrawal.	Overdose: pinpoint pupils, apnea, coma; Naloxone indicated.	Store securely, protected from humidity; controlled substance.
2.4.2	Loperamide	Tablets/Capsules 2mg, Syrup 1mg/ml	Peripheral opioid agonist with antidiarrheal activity and minimal CNS penetration.	Slows intestinal motility via mu-opioid receptor activation, increasing absorption.	Acute diarrhea, chronic diarrhea, IBS-related diarrhea.	Children <2, acute dysentery, toxic megacolon.	Constipation, abdominal distension, ileus, rare arrhythmias in overdose.	Initial 4mg then 2mg after loose stool; max 16mg/day.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Loperamide + Codeine or Hyoscine → ileus risk. [OUT] Chronic high-dose misuse with CYP inhibitors may produce arrhythmias; external misuse context. [CLASS] CNS depressants may mask toxicity symptoms, but not strong intra-table impact.	Elderly sensitive to constipation and obstruction.	Avoid <2 years old.	Use cautiously in pregnancy for acute diarrhea only.	Overdose causes cardiac arrhythmias and CNS depression; supportive care.	Store at room temperature; protect from humidity.
2.5	Laxatives													
2.5.1	Lactulose	Suspension 66.7% w/v	Synthetic non-absorbable disaccharide used as osmotic laxative and for hepatic encephalopathy management. Decreases ammonia absorption and systemic neurotoxicity.	Metabolized in colon to organic acids which increase osmotic pressure and trap ammonia in the gut as ammonium, reducing systemic absorption. Increases stool water retention and softens fecal consistency.	Chronic constipation, hepatic encephalopathy, bowel regularization in opioid-induced constipation.	Intestinal obstruction, galactosemia, caution in patients with fluid restriction or severe electrolyte disturbances.	Flatulence, abdominal cramps, diarrhea, bloating, dehydration with excessive dosing; electrolyte depletion with chronic abuse.	Constipation: 10–20ml/day titrated to soft stools. Hepatic encephalopathy: titrate to 2–3 soft stools/day; route may be oral or via NG tube.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Severe constipation risk when combined with anticholinergics (Hyoscine) or peristalsis inhibitors (Loperamide, Codeine). [MODERATE-INTRA] Dehydration risk increased with diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide) due to fluid loss from diarrhea. [CLASS] Reduced intestinal pH affects absorption of drugs requiring alkaline environment (not in current dataset) — mark external. [OUT] Avoid combined use with external electrolyte-free cathartics such as polyethylene glycol without monitoring.	Elderly prone to electrolyte imbalance; titrate gently and ensure adequate fluid intake.	Safe for pediatrics for constipation with clinician guidance; lower dosing advised.	Safe in pregnancy for constipation; preferred laxative due to non-systemic absorption.	Overdose causes severe diarrhea, dehydration, electrolyte loss and symptomatic weakness; rehydrate and correct electrolytes.	Store below 25°C, protect from heat; viscous solution remains chemically stable; cap tightly after use.
2.5.2	Glycerol	Suppository 1g, 4g	Hyperosmotic rectal laxative that softens and lubricates stool and gently stimulates rectal emptying. Commonly used for short-term relief of constipation, especially when rapid effect is desired or oral agents are unsuitable.	Draws water into the rectum by osmotic effect, softens stool, and exerts a mild local irritant action on the rectal mucosa, which increases peristalsis and promotes defecation.	Short-term treatment of constipation; bowel evacuation prior to rectal examinations or minor procedures.	Rectal bleeding of unknown origin, severe inflammatory bowel disease in distal colon or rectum, bowel obstruction, painful fissures or severe hemorrhoids where insertion is intolerable.	Generally well tolerated. Possible local irritation, rectal discomfort or burning, abdominal cramping. Rarely, diarrhea and dehydration with repeated excessive use.	Adults: typically 2–4g suppository inserted rectally once daily as needed, usually producing effect within 15–60 minutes. Children: usually 1g suppository or smaller pediatric preparation, used short term only. Lubricate suppository and insert gently to avoid mucosal trauma.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Glycerol suppositories + other constipating agents in this table (Codeine, Morphine, Loperamide, Hyoscine) are often used intentionally to counteract opioid-induced constipation, but over-reliance on rectal agents may mask severe opioid-related ileus; monitor for abdominal pain and lack of bowel sounds. [MODERATE-INTRA] Combined use with stimulant laxatives (Senna, Bisacodyl) in this table can cause rapid stool evacuation and fluid loss; in frail patients, consider electrolyte monitoring if used regularly. [CLASS] Rectal route administration may alter absorption of co-administered rectal drugs (none currently in this segment of your list; potential future external interactions if rectal NSAIDs or corticosteroids are added).
