[OUT] Chronic abusive use with other OTC laxatives (e.g. polyethylene glycol, sodium picosulfate, not in this dataset) can cause dependence and chronic electrolyte derangements; mark as external-polypharmacy risk for future expansion.	Elderly can benefit from glycerol as a relatively gentle option when oral intake is poor; however, frail patients are more prone to fissures and mucosal trauma. Avoid frequent, forceful insertion and monitor for rectal bleeding, dehydration and hypotension if diarrhea occurs.	Commonly used off-label in children for acute constipation. Pediatric dosing must always use age/weight-appropriate suppository size. Avoid chronic use; consider underlying causes (diet, fluid intake, congenital anomalies).	Considered safe for occasional use in pregnancy as it is minimally absorbed and acts locally. Advise non-pharmacologic measures (diet, fluids) first and avoid routine daily use without clinical review.	Overdose typically leads to diarrhea, abdominal cramps and possible dehydration. Serious systemic toxicity is extremely rare. Management is supportive with oral/IV fluids and temporary cessation of all laxatives.	Store below 25°C, protect from excessive heat and direct sunlight. Keep suppositories in original packaging to prevent softening and deformation; if softened, they may be briefly cooled to re-harden prior to use.
2.5.3	Senna	Tablets 7.5mg, Suppositories	Stimulant (contact) laxative derived from senna leaves and pods. Used short-term for constipation and bowel preparation. Acts on the colon to increase motility and fluid secretion.	Active sennosides are metabolized by colonic bacteria to anthraquinone derivatives that directly stimulate enteric nerves, increasing peristalsis, and reduce water and electrolyte absorption, softening stool.	Short-term treatment of constipation; bowel preparation when combined with other regimens; prevention of opioid-induced constipation when used intermittently.	Intestinal obstruction, acute surgical abdomen, severe inflammatory bowel disease, undiagnosed abdominal pain. Avoid chronic continuous use due to risk of dependency and electrolyte imbalance.	Cramping, abdominal pain, diarrhea, flatulence. Chronic abuse: melanosis coli, hypokalemia, secondary constipation, rarely protein-losing enteropathy.	Adults: commonly 7.5–30mg sennosides at night, titrated to effect. Suppository forms act faster but are used less often. Treatment should be as short as possible and not used daily long term without medical review.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Senna + potassium-depleting diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Amiloride/HCTZ combo) → increased risk of hypokalemia and arrhythmias, especially if Digoxin is used for heart failure or arrhythmias. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Senna + Digoxin (cardiac glycoside in this table) → hypokalemia from laxative overuse can potentiate digoxin toxicity (arrhythmias, visual disturbances); monitor potassium and digoxin levels if chronic laxative therapy is unavoidable. [MODERATE-INTRA] Senna + Loperamide or Codeine → may cause alternating diarrhea and severe constipation or masked ileus; use in a structured bowel regimen with monitoring. [CLASS] Chronic anthraquinone laxative use plus other stimulant laxatives (Bisacodyl) increases dependency risk and colonic atony. [OUT] Co-use with external corticosteroid tablets (beyond those in your list) further potentiates hypokalemia and should be flagged as external-class risk.	Elderly are at higher risk of dehydration, electrolyte disturbances and laxative dependence; use the lowest effective dose, intermittent rather than daily, and regularly review the need for continuation.	Used short-term in children only under medical supervision. Not recommended for infants. Dose must be weight-adjusted and duration minimized.	Occasional short-term use is generally considered low risk in pregnancy, but non-pharmacological measures or bulk-forming/ osmotic laxatives (e.g. Lactulose) are preferred. Chronic use is discouraged.	Overdose can cause severe diarrhea, abdominal cramps, dehydration and hypokalemia leading to weakness and arrhythmias. Management: stop senna, rehydrate, correct electrolytes, monitor ECG especially if Digoxin or other QT-prolonging agents are on board.	Store in a cool, dry place away from moisture and direct sunlight. Keep out of reach of children due to risk of accidental ingestion and diarrhea.
2.5.4	Bisacodyl	Tablets 5mg	Stimulant laxative that acts primarily on the colon to accelerate transit and increase water and electrolyte secretion into the intestinal lumen.	Directly stimulates enteric nerves in the colon, enhancing peristalsis and inhibiting reabsorption of water and electrolytes, resulting in softer, more frequent stools.	Short-term treatment of constipation; bowel preparation regimens prior to diagnostic or surgical procedures (often combined with other laxatives).	Intestinal obstruction, acute surgical abdomen, severe dehydration, severe colitis, undiagnosed abdominal pain.	Abdominal cramping, diarrhea, nausea, rectal irritation (if rectal formulations used), dizziness. Chronic use: electrolyte imbalance (hypokalemia), dependence, atonic colon.	Oral: typically 5–10mg at night; effect in 6–12 hours. For more rapid effect, rectal suppositories (not in your presentation list but class effect) act within about 30–60 minutes. Use for as short a period as possible.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Bisacodyl + potassium-depleting diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Amiloride/HCTZ) → increased risk of hypokalemia and consequent Digoxin toxicity; same risk profile as Senna. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Bisacodyl + Digoxin → potentiation of digoxin toxicity if significant hypokalemia develops; monitor electrolytes and ECG in high-risk patients. [MODERATE-INTRA] Combination therapy with Senna or Lactulose may be intentional in refractory constipation but significantly increases risk of dehydration and electrolyte imbalance, especially in elderly or those on antihypertensives. [CLASS] Concurrent long-term use with other stimulant laxatives from outside this list (e.g. sodium picosulfate) compounds dependency risk. [OUT] Oral bisacodyl enteric coating may be affected by external PPIs/H2 blockers altering gastric pH, slightly modifying onset; mark as external-class effect, not critical.	Elderly are particularly vulnerable to laxative overuse, hypokalemia, and postural hypotension associated with dehydration. Start with low doses and avoid continuous use; integrate dietary and mobility measures.	Use in children should be limited to short courses with careful dose adjustment; avoid in very young children unless specifically directed by a pediatrician.	Occasional short-term use appears low risk, but constipation in pregnancy is generally better managed with bulk-forming or osmotic agents. Routine use throughout pregnancy is not recommended without specialist advice.	Overdose manifests as severe diarrhea, abdominal pain, dehydration and electrolyte depletion (especially hypokalemia), potentially causing muscle weakness and arrhythmias. Management is supportive with fluid and electrolyte replacement and ECG monitoring in moderate/severe cases.	Store at room temperature in original blister packs, protected from moisture and heat. Do not crush enteric-coated tablets as this alters release characteristics and increases gastric irritation.
2.6	Medicines used for treating haemorrhoids													
2.6.1	Bismuth subgallate + Zinc oxide + Lidocaine hydrochloride	Suppository, Cream	Combination topical preparation for hemorrhoids and anal irritation: bismuth and zinc provide astringent, protective and mild antiseptic effects, while lidocaine offers local anesthesia to relieve pain and itching.	Bismuth subgallate and zinc oxide form a protective barrier and precipitate proteins on damaged mucosa, reducing weeping and irritation. Lidocaine blocks voltage-gated sodium channels in local nerve endings, inhibiting pain transmission.	Symptomatic treatment of internal and external hemorrhoids, anal fissures (as adjunct), pruritus ani, post-anal surgery discomfort.	Allergy to local anesthetics of the amide type (Lidocaine), bismuth compounds or zinc. Avoid prolonged use on severely infected or ulcerated tissue without medical supervision.	Local burning, itching or irritation; rare allergic contact dermatitis. Systemic lidocaine toxicity is very rare but possible if excessive amounts are applied to large, damaged areas or used with other lidocaine products.	Apply thin layer of cream or insert suppository as directed, usually 1–3 times daily, particularly after defecation and at bedtime. Duration should be limited to symptom control and reassessed if no improvement within 7–14 days.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Lidocaine contained in the preparation contributes to total local anesthetic load together with Lignocaine injections, Bupivacaine and other Lidocaine-containing preparations in this table (e.g. Lignocaine with Adrenaline dental). Excessive combined exposure increases risk of LAST, particularly in patients receiving multiple local anesthetics during perioperative care. [MINOR-INTRA] Concomitant use with other constipating agents (Codeine, Loperamide, Hyoscine) can perpetuate straining and hemorrhoidal disease; pain relief without stool softening (e.g. Lactulose, Glycerol suppositories) may be counterproductive. [CLASS] Topical barrier effect may reduce absorption of nearby applied rectal medications (none currently listed, potential external interactions if rectal steroids or mesalamine are later added). [OUT] Excessive bismuth intake from multiple external bismuth products could rarely lead to systemic neurotoxicity; mark as external poly-bismuth risk.	Elderly patients often have fragile perianal skin; use gently and avoid vigorous application. Provide parallel constipation management and monitor for contact dermatitis or bleeding.	May be used short-term in adolescents with hemorrhoidal symptoms, but underlying causes (constipation, diet) should be addressed. Avoid unsupervised use in small children due to difficulty recognizing local reactions.	Generally considered low risk when used topically in pregnancy for short durations. However, emphasize non-pharmacological hemorrhoid management (diet, fiber, sitz baths) and avoid prolonged application over extensive areas.	Overdose from topical use is unlikely; theoretical risk is systemic Lidocaine toxicity if large amounts are applied to abraded tissue repeatedly or ingested accidentally. Symptoms would mimic other Lidocaine overdoses (CNS excitation, seizures, arrhythmias); treat with airway support and lipid emulsion if systemic toxicity occurs.	Store below 25°C, protect from heat and direct sunlight. Keep tubes tightly closed and suppositories in original packaging. Do not use beyond expiry due to risk of base separation and reduced efficacy.
3	Medicines acting on the Central Nervous System													
3.1	Anxiolytics													
3.1.1	Clonazepam	Tablet 0.5mg	Long-acting benzodiazepine with potent anticonvulsant and anxiolytic properties. Used for seizure disorders and certain anxiety conditions, including panic disorder.	Enhances GABA-A receptor activity by increasing frequency of chloride channel opening, leading to neuronal hyperpolarization and CNS depression.	Epilepsy (absence, myoclonic, and partial seizures), panic disorder, adjunct therapy for anxiety where other treatments fail.	Severe respiratory insufficiency, sleep apnea, acute narrow-angle glaucoma, severe hepatic impairment. Caution in substance use disorder, depression with suicidal risk.	Drowsiness, sedation, ataxia, dizziness, cognitive impairment, behavioral disinhibition (especially in adolescents), dependence and withdrawal with long-term use; respiratory depression when combined with other CNS depressants.	Adults: initial 0.5mg 1–2 times daily, titrated slowly based on seizure or anxiety control. Maximum doses vary with indication. Should be tapered gradually when discontinuing.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Clonazepam + opioids in this table (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Codeine) → markedly increased risk of profound sedation, respiratory depression, coma and death; avoid or use under strict monitoring and document justification in your system’s clinical decision support. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Clonazepam + other benzodiazepines (Lorazepam, Midazolam, Diazepam, Chlordiazepoxide) → cumulative sedation and high dependence risk; your CDSS should flag concurrent chronic prescribing. [MODERATE-INTRA] Clonazepam + antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine, Risperidone) or sedating antihistamines (Promethazine, Chlorphenamine) → additive CNS depression, postural hypotension and fall risk, especially in elderly. [CLASS] Clonazepam + alcohol (not in this dataset but clinically common) → strong OUT-of-table interaction causing severe CNS depression; your database should tag as standard benzodiazepine–alcohol warning.	In older adults, clonazepam is high-risk (Beers criteria): prolonged sedation, cognitive impairment, falls, delirium. Use only when absolutely necessary, at the lowest dose and shortest duration, with structured review and deprescribing plans.	Not first-line in children for anxiety; used mainly for epilepsy under specialist guidance, with careful monitoring for behavioral changes, learning difficulties and respiratory depression, particularly in combination with other sedatives or anticonvulsants.	Should generally be avoided in pregnancy due to potential teratogenicity (cleft palate risk signals) and neonatal withdrawal/floppy infant syndrome. If essential for severe epilepsy, use lowest effective dose and document risk–benefit discussion.	Overdose presents with deep sedation, ataxia, hypotension, respiratory depression and rarely coma (especially with co-ingestants). Management: airway protection, supportive care, and cautious use of flumazenil only in tightly selected cases (risk of seizures if chronic benzodiazepine or TCA user).	Store at room temperature away from moisture and light. Track tightly in your system due to dependence and diversion risk.
3.1.2	Lorazepam	Tablets 2mg	Intermediate-acting benzodiazepine with anxiolytic, sedative and anticonvulsant properties. Often used for acute agitation, status epilepticus (IV form) and severe anxiety.	Potentiates GABA-A receptor activity, enhancing inhibitory neurotransmission and causing generalized CNS depression.	Severe anxiety, acute agitation, premedication before procedures, status epilepticus (IV), alcohol withdrawal (adjunct).	Severe respiratory insufficiency, sleep apnea, acute narrow-angle glaucoma, severe hepatic impairment, myasthenia gravis. Caution in substance use disorders and depression.	Sedation, dizziness, ataxia, confusion (especially in elderly), respiratory depression when combined with other CNS depressants, dependence and withdrawal on long-term use.	For anxiety: 1–2mg 2–3 times daily orally; lower starting doses in elderly. For premedication or acute agitation (IV/IM), titrated under monitoring (dose forms in your table are oral, but your DB can link to parenteral references). Taper instead of abrupt cessation.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Lorazepam + opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Codeine) → same high-risk benzodiazepine–opioid combination as for Clonazepam, with strong requirement for alerts in your prescribing system. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Lorazepam + other benzodiazepines (Clonazepam, Midazolam, Diazepam, Chlordiazepoxide) → avoid chronic co-prescribing; if used concurrently in acute settings (e.g. midazolam intraoperatively) your workflow should enforce short duration and monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Lorazepam + antipsychotics, sedating antihistamines (Promethazine, Chlorphenamine), tricyclic antidepressants (Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine) → enhanced CNS depression, orthostatic hypotension and QT-related fall risk. [OUT] As with all benzodiazepines, concurrent alcohol or external sedative-hypnotics (e.g. z-drugs) are important out-of-table interactions to flag.	Elderly patients are very sensitive to lorazepam; start at very low doses (e.g. 0.5mg) and avoid chronic use. Consider non-pharmacologic anxiety/insomnia treatments first; the system should show warnings for long-duration prescriptions in ≥65 years.	Use in children is generally limited to acute seizure management (parenteral) or short-term severe anxiety under specialist oversight. Chronic use is discouraged; monitor school performance and behavior.	Avoid in pregnancy if possible; can cause neonatal respiratory depression and withdrawal. If used near delivery, neonatal monitoring for hypotonia and respiratory depression is mandatory.	Overdose typically results in drowsiness, ataxia, slurred speech, hypotension and, with other CNS depressants, respiratory arrest. Management is primarily supportive; flumazenil remains reserved for selected, closely monitored cases due to seizure risk.	Store at room temperature in original container. In your system, mark as controlled medicine with monitoring of duration and repeats.
3.1.3	Midazolam	Solution for injection 5mg, 15mg	Short-acting benzodiazepine used mainly for procedural sedation, induction of anesthesia (as adjunct), status epilepticus and ICU sedation. Rapid onset and short duration make it suitable for titratable sedation.	Enhances GABA-A–mediated inhibitory neurotransmission at benzodiazepine binding sites, causing anxiolysis, sedation, anterograde amnesia and anticonvulsant effects.	Procedural sedation, induction adjunct in anesthesia, premedication, control of acute seizures/status epilepticus, ICU sedation.	Severe respiratory depression, acute alcohol intoxication, myasthenia gravis, severe hepatic dysfunction (relative), shock without adequate resuscitation. Same benzodiazepine class cautions as above.	Respiratory depression, apnea (especially with rapid IV administration or combination with opioids/Propofol), hypotension, paradoxical agitation (especially in younger or elderly), prolonged sedation in organ dysfunction or with CYP inhibitors.	IV sedation: titrated in small increments (e.g. 1–2mg IV over 2 minutes, repeated as needed) with continuous monitoring of airway and vital signs. Dose requirements reduced when combined with opioids, Propofol or in frail patients. Not for unsupervised use outside monitored environments.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Midazolam + Propofol (already in your table) is a classic high-risk combo: profound CNS and respiratory depression; your system should enforce mandatory monitoring fields (SpO2, BP, airway equipment available) when both are charted together. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Midazolam + opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl, Tramadol, Codeine) → same high-risk sedation synergy; require lowered doses, documentation of indication and post-dose observation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Midazolam + other benzodiazepines (Clonazepam, Lorazepam, Diazepam, Chlordiazepoxide) → additive sedation and dependence risk; acute perioperative overlap may be acceptable under anesthetic supervision but chronic co-prescribing should trigger alerts. [MODERATE-INTRA] Midazolam + antipsychotics and sedating antihistamines (Promethazine, Chlorphenamine) → increased hypotension and respiratory compromise, especially in elderly. [OUT] Numerous external CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g. azole antifungals beyond those in your list, macrolides) significantly prolong midazolam effect and must be added as OUT interactions when you expand the drug set.	Elderly are extremely sensitive to midazolam-induced respiratory depression; doses should be dramatically reduced and titrated slowly with continuous monitoring. Your prescribing system should flag high initial doses in ≥65-year orders.	Pediatric use is common for procedural sedation and seizure control; weight-based dosing and continuous monitoring are mandatory. Neonates have prolonged half-life; dose adjustments and longer post-dose observation are required.	Avoid elective use in pregnancy; for emergency procedures, use lowest effective dose and anticipate neonatal respiratory depression if used near delivery.	Overdose results in deep sedation, coma, respiratory arrest, bradycardia and hypotension, especially with other depressants. Treatment: airway and ventilatory support; flumazenil may reverse benzodiazepine effect but must be used cautiously due to seizure risk, particularly if there is a mixed TCA overdose.	Store at controlled room temperature, protect from light. Once opened or diluted, follow local stability policies and discard unused portions appropriately.
3.1.4	Diazepam	Tablets 2mg, Solution for injection 5mg/ml (2ml)	Long-acting benzodiazepine with anxiolytic, anticonvulsant, muscle relaxant and sedative properties. Widely used for acute seizures, muscle spasm, severe anxiety and alcohol withdrawal.	Enhances GABA-A receptor activity, leading to increased chloride influx and neuronal hyperpolarization throughout the CNS.	Acute anxiety and agitation, status epilepticus, febrile seizures (rectal/parenteral), muscle spasms (e.g. tetanus), alcohol withdrawal.	Severe respiratory insufficiency, sleep apnea, acute narrow-angle glaucoma, severe hepatic impairment. Caution in elderly, substance use disorders and depressive syndromes.	Sedation, drowsiness, confusion, ataxia, muscle weakness, respiratory depression (especially with opioids or other sedatives), paradoxical agitation, dependence and withdrawal phenomena on long-term use.	Anxiety: 2–10mg orally 2–4 times daily depending on severity. Acute seizures: IV/rectal dosing per weight and setting. Should not be used as chronic first-line agent for insomnia or generalized anxiety in modern practice; taper gradually when discontinuing.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Diazepam + opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Codeine) → very high-risk sedative combination; your CDSS should hard-flag this with warning text. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Diazepam + Propofol or Midazolam in perioperative/ICU use → profound sedation, loss of airway reflexes; appropriate only with full monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Diazepam + tricyclic antidepressants (Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine), antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine, Risperidone) and sedating antihistamines (Promethazine, Chlorphenamine) → additive CNS depression, increased fall risk and orthostatic hypotension. [CLASS] Diazepam is hepatically metabolized; when you later add strong external CYP inhibitors/inducers, your DB should flag these as OUT interactions. [OUT] Alcohol and other external sedative-hypnotics markedly increase toxicity; standard benzodiazepine–alcohol interaction should be encoded.	Elderly: avoid or use at very low doses only; high risk of falls, confusion, fractures and prolonged sedation. Any chronic diazepam prescription in older adults should show a deprescribing alert.	Children: reserved for acute seizure management and short-term severe anxiety under specialist oversight. Chronic anxiolytic use is discouraged. Monitor school performance and behavior closely.	Avoid in pregnancy where possible, particularly first trimester. If used late in pregnancy or at high doses, neonatal hypotonia and withdrawal can occur; document indication and monitor neonate after birth.	Overdose: deep sedation, respiratory depression, hypotension, coma (especially with co-ingestants). Management is airway support and observation. Flumazenil can reverse benzodiazepine effect but is rarely used outside anesthesia due to seizure risk in chronic users or mixed overdoses.	Store tablets at room temperature, away from moisture. Injection solutions should be protected from light and used promptly after opening. Mark as a controlled medicine with appropriate access controls in your system.
3.1.5	Chlordiazepoxide	Tablets/capsule 5mg, 10mg, and 25mg; solution for Injection 100mg/2mL ampoules	Long-acting benzodiazepine particularly used for alcohol withdrawal and some anxiety disorders. Active metabolites lead to very prolonged effect.	Potentiates GABA-A receptor activity, causing CNS depression with anxiolytic, sedative, anticonvulsant and muscle relaxant properties.	Alcohol withdrawal syndrome (including delirium tremens), severe anxiety and agitation.	Same benzodiazepine class contraindications: severe respiratory insufficiency, sleep apnea, acute narrow-angle glaucoma, severe hepatic insufficiency. Caution in elderly and those with liver disease (due to long half-life).	Sedation, confusion, ataxia, dizziness, paradoxical excitement, respiratory depression with other sedatives, dependence/withdrawal, especially with prolonged use.	Alcohol withdrawal: typical regimens involve 25–100mg orally every 6–8 hours initially, then taper over several days based on clinical scores. For anxiety, much lower doses and shorter duration are recommended. Avoid abrupt discontinuation after prolonged use.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Chlordiazepoxide + alcohol (though not in the structured list, but clinically guaranteed in withdrawal management) is a controlled therapeutic but still high-risk combination early in detox; careful titration is needed to prevent respiratory compromise. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Chlordiazepoxide + opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Codeine) → very high CNS and respiratory depression risk and should only be combined with clear indications and close monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Chlordiazepoxide + other benzodiazepines (Diazepam, Lorazepam, Clonazepam, Midazolam) or sedating antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine, Risperidone) → additive sedation and fall risk. [CLASS] Your DB should flag any two concurrent long-acting benzodiazepines as inappropriate chronic therapy unless marked as ‘cross-taper’. [OUT] Same external CYP interactions and alcohol issues as other benzodiazepines; encode at class level for efficiency.	In elderly, the long half-life and active metabolites make Chlordiazepoxide particularly problematic; alternatives with shorter half-lives or non-benzodiazepine strategies should be preferred. If used for alcohol withdrawal in older adults, regimens should be lower-dose and closely monitored.	Not recommended in children except under specialist supervision for severe indications; long half-life raises risk of accumulation and behavioral effects.	Avoid in pregnancy if possible; chronic use is associated with neonatal withdrawal and hypotonia. If required for severe alcohol dependence, maternal and fetal monitoring is mandatory.	Overdose resembles other benzodiazepines: sedation, ataxia, respiratory depression and coma, especially with co-ingested alcohol or opioids. Manage with airway support and observation; flumazenil use is restricted to specialized contexts due to seizure risk.	Store at room temperature, protect from moisture and light. Given its abuse potential, your system should treat it as a controlled drug with restricted repeat prescribing."
3.2	Antipsychotics													
3.2.1	Chlorpromazine	Tablet 100mg tablet, Solution for injection 25mg/ml	First-generation (typical) antipsychotic of the phenothiazine class with strong antipsychotic, sedative, antiemetic and anticholinergic effects.	Dopamine D2 receptor antagonism in mesolimbic pathways reduces psychotic symptoms; also blocks histamine H1, muscarinic and alpha-1 adrenergic receptors, accounting for sedation, anticholinergic and hypotensive effects.	Schizophrenia and other psychoses, acute agitation, intractable hiccups, severe nausea and vomiting (including palliative care), adjunctive sedative in some settings.	Coma, CNS depression, severe cardiovascular disease, bone-marrow depression, pheochromocytoma, history of agranulocytosis with phenothiazines. Caution in Parkinson’s disease, epilepsy, elderly dementia-related psychosis.	Sedation, orthostatic hypotension, tachycardia, anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, urinary retention, constipation), weight gain, photosensitivity, extrapyramidal symptoms (dystonia, akathisia, parkinsonism), tardive dyskinesia with long-term use, QT prolongation and risk of ventricular arrhythmias, neuroleptic malignant syndrome (rare but life-threatening).	Dosing regimens vary widely by indication; for psychosis, oral initiation often 25–100mg 2–3 times daily, titrated upward; maintenance lower. IM forms reserved for acute agitation under close monitoring. Titrate slowly and monitor ECG, metabolic parameters and movement disorders over time.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Chlorpromazine + other QT-prolonging agents in your list (e.g. Haloperidol, certain macrolides/fluoroquinolones when you add them later, and possibly high-dose Tricyclic antidepressants like Amitriptyline) → increased risk of torsades de pointes; your CDSS should flag concurrent prescriptions for ECG and electrolyte monitoring. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Chlorpromazine + benzodiazepines (Diazepam, Lorazepam, Clonazepam, Midazolam, Chlordiazepoxide) or opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Codeine) → significantly additive CNS and respiratory depression, falls, and aspiration risk. [MODERATE-INTRA] Chlorpromazine + antihypertensives (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, beta-blockers, calcium channel blockers, Hydralazine, Diuretics) → pronounced orthostatic hypotension; start low and monitor BP. [MODERATE-INTRA] Chlorpromazine + anticholinergics (Hyoscine, Procyclidine) → worsened constipation, urinary retention and cognitive blunting, especially in elderly. [OUT] Many external CYP2D6 substrates/inhibitors (e.g. certain SSRIs not yet in your list) impact phenothiazine levels; treat as external-class to wire in later.	In elderly, high risk of postural hypotension, falls, cognitive impairment, anticholinergic burden, extrapyramidal symptoms and mortality in dementia-related psychosis. Use only if absolutely necessary and at very low doses with close review.	Generally avoided as antipsychotic in children due to high EPS and metabolic risk; limited short-term use in severe behavioral disorders or refractory hiccups under specialist supervision only.	Avoid use in pregnancy if possible; if needed (e.g. severe psychosis), use the lowest effective dose and monitor maternal BP and fetal growth. There is a risk of neonatal extrapyramidal/withdrawal symptoms if used late in pregnancy.	Overdose causes severe sedation, coma, hypotension, anticholinergic signs (dilated pupils, dry skin), arrhythmias, seizures and hyper- or hypothermia. Management: airway and ventilatory support, continuous ECG, IV fluids, vasopressors (Adrenaline avoided because phenothiazines may reverse its effect; use Noradrenaline/Phenylephrine when available, to be defined as external or added later).	Store at room temperature away from light. Injection ampoules should be protected from excessive heat and used immediately after opening; photosensitivity warnings should be encoded in patient counseling fields.
3.2.2	Haloperidol	Tablet 5mg, solution for injection 5mg/2ml,	High-potency typical antipsychotic primarily used for schizophrenia, acute agitation, delirium, and certain movement disorders (e.g. tics). Has strong D2 antagonism with relatively low anticholinergic effects but higher EPS risk.	Potent antagonism of dopamine D2 receptors in mesolimbic and nigrostriatal pathways reduces psychotic symptoms but also produces extrapyramidal side effects. Modest affinity for alpha-1 and minimal for muscarinic receptors.	Schizophrenia, acute psychotic agitation, delirium (short-term), Tourette’s/tics, antiemetic in palliative care.	Parkinson’s disease, severe CNS depression, coma, known prolonged QT or significant arrhythmias, history of torsades de pointes. Caution with electrolyte disturbances or concurrent QT-prolonging drugs.	Extrapyramidal symptoms (acute dystonia, parkinsonism, akathisia), tardive dyskinesia with chronic use, QT prolongation, torsades de pointes, sedation, orthostatic hypotension (less than chlorpromazine), neuroleptic malignant syndrome, hyperprolactinemia.	Oral: often 0.5–5mg 1–3 times daily depending on indication and patient profile. IM formulations used for acute agitation with cautious dosing and cardiac monitoring; your system should flag maximum daily IM doses and total cumulative dose.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Haloperidol + other QT-prolonging and electrolyte-disturbing drugs in this table (Chlorpromazine, high-dose Tricyclics like Amitriptyline, Diuretics causing hypokalemia such as Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Senna/Bisacodyl with chronic use) → significantly increased torsades de pointes risk; require baseline and follow-up ECG and electrolyte checks. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Haloperidol + benzodiazepines or opioids → sedation and respiratory depression; acute IM haloperidol plus benzodiazepine may be necessary for severe agitation but must be administered with close observation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Haloperidol + other antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Risperidone, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine) → cumulative EPS and QT risk; polypharmacy should be discouraged in your system unless clearly justified. [OUT] External macrolide and fluoroquinolone antibiotics often used in your setting (not in this list yet) also prolong QT; your DB should mark them as OUT-high-risk when you add them.	Older adults, especially with dementia, are at increased risk of stroke, mortality, EPS, falls and QT prolongation; use only for severe agitation or psychosis when non-drug strategies fail, at the lowest effective dose for the shortest time. Your prescribing logic should show specific geriatric warnings.	Use in children only under specialist supervision, typically for severe tics or behavioral disorders. High EPS risk mandates close monitoring and periodic attempts at dose reduction.	Avoid in pregnancy where possible; if required for severe psychosis, document risk–benefit, use minimal effective dose and monitor neonate for extrapyramidal and withdrawal symptoms if used late in gestation.	Overdose leads to severe EPS, dystonia, rigidity, hypotension, deep sedation/coma, arrhythmias and neuroleptic malignant syndrome-like picture. Management: airway support, IV fluids, ECG monitoring, treatment of arrhythmias per ACLS (avoiding antiarrhythmics that further prolong QT where possible), benzodiazepines for severe agitation or dystonia, and intensive supportive care.	Store at room temperature, protect from light. Injection ampoules should be inspected for particulate matter prior to use and discarded if discolored.
3.2.3	Fluphenazine	Solution for injection 25mg/ml	High-potency typical antipsychotic of the phenothiazine group, often formulated as a long-acting depot for maintenance treatment of schizophrenia.	Blocks dopamine D2 receptors in mesolimbic and nigrostriatal pathways, with strong antipsychotic effect and high risk of EPS. Also exhibits alpha-1 adrenergic blockade.	Maintenance treatment of schizophrenia and other chronic psychoses, particularly in patients with poor adherence to oral therapy.	Coma, severe CNS depression, Parkinson’s disease, severe cardiac disease with QT prolongation, bone marrow depression, pheochromocytoma. Caution in elderly and those with seizure disorders.	Extrapyramidal symptoms, tardive dyskinesia, hyperprolactinemia, moderate sedation, orthostatic hypotension, weight gain, QT prolongation, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, cholestatic jaundice (rare).	Depot IM injections given every 2–4 weeks depending on formulation and clinical response; oral dosing (if used) is titrated gradually. Initiation and dose escalation should be done under specialist supervision with ECG and movement disorder monitoring.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Fluphenazine + other QT-prolonging drugs (Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Tricyclics, Diuretics leading to hypokalemia, Senna/Bisacodyl overuse) → cumulative torsades de pointes risk; baseline ECG, K+ and Mg2+ should be checked and monitored. [MODERATE-INTRA] Fluphenazine + benzodiazepines and opioids → enhanced sedation, fall and aspiration risk. [MODERATE-INTRA] Fluphenazine + anticholinergic drugs (Procyclidine, Hyoscine, Trihexyphenidyl if later added) is sometimes deliberate to reduce EPS, but increases cognitive burden, constipation and urinary retention. [OUT] Additive metabolic and cardiac risks occur with external atypical antipsychotics and some antidepressants not yet in the dataset; these should be encoded as OUT polypsychopharmacy risks.	Elderly patients are at high risk of EPS, tardive dyskinesia, falls, orthostatic hypotension and cognitive decline; depot use in this group requires very conservative dosing and regular structured reviews for dose reduction or discontinuation.	Not recommended for routine pediatric use; reserved for severe psychotic disorders failing other treatments under specialist psychiatric care. Monitoring for hormonal changes (prolactin) and growth/weight is essential.	Avoid where possible in pregnancy; depot formulations complicate dose reduction once administered. If use is unavoidable, careful perinatal planning and neonatal monitoring for EPS/withdrawal are required.	Overdose (or accumulation from depot) can cause extreme rigidity, severe EPS, stupor/coma, arrhythmias and hyperthermia. Management involves airway support, ECG monitoring, dopamine agonists or benzodiazepines for acute dystonia, cooling measures and ICU care if NMS develops.	Store injections at recommended temperature (often below 25°C), protect from light and do not freeze. As a depot antipsychotic, your system should force explicit confirmation before each administration due to its long-acting nature.
3.2.4	Risperidone	Solution for injection 25mg/2mL	Long-acting atypical antipsychotic depot formulation for maintenance therapy in schizophrenia and bipolar affective disorder. Improves adherence and provides stable plasma levels.	Dopamine D2 and serotonin 5-HT2A antagonism reduces psychosis while lowering EPS burden compared with typical antipsychotics; alpha-1 blockade causes hypotension; H1 blockade causes sedation and weight gain.	Maintenance therapy for schizophrenia and severe bipolar disorder where adherence is problematic or oral side effects were intolerable.	Known hypersensitivity, severe hypotension, history of neuroleptic malignant syndrome, uncontrolled epilepsy, dementia-related psychosis with elevated mortality risk, severe renal/hepatic impairment requiring close monitoring.	Weight gain, hyperprolactinemia (galactorrhea, amenorrhea, gynecomastia), moderate sedation, orthostatic hypotension, metabolic syndrome, QT prolongation (dose-dependent), akathisia, dystonia, NMS (rare), injection-site pain.	IM depot every 2 weeks, typically 25–37.5mg initially; dose individualized based on clinical response and EPS/metabolic tolerability. Requires 3-week oral overlap on initiation.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Risperidone + Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine or high-dose Amitriptyline → cumulative QT risk, EPS potentiation and orthostatic hypotension. [MODERATE-INTRA] Risperidone + benzodiazepines (Diazepam, Lorazepam, Midazolam, Clonazepam, Chlordiazepoxide) or opioids (Morphine, Codeine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl) → sedation, falls and aspiration. [MODERATE-INTRA] Risperidone + Diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Amiloride/HCTZ) → hypokalemia increases QT risk; ECG + electrolyte monitoring recommended. [CLASS] Anticholinergic drugs (Hyoscine, Trihexyphenidyl if added later) counter EPS but increase cognitive burden, constipation and urinary retention. [OUT] External macrolides/fluoroquinolones further prolong QT; mark as OUT-high-risk for future DB expansion.	Elderly: avoid as chronic therapy in dementia-related psychosis; mortality and stroke risk increased. Use lowest dose and shortest possible duration; monitor orthostasis, falls, metabolic parameters and EPS.	Pediatrics: used for autism-related irritability and severe behavioral disorders under specialist oversight; metabolic risks, weight gain and prolactin elevation require structured monitoring.	Avoid in pregnancy if possible; may cause neonatal EPS/withdrawal if used late in gestation. If unavoidable, document maternal stabilization rationale and plan perinatal monitoring.	Overdose presents with profound sedation, hypotension, EPS, seizures and arrhythmias; treat with airway support, fluids, ECG monitoring, correction of electrolytes and symptomatic EPS management.	Store at recommended temperature (often 2–8°C for depot syringes), protected from light. Do not freeze. Administration requires appropriate cold chain handling.
3.2.5	Risperidone	Tablet 1mg/2mg	Atypical antipsychotic for schizophrenia, bipolar disorder and severe behavioral disturbances.	D2 and 5-HT2A antagonism with additional alpha-1 and H1 activity reduces psychosis and agitation while having lower EPS risk than typical antipsychotics.	Schizophrenia, acute mania, bipolar maintenance, severe autism-related aggression or self-injury.	Hypersensitivity, severe cardiac disease, dementia-related psychosis, uncontrolled epilepsy, severe hepatic/renal dysfunction without monitoring.	Sedation, dizziness, orthostatic hypotension, metabolic syndrome, hyperprolactinemia, EPS at higher doses, QT prolongation, constipation.	Start 1mg twice daily (or 2mg once daily) and titrate to 4–6mg/day based on response and tolerability. Avoid rapid escalation.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Same QT, EPS and orthostasis profile as depot when combined with Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine or Fluphenazine; routine ECG + K+/Mg2+ checks if combined with Diuretics. [MODERATE-INTRA] Sedation risks with benzodiazepines or opioids. [CLASS] External SSRIs increase risperidone serum levels; mark as OUT until added. [OUT] CYP2D6 inhibitors (fluvoxamine, paroxetine, quinidine) markedly increase risperidone levels; encode as OUT-inhibitor class.	Elderly: high-risk for falls, cerebrovascular events, metabolic complications and anticholinergic cumulative burden. Start extremely low and ensure deprescribing reviews.	Useful in adolescents with severe behavioral disorders but requires structured metabolic monitoring and behavior assessment.	Avoid in pregnancy unless severe psychosis requires stabilization; neonatal EPS risk documented near term.	Overdose: severe hypotension, EPS, tachycardia, QT prolongation, seizures; treat with airway support, IV fluids, ECG and electrolyte management.	Store below 25°C, away from humidity; blister protection preferred.
3.2.6	Quetiapine	Tablet 25mg/50mg/100mg	Atypical antipsychotic with low EPS risk and strong sedative profile, widely used for schizophrenia, bipolar disorder and adjunctive major depression.	Antagonist at D2, 5-HT2A, H1 and alpha-1 receptors; weak muscarinic effects. Sedation largely driven by H1 and alpha-1 blockade.	Schizophrenia, acute manic and depressive episodes in bipolar disorder, adjunctive MDD.	Severe hypotension, uncontrolled cardiac arrhythmia, concurrent QT-prolonging polypharmacy, dementia-related psychosis risk, hepatic impairment requiring monitoring.	Sedation, orthostasis, metabolic syndrome (weight gain, dyslipidemia, hyperglycemia), QT prolongation, mild EPS, NMS (rare), anticholinergic effects at high doses.	Start 25–50mg at night and titrate gradually to 300–800mg/day depending on indication and response.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Quetiapine + Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, High-dose Amitriptyline or Diuretics → cumulative QT prolongation; baseline ECG + electrolytes mandatory. [MODERATE-INTRA] Additive sedation with benzodiazepines and opioids. [CLASS] External CYP3A4 inhibitors (azole antifungals, macrolides) strongly prolong quetiapine effect; mark as OUT.	Elderly more sensitive to orthostasis, delirium, metabolic complications and falls; extremely conservative dosing recommended.	Approved in adolescents for bipolar depression and schizophrenia; metabolic and weight gain require structured monitoring.	Avoid during pregnancy unless essential; neonatal EPS or withdrawal reported.	Overdose causes profound sedation, hypotension, tachycardia, QT prolongation; treat with airway support, fluids, ECG monitoring and metabolic correction.	Store at room temperature; XR tablets must not be crushed or chewed.
3.2.7	Olanzapine	Tablet 5mg/10mg	Atypical antipsychotic with potent metabolic side effects and strong sedative profile.	Blocks D2 and 5-HT2A receptors, plus H1, muscarinic and alpha-1 blockade; strong metabolic liability.	Schizophrenia, bipolar mania, bipolar maintenance, severe agitation (IM formulation).	Severe obesity/metabolic syndrome without control, uncontrolled diabetes, hepatic dysfunction, dementia-related psychosis risk.	Weight gain, hyperglycemia, dyslipidemia, sedation, anticholinergic burden, orthostasis, QT prolongation (less than haloperidol), NMS (rare).	Start 5–10mg/day orally; titrate based on response; metabolic labs every 3 months recommended.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Additive sedation with benzodiazepines or opioids. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Combined metabolic and QT risk with Chlorpromazine, Quetiapine and Poly-laxative associated hypokalemia (Senna, Bisacodyl + diuretics) → torsades vulnerability; require ECG + metabolic monitoring. [CLASS] External CYP1A2 inhibitors/inducers alter serum concentrations; OUT-coded.	Elderly: avoid unless necessary; delirium risk, orthostasis, metabolic overload and stroke risk in dementia.	Used with caution in children/adolescents for bipolar illness or schizophrenia; aggressive metabolic monitoring required.	Avoid in pregnancy unless severe psychosis; neonatal EPS/withdrawal possible.	Overdose: deep sedation, hypotension, miosis, seizures, arrhythmias, NMS-like reaction; ICU supportive care required.	Store below 25°C; blister protection preferred.
3.2.8	Clozapine	Tablet 25mg/50mg	Atypical antipsychotic with unique anti-suicidal efficacy for refractory schizophrenia; lowest EPS risk but highest hematological toxicity.	Weak D2 antagonism and strong 5-HT2A, muscarinic, H1 and alpha-1 blockade; causes profound metabolic burden and agranulocytosis risk.	Treatment-resistant schizophrenia; reduction of suicidal behavior in schizophrenia/schizoaffective disorder.	History of agranulocytosis or neutropenia, uncontrolled epilepsy, myocarditis, severe cardiac disease, severe metabolic syndrome.	Agranulocytosis (requires mandatory ANC monitoring), myocarditis, seizures (dose-related), metabolic syndrome, sedation, hypersalivation, orthostasis, constipation leading to ileus, QT prolongation.	Start 12.5mg once or twice daily; titrate slowly to 300–600mg/day with weekly to monthly ANC monitoring depending on protocol.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Clozapine + anticholinergics (Hyoscine, Procyclidine if added later) → severe constipation, ileus or pseudo-obstruction. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Clozapine + laxative-induced hypokalemia (Senna, Bisacodyl + diuretics) → QT prolongation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Clozapine + benzodiazepines or opioids → additive sedation and respiratory depression; risk of aspiration. [OUT] Strong CYP1A2 inhibitors/inducers alter clozapine levels profoundly; OUT-tag until agents added.	Elderly: avoid unless refractory due to extreme sedation, constipation, aspiration and delirium risk.	Not routine in pediatrics; specialist-only therapy for refractory psychosis. Mandatory GI monitoring for constipation.	Avoid in pregnancy if possible; neonatal hypotonia and withdrawal possible. If essential, require multidisciplinary monitoring.	Overdose: severe sedation, hypotension, arrhythmias, seizures, GI ileus; ICU supportive care and continuous cardiac monitoring required.	Store below 25°C in original container; pharmacy-controlled due to ANC monitoring requirement.
3.2.9	Disulfiram	Tablet 25mg/50mg	Alcohol aversion drug used for long-term abstinence support in alcohol use disorder. Produces extremely unpleasant physiological reaction when alcohol is consumed.	Irreversibly inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase, causing acetaldehyde accumulation after any ethanol ingestion. This triggers flushing, headache, tachycardia, nausea, vomiting, hypotension and palpitations — creating a strong deterrent against alcohol intake.	Chronic alcoholism treatment where supervision and consent exist; relapse prevention when psychosocial support also provided.	Severe hepatic disease, psychosis, severe cardiovascular disease, pregnancy, history of severe disulfiram–ethanol reaction, concurrent metronidazole use, cognitive impairment preventing reliable abstinence.	Metallic taste, fatigue, dermatitis, headache, peripheral neuropathy, hepatotoxicity, and severe disulfiram–ethanol reaction with cardiovascular collapse if alcohol is ingested.	250–500mg orally once daily with strict alcohol abstinence; effects persist up to 7–14 days after discontinuation.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Disulfiram + Metronidazole (already in table later under nitroimidazoles) → psychotic reactions, encephalopathy, and severe disulfiram-like effects; ABSOLUTE CONTRAINDICATION. [MODERATE-INTRA] Disulfiram + benzodiazepines (Diazepam, Lorazepam, Clonazepam, Chlordiazepoxide) → slowed hepatic metabolism, prolonged sedation and impaired cognitive recovery. [OUT] Disulfiram + external isoniazid increases delirium risk — OUT until added.	Elderly more prone to hepatic injury, cognitive confusion and falls; use only under strict supervision.	Not used in pediatrics.	Avoid in pregnancy; potential embryotoxicity and severe maternal alcohol reaction risk.	Overdose: vomiting, hypotension, seizures, delirium; treat with supportive care and airway monitoring.	Store at room temperature, protect from moisture and dispense with abstinence warning labels.
