Recent MI, severe arrhythmia, glaucoma, urinary retention, hepatic failure, MAOI use.	Sedation, dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, QT prolongation, sexual dysfunction, EPS exacerbation (via dopamine blockade synergy).	Start 25mg/day and titrate cautiously to 100–250mg/day.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Same QT synergy profile with Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Diuretics + Senna/Bisacodyl → torsades risk; labs + ECG mandatory. [MODERATE-INTRA] Additive sedation with benzodiazepines and opioids. [CLASS] External CYP450 inhibitors require OUT coding.	Elderly: avoid if possible due to delirium, constipation, urinary retention and falls.	Not routine for pediatrics; OCD under specialist oversight possible.	Avoid if possible in pregnancy; neonatal withdrawal or hypotonia possible.	Overdose: severe anticholinergic delirium, seizures, ventricular arrhythmias; treat with airway, benzodiazepines and sodium bicarbonate.	Store at room temperature, away from heat and moisture.
3.5	Mood stabilizers													
3.5.1	Lithium carbonate	Tablets 300mg	Classic mood stabilizer requiring serum level monitoring.	Modulates second messenger systems, enhances serotonergic signaling, affects sodium transport across membranes.	Bipolar disorder maintenance, acute mania.	Renal failure, severe dehydration, sodium imbalance, pregnancy, ACE inhibitor or diuretic interactions.	Tremor, polyuria, hypothyroidism, weight gain, renal impairment; toxicity narrow therapeutic index.	Dose to achieve serum level 0.6–1.2 mmol/L; frequent lab monitoring.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Lithium + Diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Amiloride/HCTZ) → severe toxicity due to sodium loss; mandatory CDSS hard-stop rules. [MODERATE-INTRA] Lithium + ACE inhibitors or ARBs (if added later) → toxicity risk; OUT until added.	Elderly at extreme toxicity risk; avoid if alternatives exist.	Not recommended for pediatrics except specialist bipolar cases.	Avoid in pregnancy; Ebstein’s anomaly risk.	Overdose: coarse tremor, confusion, seizures, renal failure; hemodialysis often required.	Store tightly closed; protect from moisture.
3.5.2	Sodium valproate	Tablets 200mg	Broad-spectrum anticonvulsant and mood stabilizer.	Increases GABA levels by inhibiting GABA transaminase and modulates sodium/calcium channels.	Epilepsy, bipolar disorder, migraine prophylaxis.	Liver disease, urea cycle disorders, pregnancy due to teratogenicity, thrombocytopenia.	Nausea, tremor, weight gain, hair loss, hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis, thrombocytopenia, sedation.	Start 10–15mg/kg/day and titrate; adjust by serum levels for epilepsy.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Sedation increased with benzodiazepines and opioids. [OUT] Protein-binding competition with warfarin, aspirin; OUT-class until added.	Elderly: risk hepatotoxicity; lower starting doses and LFT monitoring.	Not ideal in children <2 due to hepatotoxic risk; specialist supervision required.	Contraindicated in pregnancy due to neural tube defects; avoid unless life-saving.	Overdose: CNS depression, hepatotoxicity, hyperammonemia; treat with L-carnitine and ICU care.	Store at room temperature; protect liquid from light.
3.5.3	Divalproex sodium	Tablet 500mg	Enteric-coated valproate formulation that dissociates to valproate ion; antiepileptic and mood stabilizer.	Increases brain gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) by inhibiting GABA transaminase and possibly enhancing GABA synthesis; blocks voltage-gated sodium channels and T-type calcium channels at higher concentrations, stabilizing neuronal membranes and reducing high-frequency firing.	Focal and generalized seizures (absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic); prophylaxis of migraine; acute and maintenance treatment of bipolar manic episodes.	Hepatic impairment or significant liver disease; urea cycle disorders; known mitochondrial disorders due to POLG mutations; hypersensitivity to valproate; pregnancy when used for migraine or where safer alternatives exist for epilepsy/bipolar (per many guidelines).	Dose-related: nausea, vomiting, dyspepsia, tremor, weight gain, hair loss, sedation; serious: hepatotoxicity (especially in young children), pancreatitis, thrombocytopenia, hyperammonemic encephalopathy, teratogenicity (neural tube defects), menstrual disturbances/PCOS-like features.	Usual adult: 10–15 mg/kg/day PO in divided doses, titrated by 5–10 mg/kg/week to effect; typical maintenance 15–60 mg/kg/day depending on indication and response. Swallow enteric-coated tablets whole; adjust dose in hepatic impairment (often avoid).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Carbamazepine – increased risk of hepatotoxicity and altered levels (mutual induction/inhibition). CRITICAL-INTRA: Phenytoin – complex bidirectional kinetic interaction; may increase free phenytoin and alter valproate levels. CRITICAL-INTRA: Phenobarbitone – valproate inhibits metabolism, increasing barbiturate levels and sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: Lamotrigine – valproate markedly reduces lamotrigine clearance, increasing risk of serious rash (dose of lamotrigine must be reduced). MODERATE-INTRA: Aspirin – displaces valproate from protein binding and inhibits metabolism, increasing free levels and toxicity. CLASS/OUT: Other hepatotoxic drugs (e.g., isoniazid, methotrexate) – additive liver injury. OUT: Carbapenem antibiotics (e.g., meropenem) – can markedly reduce valproate concentrations and efficacy.	Use lower initial doses and slower titration; monitor liver function, platelets, and ammonia periodically; heightened risk of sedation, falls, and hepatotoxicity; consider dose reduction in frail patients.	Use with caution; higher risk of hepatotoxicity in children under 2 years, particularly with polytherapy; use lowest effective dose and close monitoring.	Generally contraindicated or avoided unless no suitable alternative; major teratogen (neural tube defects, craniofacial anomalies, cognitive impairment); use effective contraception if of childbearing potential.	Symptoms: CNS depression, confusion, coma, respiratory depression, hypotension, metabolic acidosis, hyperammonemia, hepatotoxicity; management: supportive care, airway protection, consideration of L-carnitine in severe toxicity, hemodialysis in massive overdose or severe hyperammonemia.	Store below 25°C, in a dry place, protected from light; keep in original blister/container and out of reach of children.
3.6	Antiepileptic medicines													
3.6.1	Carbamazepine	Tablets 200mg, injection 5mg/ml	Dibenzazepine tricyclic antiepileptic with mood-stabilizing and analgesic (neuropathic pain) properties.	Use-dependent blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels, stabilizing hyperexcited neuronal membranes and reducing repetitive firing and synaptic transmission.	Focal (partial) seizures with or without secondary generalization; trigeminal neuralgia; neuropathic pain; bipolar disorder (as a mood stabilizer) in some protocols.	History of bone marrow depression; hepatic porphyria; AV block; concomitant use of monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs); hypersensitivity to carbamazepine or tricyclics; caution in severe hepatic impairment.	Common: dizziness, ataxia, diplopia, nystagmus, nausea, vomiting, hyponatremia (SIADH); serious: agranulocytosis, aplastic anemia, Stevens–Johnson syndrome/toxic epidermal necrolysis (HLA-associated), hepatic dysfunction, cardiac conduction disturbances.	Adult epilepsy: initial 100–200 mg 1–2 times daily, titrated to 800–1,200 mg/day in divided doses; max ~1,600–2,000 mg/day. Trigeminal neuralgia: start 100 mg 1–2 times daily; titrate up to 600–800 mg/day as needed. For IV (where available), use under specialist guidance with careful monitoring.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex sodium – mutual hepatic metabolism interactions and additive hepatotoxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Phenobarbitone, Phenytoin – strong enzyme-inducing combination; may alter levels of each drug and increase CNS depression. MODERATE-INTRA: Sodium valproate (antiepileptic entry) – see above. CLASS/OUT: Other enzyme inducers (e.g., rifampicin) – reduced carbamazepine levels. CLASS/OUT: CYP3A4 substrates (e.g., many statins, oral contraceptives) – reduced efficacy due to induction.	Start at lower doses and titrate slowly; increased risk of hyponatremia, gait disturbance, and cognitive impairment; monitor sodium, CBC, and liver function more closely.	Use with caution; children may clear carbamazepine faster and require higher mg/kg doses; monitor for rash and hematologic toxicity.	Generally category of risk in pregnancy is unfavorable but can be used if benefits outweigh risks; risk of neural tube defects and other malformations – use folic acid and aim for monotherapy at lowest effective dose.	Symptoms: CNS depression, seizures, cardiac conduction abnormalities, hypotension, coma; management: supportive care, cardiac monitoring, activated charcoal if early, consider multiple-dose charcoal in large ingestions.	Store below 25°C, protected from moisture and light; keep in tightly closed container.
3.6.2	Lamotrigine	Tablets 25mg, 50mg	Phenyltriazine antiepileptic and mood stabilizer.	Blocks voltage-sensitive sodium channels, stabilizing neuronal membranes and modulating presynaptic release of excitatory amino acids (e.g., glutamate, aspartate).	Focal and generalized seizures including Lennox–Gastaut syndrome; bipolar disorder (maintenance to delay mood episodes).	Hypersensitivity to lamotrigine; history of serious rash (e.g., Stevens–Johnson syndrome) with prior lamotrigine therapy.	Common: headache, dizziness, diplopia, ataxia, nausea; serious: Stevens–Johnson syndrome/TEN, hypersensitivity reactions with multi-organ involvement, blood dyscrasias, aseptic meningitis.	Adult epilepsy: initial doses depend strongly on whether coadministered with valproate or enzyme inducers. With valproate: start 25 mg every other day, titrating gradually (max 200 mg/day). Without valproate/enzyme inducers: start 25 mg once daily for 2 weeks, then 50 mg once daily for 2 weeks, then increase by 50–100 mg/day every 1–2 weeks (usual 100–500 mg/day). Follow local guidelines for bipolar disorder dosing.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex sodium and Sodium valproate – valproate significantly inhibits lamotrigine metabolism, increasing levels and risk of serious rash; lamotrigine dose must be reduced. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone – enzyme induction increases lamotrigine clearance, reducing levels. OUT: Valproate + other antiepileptic combinations – additive CNS effects; adjust per clinical response.	Elderly may require slower dose titration; monitor for dizziness, gait instability, and rash; no major specific adjustment but consider renal/hepatic status.	Approved for pediatric epilepsy in many age groups; dosing is weight-based and influenced by concomitant valproate/inducers; increased risk of rash, especially with rapid titration – use strict titration schedules.	Pregnancy: use only if benefits outweigh risks; some data suggest modest teratogenic risk; lamotrigine clearance increases in pregnancy, so dose adjustment may be required.	Overdose may cause dizziness, ataxia, nystagmus, seizures, coma, and cardiac conduction disturbances; management is supportive with airway, seizure control, and cardiac monitoring.	Store at room temperature (below 25°C), in a dry place, protected from light; keep in original blister.
3.6.3	Ethosuximide	Capsules 250mg	Succinimide antiepileptic specifically for absence seizures.	Reduces low-threshold T-type calcium currents in thalamic neurons, dampening the thalamocortical oscillations associated with absence seizures.	Absence (petit mal) seizures – especially when other drugs (e.g., valproate) are not suitable or as adjunct.	Hypersensitivity to ethosuximide or succinimides; caution in severe hepatic or renal impairment.	Common: GI upset (nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain), fatigue, dizziness, headache; serious: blood dyscrasias, lupus-like syndrome, Stevens–Johnson syndrome, behavioral changes.	Adult and adolescent: usual starting dose 250–500 mg once daily; titrate in increments of 250 mg at 4–7 day intervals to typical maintenance 1–1.5 g/day in divided doses; children require weight-based dosing (e.g., 20 mg/kg/day).	MODERATE-INTRA: Valproate (Divalproex/Sodium valproate) – valproate may increase ethosuximide levels; additive CNS depression; monitor clinically. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone – enzyme induction may lower ethosuximide levels. OUT: Other CNS depressants – additive sedation.	Use cautiously with lower starting doses; monitor CBC and liver function periodically; watch for CNS adverse effects and falls.	Used in children for absence seizures with weight-based dosing; younger patients may be more prone to behavioral changes and GI intolerance.	Use only if benefits outweigh risks; limited but generally not strongly teratogenic data; absence seizure control may require continuation during pregnancy.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, CNS depression, respiratory depression, coma; treatment is supportive with airway protection and monitoring.	Store at room temperature, tightly closed, protected from moisture and light.
3.6.4	Phenobarbitone	Tablets 30mg, Solution for injection 200mg/ml	Long-acting barbiturate with antiepileptic and sedative properties.	Enhances GABA-mediated chloride influx by prolonging opening of GABA_A receptor channels; at higher doses, may directly activate GABA_A; also decreases excitatory neurotransmission, raising seizure threshold.	Generalized and partial seizures (especially in resource-limited settings); status epilepticus (IV) where first-line benzodiazepines are unavailable; sedation in some contexts.	Severe respiratory depression; significant hepatic impairment; history of barbiturate dependence; acute intermittent porphyria; uncontrolled severe obstructive pulmonary disease.	Common: sedation, cognitive impairment, ataxia, depression, behavioral disturbance (especially in children); chronic: dependence, tolerance; serious: respiratory depression, hypotension (IV), rash, megaloblastic anemia, osteomalacia.	Adult oral: 60–180 mg/day in divided doses for epilepsy; status epilepticus: 10–20 mg/kg IV at a max rate per local protocol with cardiorespiratory monitoring. Dose adjustments required in hepatic impairment.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – valproate inhibits phenobarbital metabolism, increasing sedation and toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin – mutual enzyme induction; alters serum levels of each; monitor concentrations and clinical response. OUT: Other CNS depressants (e.g., opioids like Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl) – additive respiratory depression and sedation. CLASS/OUT: Oral anticoagulants, many other drugs – marked CYP induction reduces their effect.	Greater risk of sedation, cognitive decline, and falls; use lowest effective dose and consider alternative antiepileptics when possible.	Generally avoided if alternatives available due to behavioral and cognitive effects; if used, doses are weight-based and require close monitoring.	Use in pregnancy only if necessary; risk of neonatal sedation, withdrawal, and possible congenital malformations; supplement maternal folate and vitamin K according to guidelines.	Overdose leads to profound CNS and respiratory depression, hypotension, coma, potentially death; management: airway support, vasopressors, enhanced elimination (e.g., urinary alkalinization or hemodialysis in some settings), close monitoring.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; IV solution as per manufacturer’s recommendations (avoid freezing).
3.6.5	Phenytoin	Tablets 100mg, Solution for injection 250mg	Hydantoin antiepileptic with non-sedative profile at therapeutic doses.	Use-dependent blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels, stabilizing neuronal membranes and reducing repetitive firing; exhibits saturable (zero-order) kinetics at higher concentrations.	Tonic-clonic and partial seizures; status epilepticus (IV phenytoin/fosphenytoin) after benzodiazepines.	Sinus bradycardia, sinoatrial block, second- or third-degree AV block (for IV use); history of phenytoin hypersensitivity; pregnancy use with caution due to teratogenic risk.	Common: nystagmus, ataxia, diplopia, gingival hyperplasia, hirsutism, coarsening of facial features; long-term: osteomalacia, peripheral neuropathy; serious: Stevens–Johnson syndrome, blood dyscrasias, hepatotoxicity, arrhythmias (IV), purple glove syndrome.	Oral adult: 3–5 mg/kg/day in 1–2 divided doses; adjust based on levels and clinical response. IV loading for status epilepticus ~15–20 mg/kg at a controlled rate (e.g., ≤50 mg/min) with ECG and BP monitoring; maintenance 4–6 mg/kg/day IV or PO.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – valproate can displace phenytoin from protein binding and inhibit metabolism, increasing free phenytoin; monitor levels and toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenobarbitone – mutual enzyme induction; adjust doses based on clinical response and levels. OUT: Many CYP-metabolized drugs (warfarin, corticosteroids, oral contraceptives, some antiretrovirals) – reduced effect due to induction; monitor and adjust.	Use lower initial doses and monitor serum levels, ECG (if IV), and for CNS adverse effects; high risk of falls and cognitive decline in frail patients.	Use with caution; weight-based dosing; higher risk of cosmetic adverse effects and behavioral issues; monitor carefully.	Teratogenic (fetal hydantoin syndrome, cleft palate, growth deficiency); use only if necessary and preferably as monotherapy at lowest effective dose with folate supplementation.	Overdose causes nystagmus, ataxia, slurred speech, CNS depression, coma, hypotension, arrhythmias; treatment is supportive with cardiac monitoring, airway management; hemodialysis can be considered in severe toxicity.	Store at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; IV formulations per manufacturer guidelines.
3.6.6	Sodium valproate	Tablets 200mg, Syrup 200mg/5ml	Simple valproate salt antiepileptic; formulation with rapid systemic availability; same active moiety as divalproex sodium.	Increases GABA levels (inhibits GABA transaminase, enhances synthesis) and blocks voltage-gated sodium and T-type calcium channels, stabilizing neuronal firing.	Focal and generalized seizures, including absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic; sometimes used for bipolar disorder and migraine prophylaxis (depending on local practice).	As for divalproex sodium: hepatic disease or dysfunction; urea cycle disorders; mitochondrial POLG-related disease; hypersensitivity to valproate; strong pregnancy restrictions where alternatives exist.	Adverse effects profile essentially identical to divalproex: GI disturbance, tremor, weight gain, alopecia; serious hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis, hyperammonemia, thrombocytopenia, teratogenicity.	Adult: 10–15 mg/kg/day PO in divided doses, titrated to 20–30 mg/kg/day (max ~60 mg/kg/day) depending on response; syrup useful for pediatrics and titration; avoid abrupt withdrawal.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Same as for Divalproex – Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone (mutual hepatic interactions, increased toxicity risk); MODERATE-INTRA: Lamotrigine (reduced clearance, higher rash risk). OUT: Carbapenems (e.g., Meropenem), other hepatotoxic drugs – either marked reduction in valproate levels or additive liver injury.	Use cautiously with regular monitoring of LFTs, CBC, and ammonia; consider lower doses in frail or hepatic-compromised elderly.	Widely used in pediatric epilepsy but high risk of hepatotoxicity in young children (<2 years) and severe teratogenic risk later in life; use lowest effective dose and close monitoring.	Same concerns as divalproex: strong teratogen; generally avoided for women of child-bearing potential unless no alternative; effective contraception mandatory if used.	Overdose as for valproate: CNS depression, metabolic acidosis, hyperammonemia; treat supportively, consider L-carnitine, and hemodialysis in severe cases.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; syrup tightly closed; keep out of reach of children.
3.6.7	Gabapentin	Tablets 600mg, Oral solution 250mg/5ml	Structural analogue of GABA used as antiepileptic and for neuropathic pain.	Binds to the α2δ subunit of voltage-gated calcium channels in the CNS, reducing excitatory neurotransmitter release; does not act directly on GABA receptors.	Adjunctive therapy for partial seizures; neuropathic pain (e.g., postherpetic neuralgia, diabetic neuropathy); sometimes used off-label for other pain/anxiety states.	Hypersensitivity to gabapentin; caution in severe renal impairment and in history of substance misuse.	Common: dizziness, somnolence, peripheral edema, weight gain, ataxia; less common: mood changes, visual disturbances; rare: respiratory depression in combination with CNS depressants.	Adult epilepsy/neuropathic pain: initial 300 mg once daily, then titrated to 300 mg three times daily; usual effective range 900–3,600 mg/day in divided doses. Adjust dose in renal impairment. Pediatric dosing is weight-based where indicated.	MODERATE-INTRA: Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – additive CNS and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other sedatives (e.g., benzodiazepines like Diazepam, Midazolam) – increased sedation. OUT: Antacids containing aluminium/magnesium – may reduce gabapentin bioavailability; separate dosing.	Elderly more prone to dizziness, somnolence, and falls; start low, titrate slowly; dose reduction may be required due to reduced renal function.	Approved for pediatric partial seizures in many regions; dosing is weight-based; monitor for behavioral changes and sedation.	Limited but generally not strongly teratogenic data; use only if benefits outweigh risks; adjust dose in pregnancy if needed.	Overdose manifestations: marked somnolence, dizziness, ataxia, hypotension; management is supportive; hemodialysis can remove gabapentin in severe renal impairment.	Store at room temperature, protected from moisture; oral solution refrigerated or as per manufacturer’s recommendations.
3.6.8	Levetiracetam	Tablets 250mg, suspension 100mg/ml, injection 5mg/ml	Pyrrolidone derivative antiepileptic with broad spectrum and favorable interaction profile.	Binds to synaptic vesicle protein SV2A, modulating neurotransmitter release and reducing neuronal excitability; exact mechanism not fully defined.	Adjunctive or monotherapy for focal seizures and some generalized seizures (e.g., myoclonic, tonic-clonic) depending on guidelines.	Hypersensitivity to levetiracetam; caution in severe renal impairment and in patients with significant psychiatric history.	Common: somnolence, asthenia, dizziness; behavioral changes (irritability, aggression, mood swings); rare: psychosis, severe dermatologic reactions.	Adult: initial 500 mg twice daily; may increase by 500 mg twice daily every 2 weeks to a maximum of about 1,500 mg twice daily. Adjust dose according to renal function. IV dosing mirrors oral total daily dose.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (e.g., Morphine, Tramadol, Diazepam) – additive sedation. Advantage: minimal clinically significant CYP interactions with other antiepileptics (Carbamazepine, Valproate, Phenytoin) though additive CNS effects may occur. OUT: Alcohol – increased CNS depression.	Generally well tolerated in elderly; adjust dose based on renal function; monitor for mood or behavioral changes.	Approved for various pediatric seizure syndromes; dosing is weight-based; monitor for behavioral changes.	No major teratogenic signal compared to older antiepileptics, but use only if benefits outweigh risks; adjust dose in pregnancy due to altered clearance.	Overdose may cause somnolence, agitation, respiratory depression; treat supportively; hemodialysis can enhance elimination.	Store at room temperature; protect oral solution from light; use IV solution as per manufacturer guidelines.
3.7	Medicines used in Parkinsonism and related disorders													
3.7.1	Bezhexol	Tablets 5mg	Anticholinergic antiparkinsonian agent (likely trihexyphenidyl under local brand name).	Competitive antagonist at central muscarinic receptors, restoring dopaminergic–cholinergic balance in basal ganglia; also has peripheral anticholinergic effects.	Parkinsonism (idiopathic and drug-induced); adjunct in extrapyramidal symptoms due to antipsychotics such as Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine.	Narrow-angle glaucoma; GI obstruction; urinary retention; myasthenia gravis; uncontrolled tachyarrhythmias; hypersensitivity.	Common: dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, tachycardia; CNS: confusion, agitation, hallucinations (especially at higher doses or in elderly).	Adults: start 1 mg/day, increase by 1–2 mg every few days to typical 6–15 mg/day in divided doses; use lowest effective dose, particularly for antipsychotic-induced extrapyramidal symptoms.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics on list (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine) – therapeutic use to counteract extrapyramidal side effects but increases anticholinergic load (confusion, constipation, urinary retention). MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (e.g., Hyoscine butyl bromide, Atropine) – additive anticholinergic adverse effects. OUT: CNS depressants – increased risk of confusion/sedation in combination.	Avoid or use extreme caution in elderly due to high risk of confusion, delirium, urinary retention, constipation, and falls; favor lowest possible dose or alternatives.	Use generally avoided in young children; if used for dystonia or EPS, dosing is weight-based with close monitoring.	Use only if clearly indicated; potential for fetal anticholinergic effects is poorly defined; avoid in pregnancy where possible.	Overdose: severe anticholinergic syndrome (hyperthermia, mydriasis, delirium, tachycardia, urinary retention, seizures); management includes supportive care, physostigmine in selected severe cases under specialist supervision.	Store below 25°C, protected from light and moisture; keep out of reach of children.
3.7.2	Bromocriptine	Tablets 2.5mg	Ergot-derived dopamine agonist acting on D2 receptors.	Stimulates D2 receptors and inhibits prolactin secretion; in Parkinson’s disease, augments dopaminergic activity in the striatum.	Parkinsonism (as adjunct to levodopa or other therapy); hyperprolactinemia, prolactinomas; suppression of lactation where indicated.	Uncontrolled hypertension; severe cardiovascular disease; history of ergot-induced vasospasm; uncontrolled psychosis; hypersensitivity to ergot derivatives.	Common: nausea, vomiting, orthostatic hypotension, headache, dizziness; serious: cardiac valvulopathy (mainly at higher doses), fibrosis (retroperitoneal, pleural), psychosis, impulse-control disorders.	Parkinsonism: start 1.25 mg once daily with food, increase gradually to typical 10–30 mg/day in divided doses; hyperprolactinemia usually needs lower doses (e.g., 1.25–2.5 mg 2–3 times daily).	MODERATE-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – functional antagonism (antipsychotics block dopamine, bromocriptine stimulates), reducing efficacy of both and increasing risk of EPS/psychosis. OUT: Antihypertensives (e.g., Atenolol, Amlodipine, etc.) – additive hypotension; adjust monitoring. CLASS/OUT: Other ergot derivatives – increased risk of vasospasm and fibrosis.	Elderly are at higher risk of orthostatic hypotension, hallucinations, and confusion; start very low and titrate slowly.	Use with caution; not first-line in pediatric patients; if used for prolactinomas, dose is weight and response based.	Avoid in pregnancy for Parkinsonism unless essential; may be used for prolactinomas in some settings with specialist guidance; monitor blood pressure closely.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, hypotension, hallucinations, confusion; treatment is supportive, including gastric decontamination if early and cardiovascular monitoring.	Store in tightly closed container at room temperature, protected from light and moisture.
3.7.3	Procyclidine	Tablets 5mg, injection 5mg/ml, (2ml)	Synthetic anticholinergic antiparkinsonian and anti-EPS agent.	Central muscarinic receptor antagonist, reducing cholinergic tone in the basal ganglia and helping correct dopamine–acetylcholine imbalance; also peripheral anticholinergic effects.	Parkinsonism; treatment of extrapyramidal symptoms (acute dystonia, drug-induced parkinsonism) due to antipsychotics like Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, etc.	Narrow-angle glaucoma; GI obstruction; urinary retention; myasthenia gravis; tachyarrhythmias; hypersensitivity to procyclidine.	Dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, tachycardia; CNS effects including confusion, agitation, hallucinations especially at higher doses or in the elderly.	Oral: usually 2.5–5 mg three times daily, adjusted according to response; in acute dystonic reactions, 5–10 mg IM/IV may be used, repeated as needed per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics on list (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine) – used to treat their EPS but increases cumulative anticholinergic load and risk of confusion, constipation, urinary retention. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Bezhexol, Hyoscine butyl bromide, Atropine) – additive adverse effects. OUT: CNS depressants – additive confusion and sedation.	Avoid where possible in elderly due to high risk of delirium and falls; if used, lowest possible dose and close monitoring.	Not usually first-line in children; may be used for acute dystonia under specialist supervision with weight-based dosing.	Avoid in pregnancy unless clearly needed; data are limited; theoretical risk of fetal anticholinergic effects.	Overdose causes anticholinergic syndrome: hyperthermia, mydriasis, flushed skin, tachycardia, delirium, seizures; supportive management, benzodiazepines for agitation, physostigmine in carefully selected severe cases.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; keep injection away from extremes of temperature.
3.8	Medicines used for Nausea and Vomiting													
3.8.1	Cyclizine	Tablets 50mg, Solution for injection 50mg/ml	First-generation antihistamine with antiemetic and anticholinergic properties.	H1 receptor antagonist with central anticholinergic and antiserotonergic effects in the vomiting center and vestibular pathways, reducing motion sickness and nausea.	Prevention and treatment of nausea and vomiting including postoperative, motion sickness, and in some vertigo conditions.	Severe heart failure; neonates; hypersensitivity; caution in glaucoma, urinary retention, severe hepatic impairment.	Drowsiness, dry mouth, blurred vision, urinary retention, constipation; less common: headache, palpitations, hypotension, confusion (especially in elderly).	Adults: 50 mg PO up to 3 times daily (max 150 mg/day); IM/IV 50 mg up to 3 times daily as needed, according to local protocols.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Promethazine) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Hyoscine butyl bromide, Procyclidine, Bezhexol) – additive anticholinergic effects. OUT: Alcohol – increased sedation.	Use cautiously; increased sensitivity to anticholinergic and sedative effects; higher risk of confusion and falls.	Use with caution; avoid in neonates; dosing in children is weight/age-based per local guidelines; increased risk of paradoxical excitation.	Avoid in late pregnancy unless clearly indicated; may cause neonatal sedation if used near delivery.	Overdose: anticholinergic and antihistaminic toxicity – agitation, hallucinations, seizures, tachycardia, arrhythmias; manage supportively with cardiac monitoring and benzodiazepines for seizures.	Store at room temperature away from light; keep ampoules in outer carton.
3.8.2	Domperidone	Tablets 10mg	Peripheral dopamine D2 receptor antagonist with antiemetic and prokinetic effects.	Blocks D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone (outside the BBB) and in the GI tract, enhancing gastric motility and accelerating gastric emptying.	Nausea and vomiting of various causes; symptomatic relief of upper GI motility disorders such as gastroparesis (subject to regulatory restrictions).	Prolactin-releasing pituitary tumor; known QT prolongation, significant cardiac disease, or concomitant QT-prolonging drugs; moderate to severe hepatic impairment.	Headache, dry mouth, abdominal cramps, diarrhea; increased prolactin leading to galactorrhea or gynecomastia; rare: QT prolongation and serious arrhythmias.	Adult: 10 mg up to three times daily, 15–30 minutes before meals; maximum dose and duration may be restricted by local regulatory guidance (e.g., shortest duration at lowest dose).	MODERATE-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – antagonism at dopamine receptors may oppose domperidone’s effect and increase risk of extrapyramidal symptoms with some agents. OUT: QT-prolonging drugs (e.g., some macrolides like Clarithromycin, fluoroquinolones like Ciprofloxacin, antipsychotics) – additive QT risk. OUT: Potent CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., Azole antifungals like Itraconazole, macrolides) – increased domperidone levels and arrhythmia risk.	Increased risk of cardiac arrhythmias and sudden death, particularly >60 years; use with great caution, at lowest dose and shortest duration or avoid.	Not recommended in very young infants; in older children use weight-based dosing with caution and cardiac risk assessment if prolonged use anticipated.	Avoid during pregnancy unless benefit clearly outweighs risk; limited human data; avoid high doses or prolonged use.	Overdose: extrapyramidal symptoms (rare), agitation, arrhythmias; management is supportive with ECG monitoring; anticholinergics or benzodiazepines for dystonia if present.	Store below 25°C, protected from moisture and light.
3.8.3	Metoclopramide	Tablets 10mg, Solution for injection 5mg/ml (2ml)	Dopamine D2 antagonist with antiemetic and prokinetic activity.	Blocks D2 receptors centrally in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and peripherally in GI tract; at higher doses also antagonizes 5-HT3 receptors and agonizes 5-HT4 receptors, enhancing gastric emptying and upper GI motility.	Nausea and vomiting (postoperative, chemotherapy-related, migraine-associated); diabetic gastroparesis; to facilitate gastric emptying and procedures (e.g., small bowel intubation).	Pheochromocytoma, GI hemorrhage or obstruction, perforation, epilepsy, history of tardive dyskinesia with dopamine antagonists; children <1 year (high risk of extrapyramidal reactions).	Common: drowsiness, fatigue, diarrhea, restlessness; serious: acute dystonic reactions (especially in young adults), Parkinsonian symptoms, tardive dyskinesia with long-term use, hyperprolactinemia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome (rare).	Adult: 10 mg up to three times daily PO/IM/IV, 30 minutes before meals; for IV, give slowly; limit duration (often ≤5 days) to reduce risk of extrapyramidal adverse effects.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – additive dopamine blockade, greatly increased risk of extrapyramidal symptoms and tardive dyskinesia. MODERATE-INTRA: Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – may reduce opioid-induced nausea but adds CNS depression; monitor. OUT: Other QT-prolonging drugs – additive risk of arrhythmia.	Use cautiously in elderly due to higher risk of extrapyramidal effects and tardive dyskinesia; use lowest effective dose and shortest duration.	Avoid in very young children; use with extreme caution in older children with strict adherence to weight-based dosing and maximum duration (often maximum 5 days).	Avoid in pregnancy unless clearly needed, particularly near term (risk of extrapyramidal effects in neonate); however, often considered relatively safe if short term and at lowest dose when benefits outweigh risks.	Overdose: drowsiness, confusion, extrapyramidal reactions, cardiac conduction abnormalities; treat with supportive care, consider anticholinergic or benzodiazepine for dystonia, and ECG monitoring.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; do not freeze injection.
3.8.4	Prochlorperazine	Tablets 5mg	Phenothiazine antipsychotic used primarily at low dose as antiemetic.	Blocks dopamine D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and limbic system; also has anticholinergic and antihistaminic properties.	Nausea and vomiting; vertigo; at higher doses for psychotic disorders.	Severe CNS depression, coma; bone marrow suppression; pheochromocytoma; hepatic failure; children <2 years or <10 kg (formulation-specific).	Sedation, orthostatic hypotension, anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, blurred vision), extrapyramidal symptoms, QT prolongation, rare agranulocytosis, neuroleptic malignant syndrome.	Antiemetic: 5–10 mg two or three times daily PO; maximum dose per local guidelines; higher doses for psychiatric indications under specialist supervision.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – additive dopamine blockade and EPS risk. MODERATE-INTRA: Metoclopramide – markedly increased risk of dystonia/EPS when combined. OUT: QT-prolonging agents (e.g., Ciprofloxacin, Macrolides, Domperidone) – additive arrhythmia risk.	Elderly have higher risk of orthostatic hypotension, sedation, EPS, and falls; use lowest dose and consider alternatives in dementia.	Avoid in very young children; in older pediatric patients, restrict to specific indications with strict weight-based dosing and monitoring for EPS.	Avoid or restrict in pregnancy; phenothiazines have some historical use for hyperemesis but should be used only when benefits clearly outweigh risks and at low doses.	Overdose: marked sedation, hypotension, EPS, arrhythmias; treat with supportive measures, airway management, fluids/vasopressors, and ECG monitoring; consider anticholinergics for dystonia.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; keep in well-closed containers.
3.8.5	Promethazine	Tablet 25mg, Solution for injection 25mg/ml (2ml)	First-generation antihistamine with potent sedative and antiemetic properties.	H1 receptor antagonist with anticholinergic and antidopaminergic actions; depresses the labyrinthine function and chemoreceptor trigger zone, reducing nausea and vomiting; strong sedative effects via central H1 blockade.	Allergic conditions (rhinitis, urticaria); nausea and vomiting; premedication and sedation; motion sickness.	Children <2 years (risk of severe respiratory depression); comatose states; hypersensitivity; caution in asthma, sleep apnea, glaucoma, urinary retention.	Sedation, dizziness, confusion, dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention; serious: respiratory depression, especially in young children; neuroleptic malignant syndrome (rare), leukopenia.	Adult oral: 10–25 mg at night or 25 mg 2–3 times daily depending on indication; IM/IV doses of 12.5–25 mg for perioperative use according to local protocols; IV must be given slowly and cautiously.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Hyoscine, Procyclidine, Bezhexol) – increased anticholinergic adverse effects. OUT: Alcohol – greatly increased sedation.	High risk of confusion, falls, urinary retention, and anticholinergic adverse effects in elderly; avoid or use minimal doses with close monitoring.	Contraindicated in children under 2 years; for older children use lowest effective dose; risk of respiratory depression and paradoxical excitation.	Avoid in late pregnancy and near delivery due to risk of neonatal CNS depression; use only when benefits outweigh risks.	Overdose: pronounced sedation to coma, respiratory depression, anticholinergic signs, seizures; treatment is supportive with airway protection, activated charcoal if early, benzodiazepines for seizures, and monitoring.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; injections in original packaging.
3.8.6	Ondansetron	Tablets 4mg, Oral suspension 2mg, Solution for injection 8mg/2ml	Selective 5-HT3 receptor antagonist antiemetic.	Blocks serotonin 5-HT3 receptors centrally in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and peripherally on vagal nerve terminals, preventing chemotherapy- and surgery-induced nausea and vomiting.	Prevention and treatment of nausea and vomiting associated with chemotherapy, radiotherapy, and postoperative state.	Hypersensitivity to ondansetron or other 5-HT3 antagonists; caution in congenital long QT syndrome, severe hepatic impairment.	Headache, constipation, flushing; rare: QT interval prolongation, arrhythmias, serotonin syndrome when combined with other serotonergic drugs.	Adult: 4–8 mg PO 1–2 times daily for chemotherapy or postoperative nausea; IV 4–8 mg slowly before chemotherapy or surgery as per guideline; adjust dose in hepatic impairment.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other QT-prolonging medicines (e.g., Prochlorperazine, Metoclopramide, Domperidone, some antipsychotics like Haloperidol) – additive QT risk. OUT: Serotonergic agents (SSRIs like Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram; Tramadol) – rare risk of serotonin syndrome; monitor clinically.	Generally well tolerated; consider ECG monitoring if multiple QT-prolonging agents or cardiac risk; adjust dose if hepatic function is impaired.	Commonly used in children with weight-based dosing; good safety profile when dosed appropriately; monitor for constipation and QT issues in high-risk groups.	Generally considered relatively safe in pregnancy (especially for hyperemesis gravidarum) but use only when clearly indicated; follow local guidelines.	Overdose may cause visual disturbances, severe constipation, hypotension, QT prolongation, and arrhythmias; management is supportive with ECG monitoring.	Store at room temperature, away from light and moisture; protect injection from light.
3.9	Analgesics													
3.9.1	Non-opioids analgesics													
3.9.1.1	Paracetamol (Acetaminophen)	Tablets 100mg, 500mg, Suppository 125mg, 250mg, Syrup 120mg/5ml,	Analgesic and antipyretic with minimal anti-inflammatory activity.	Inhibits central cyclooxygenase (COX) enzymes and prostaglandin synthesis, raising pain threshold and reducing fever; negligible peripheral anti-inflammatory effect at therapeutic doses.	Mild to moderate pain and fever in adults and children; adjunct to opioid analgesics for moderate to severe pain.	Severe hepatic impairment, acute liver failure; hypersensitivity to paracetamol; caution in chronic alcohol use and malnutrition.	Generally well tolerated at therapeutic doses; rash, rare thrombocytopenia; serious: dose-dependent hepatotoxicity in overdose or chronic excessive dosing.	Adults: 500–1,000 mg every 4–6 hours as needed (max 3–4 g/day depending on guideline). Children: 10–15 mg/kg per dose every 4–6 hours (maximum daily dose according to age/weight). Rectal doses similar on mg/kg basis with longer dosing intervals.	MODERATE-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – additive hepatotoxic risk; monitor liver function if used chronically together. OUT: Chronic alcohol use and enzyme-inducing antiepileptics (Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone) – increased formation of hepatotoxic metabolites with overdose risk. OUT: Warfarin and other vitamin K antagonists – prolonged regular high-dose use may enhance anticoagulant effect.	Elderly usually tolerate standard doses but maximum daily dose often limited to 3 g/day; monitor liver function with chronic use or polypharmacy.	Widely used and first-line antipyretic/analgesic; doses strictly weight-based; educate caregivers to avoid unintentional overdose from multiple paracetamol-containing products.	Generally considered safe in pregnancy at recommended doses; analgesic/antipyretic of choice when needed, using lowest effective dose for shortest duration.	Early overdose: nausea, vomiting, diaphoresis; later: right upper quadrant pain, elevated liver enzymes, hepatic failure; management: rapid assessment of amount/time, measurement of serum levels where available, and administration of N-acetylcysteine as soon as possible.	Store below 25°C, in a dry place; protect syrup from light; keep out of reach of children.
3.9.1.2	Paracetamol (Acetaminophen)	Solution for injection 10mg/ml	IV formulation of paracetamol for analgesia and antipyresis when oral/rectal routes are unsuitable.	Same as oral paracetamol: central inhibition of prostaglandin synthesis via COX inhibition, providing analgesic and antipyretic effects.	Short-term treatment of moderate pain and fever when IV route is clinically appropriate (e.g., perioperative, critically ill patients).	Severe hepatic impairment or active liver disease; hypersensitivity to paracetamol; severe volume depletion or hemodynamic instability without careful monitoring.	Same as oral but IV route may cause local pain, injection-site reactions, hypotension (especially with rapid infusion), and rare anaphylactoid reactions.	Adult: typically 1 g IV every 6 hours (or 650 mg every 4 hours) with maximum daily dose 4 g; infused over ≥15 minutes. Pediatric doses are weight-based per guideline; maximum daily totals not to be exceeded.	MODERATE-INTRA: As with oral paracetamol – additive hepatotoxic risk with Divalproex/Sodium valproate and enzyme inducers (Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone). OUT: Concurrent multiple paracetamol-containing products – cumulative dose toxicity; ensure total daily dose within limit.	Use with caution in elderly with hepatic impairment, malnutrition, or polypharmacy; consider lower maximum daily doses.	Dose is strictly weight-based; avoid exceeding recommended daily total; monitor liver function in prolonged use or in malnourished/chronically ill children.	Preferred antipyretic/analgesic IV option in pregnancy when needed; use lowest effective dose for shortest duration; same hepatotoxicity concerns as oral.	Overdose: identical hepatic toxicity pattern as oral but can occur more quickly with IV misdosing; treat promptly with N-acetylcysteine and supportive measures.	Store below 25°C; do not refrigerate or freeze; protect from light; use immediately after opening in line with manufacturer’s instructions.
3.9.2	Non-Steroidal Anti-Inflammatory													
3.9.2.1	Aspirin	Tablets 75mg, 300mg	 300mg	Salicylate with antiplatelet, analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory effects.	Irreversibly inhibits cyclooxygenase (COX-1 and COX-2), reducing prostaglandin and thromboxane synthesis; at low doses, selectively blocks platelet COX-1, inhibiting thromboxane A2 and platelet aggregation for the platelet lifespan.	Low-dose: secondary prevention of myocardial infarction, stroke, and other thrombotic events; high-dose: mild to moderate pain, fever, and inflammatory conditions (e.g., rheumatic disease) depending on guidelines.	Active peptic ulcer; bleeding disorders; history of aspirin-induced asthma or NSAID hypersensitivity; severe renal or hepatic impairment; children with viral infections (risk of Reye’s syndrome).	Dyspepsia, GI bleeding, bruising; tinnitus at high doses; serious: GI ulceration, hemorrhage, hypersensitivity reactions (bronchospasm, anaphylaxis), Reye’s syndrome in children.	Low-dose antiplatelet: 75–150 mg once daily. Analgesic/anti-inflammatory: 300–900 mg every 4–6 hours up to max daily dose per guideline (often 4 g).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – displaces valproate from protein binding and may inhibit its metabolism, increasing free valproate levels and hepatotoxic risk. MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Ibuprofen, Diclofenac, Mefenamic acid) – increased GI bleeding risk. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Enoxaparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk. OUT: Corticosteroids (Prednisolone, Dexamethasone) – additive GI ulceration risk.	Use cautiously due to high risk of GI bleeding and renal impairment; co-prescribe gastroprotection where indicated; consider lower doses or alternative agents.	Avoid for analgesia/antipyresis in children due to Reye’s syndrome risk; may be used in specific cardiac conditions under specialist care.	Use in pregnancy near term is generally avoided (risk of premature closure of ductus arteriosus and maternal bleeding); low-dose aspirin may be used in selected high-risk obstetric conditions under specialist guidance.	Acute overdose: tinnitus, vomiting, hyperventilation, metabolic acidosis, hyperthermia, confusion, pulmonary edema, coma; management includes activated charcoal, correction of acid–base disturbances, and hemodialysis in severe poisoning.
3.9.2.2	Ibuprofen	Tablets 200mg, 400mg	 400mg	Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) with analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory actions.	Non-selective inhibitor of COX-1 and COX-2, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis, thus reducing inflammation, pain, and fever.	Mild to moderate pain, dysmenorrhea, inflammatory conditions (e.g., arthritis), and fever.	Active or history of GI ulceration/bleeding, severe heart failure, severe renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID-induced asthma or hypersensitivity.	Dyspepsia, nausea, abdominal pain; serious: GI bleeding/ulceration, renal impairment, fluid retention, increased cardiovascular risk with long-term use, bronchospasm in susceptible individuals.	Adults: 200–400 mg every 6–8 hours as needed; maximum daily dose typically 1,200–2,400 mg depending on indication and guideline. Lower doses are used OTC.	MODERATE-INTRA: Aspirin, Diclofenac, Mefenamic acid – additive GI bleeding and renal risk with other NSAIDs. OUT: ACE inhibitors (Captopril, Lisinopril, Enalapril), ARBs (Losartan, Telmisartan), Diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorthiazide, Amiloride) – reduced antihypertensive/diuretic effect and increased nephrotoxicity risk. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk.	Elderly at higher risk of GI bleeding, renal impairment, and cardiovascular events; use lowest effective dose for shortest duration with gastroprotection where indicated.	Use short-term and at lowest effective dose in children, strictly weight-based; avoid in dehydration due to renal risk.	Avoid in third trimester (risk of premature closure of ductus arteriosus, fetal renal impairment); use with caution in earlier pregnancy if benefit outweighs risk.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, epigastric pain, drowsiness; severe cases may cause metabolic acidosis, renal failure, seizures; management is mainly supportive, activated charcoal if early, monitoring of renal function.
3.9.2.3	Diclofenac	Tablets 50mg, 100mg, Solution for injection 75mg, spray	NSAID of phenylacetic acid class with strong anti-inflammatory and analgesic effects.	Non-selective COX inhibitor with some preference for COX-2; reduces prostaglandin synthesis and inflammatory mediators.	Acute pain, postoperative pain, musculoskeletal and rheumatic disorders, dysmenorrhea.	Active peptic ulcer or GI bleeding, severe heart failure, significant renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID hypersensitivity, third-trimester pregnancy.	Dyspepsia, abdominal pain, nausea; serious: GI bleeding and ulceration, renal impairment, edema, increased cardiovascular risk (MI, stroke), hepatotoxicity, injection-site reactions (for IM).	Adults: orally 50 mg 2–3 times daily or 100 mg modified-release once/twice daily; IM 75 mg as a deep injection, often once daily for short durations; follow local protocols.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Aspirin, Ibuprofen, Mefenamic acid) – significant additive GI and renal toxicity. OUT: ACE inhibitors/ARBs and Diuretics (Captopril, Lisinopril, Losartan, Frusemide, Hydrochlorthiazide, Spironolactone) – decreased antihypertensive effect, increased risk of renal impairment. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk.	Avoid or use with great caution in elderly; high risk of GI and cardiovascular adverse effects; use minimal effective dose for shortest duration with gastroprotection.	Use in children is limited and formulation-dependent; follow weight-based dosing where approved; monitor GI and renal function.	Avoid in third trimester; use in first and second trimester only if essential and at lowest effective dose; increased risk of miscarriage and cardiac malformations has been reported with NSAIDs.	Overdose: GI irritation, renal failure, hypotension, CNS depression, rarely liver injury; management is supportive, activated charcoal early, monitoring of renal and hepatic function.	Store at room temperature in a dry place; protect from light; injection stored as per manufacturer guidance.
3.9.2.4	Mefenamic acid	Tablets 250mg, 500mg, Suppository 125mg	NSAID of anthranilic acid (fenamate) class used mainly for dysmenorrhea and pain.	Non-selective inhibition of COX-1 and COX-2, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis and thus pain and inflammation.	Mild to moderate pain, primary dysmenorrhea, menorrhagia (depending on guideline).	Active peptic ulcer disease, inflammatory bowel disease, severe renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID-induced asthma or hypersensitivity.	GI upset (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea), abdominal pain; serious: GI bleeding/ulceration, renal impairment, rare hemolytic anemia and blood dyscrasias, hypersensitivity reactions.	Adults: 500 mg initially, then 250–500 mg every 6–8 hours as needed for short-term use; limit duration (often ≤7 days).	MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Aspirin, Ibuprofen, Diclofenac) – additive GI and renal toxicity. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk. OUT: ACE inhibitors/ARBs and Diuretics – decreased antihypertensive effect and increased risk of renal dysfunction.	Use cautiously and for shortest possible duration; elderly have increased risk of GI and renal adverse effects; consider alternative analgesics.	Use only with caution in adolescents; avoid prolonged use; monitor for GI and renal issues.	Avoid especially in third trimester; use earlier in pregnancy only if absolutely necessary and short term.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, drowsiness; serious cases can cause seizures, renal failure, metabolic acidosis; treatment is supportive; consider activated charcoal early.	Store at room temperature in a dry, cool place; protect from moisture.
3.9.3	Opioid analgesics		Strong opioid analgesic; prototype μ-opioid receptor agonist.	Binds to μ-opioid receptors in CNS and GI tract, inhibiting ascending pain pathways, altering perception and response to pain; causes generalized CNS depression and reduced GI motility.	Moderate to severe acute and chronic pain (e.g., postoperative, cancer pain); pulmonary edema in some settings.	Significant respiratory depression; acute asthma or severe COPD; paralytic ileus; raised intracranial pressure in uncontrolled head injury; hypersensitivity to morphine; caution in elderly, renal/hepatic impairment.	Common: constipation, nausea, vomiting, sedation, pruritus, urinary retention; serious: respiratory depression, hypotension, bradycardia, dependence and tolerance, confusion, myoclonus at high doses.	Dosing must be individualized. Parenteral adult starting doses: 2.5–10 mg IM/IV/SC every 4 hours as needed, titrated to pain and respiratory status. Oral doses depend on formulation (immediate vs. sustained release) and prior opioid exposure. Use standard opioid titration protocols.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – additive sedation and respiratory depression; monitor closely. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol – additive opioid effects and risk of serotonin syndrome/seizures. OUT: Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (not in list) – risk of severe CNS and respiratory depression or excitation.	Elderly particularly sensitive to respiratory depression and confusion; start very low and titrate cautiously; monitor for constipation and falls.	Use with great caution; dosing is weight-based; monitor for respiratory depression, especially in opioid-naïve children; avoid codeine-like combinations confusion, ensure safe dosing.	Use only when benefits outweigh fetal and neonatal risks; may cause neonatal respiratory depression and withdrawal; prolonged use in pregnancy requires neonatal monitoring.	Overdose: pinpoint pupils, profound respiratory depression, coma, hypotension; treatment with airway support and IV naloxone titrated to adequate ventilation; continuous monitoring needed due to shorter naloxone half-life than morphine.	Store in a secure, locked cabinet in accordance with controlled-drug regulations; protect solution from light; discard opened ampoules as per policy.
3.9.3.1	Morphine	Powder 5g, Solution for injection 10mg/ml ampoule	Synthetic opioid analgesic (also known as meperidine).	μ-opioid receptor agonist with additional local anesthetic-like properties; metabolized to normeperidine, which has CNS excitatory effects (proconvulsant).	Moderate to severe pain, including obstetric analgesia, although use has declined in many guidelines due to safety concerns.	Significant respiratory depression; MAOI use within 14 days; severe hepatic or renal impairment (due to metabolite accumulation); history of seizures; hypersensitivity to pethidine.	Sedation, nausea, vomiting, constipation, orthostatic hypotension; serious: respiratory depression, seizures (due to normeperidine accumulation), serotonin syndrome with serotonergic agents.	Adult: 25–100 mg IM/SC/slow IV every 3–4 hours as required, maximum daily dose per local guidance; avoid prolonged repeated dosing, especially in renal or hepatic impairment.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (not in list) – risk of life-threatening serotonin syndrome and excitation. MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Fentanyl, Tramadol, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol and serotonergic antidepressants (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine) – increased serotonin syndrome risk.	Use with great caution; high risk of confusion, falls, and metabolite accumulation leading to seizures; often avoided in elderly in favor of safer opioids.	Avoid in neonates and infants; in older children use with caution and weight-based dosing; risk of respiratory depression and seizures.	Use with caution in labor; crosses placenta and may cause neonatal respiratory depression; avoid in late pregnancy unless adequate neonatal resuscitation is available.	Overdose: respiratory depression, CNS depression, seizures, hypotension; treat with airway support, naloxone for opioid effect, benzodiazepines for seizures, and monitoring for recurrent toxicity due to metabolite.	Store securely as controlled drug, protected from light; avoid extreme temperatures; follow local narcotic storage regulations.
3.9.3.2	Pethidine	Tablets 50mg, Solution for injection 50mg/ml	Atypical centrally acting analgesic with weak opioid and monoaminergic activity.	Weak μ-opioid receptor agonist and inhibitor of serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake in the CNS, modulating pain pathways.	Moderate pain where non-opioid analgesics are inadequate; sometimes as step-2 analgesic in WHO pain ladder.	Acute intoxication with alcohol, hypnotics, opioids, or psychotropics; uncontrolled epilepsy; concomitant or recent (14 days) MAOI use; severe respiratory depression.	Nausea, vomiting, dizziness, sedation, dry mouth, sweating, constipation; serious: seizures (especially at high doses or with predisposition), serotonin syndrome with serotonergic drugs, respiratory depression, dependence.	Adults: 50–100 mg PO/IM/IV every 4–6 hours as needed (max 400 mg/day; lower in elderly or hepatic/renal impairment).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Serotonergic antidepressants (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine) – increased risk of serotonin syndrome and seizures. MODERATE-INTRA: Other opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl) and CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive respiratory and CNS depression. OUT: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin – induce metabolism, reducing tramadol efficacy and potentially increasing neurotoxicity.	Use reduced doses and longer dosing intervals; increased risk of confusion, falls, and seizures; avoid or use cautiously in patients with dementia or seizure history.	Not recommended in children under some ages per evolving guidance (e.g., <12 years) due to variable metabolism and risk of respiratory depression; avoid in post-tonsillectomy/adenoidectomy patients.	Avoid in late pregnancy due to neonatal respiratory depression and withdrawal; use only if benefits outweigh risks; avoid during breastfeeding in some guidelines due to infant toxicity risk.	Overdose: CNS depression, seizures, respiratory depression, hypotension, serotonin syndrome; treatment includes airway support, benzodiazepines for seizures, consideration of naloxone (may only partially reverse and potentially precipitate seizures).	Store at room temperature, in tightly closed container; keep out of reach of children; handle as controlled substance where applicable.
3.9.3.3	Tramadol	Tablets/Capsule 50mg; Solution for injection 50mg/ml	Highly potent synthetic opioid analgesic.	Selective μ-opioid receptor agonist with rapid onset and short duration (IV); transdermal patches provide sustained systemic delivery; 50–100 times more potent than morphine.	Severe acute pain (perioperative, ICU) and chronic cancer pain (especially with transdermal patches in opioid-tolerant patients).	Significant respiratory depression; acute severe asthma or respiratory compromise; patients not opioid-tolerant (for high-dose patches); hypersensitivity to fentanyl or adhesives; caution in increased intracranial pressure and bradyarrhythmias.	Profound respiratory depression, bradycardia, chest wall rigidity (with rapid IV infusion), nausea, vomiting, constipation, sedation, dependence and tolerance.	IV: 25–100 mcg given slowly for analgesia or as part of anesthesia, titrated to response; higher doses used in anesthesia protocols. Transdermal: apply patch delivering 12–100 mcg/hour every 72 hours; only for opioid-tolerant patients; dose conversion from prior opioids must follow established guidelines.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – high risk of additive respiratory depression and sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: CYP3A4 inhibitors (not in table but may include Azole antifungals, macrolides like Clarithromycin, some antiretrovirals) – can increase fentanyl levels and prolong toxicity. OUT: Serotonergic agents (SSRIs such as Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram; Mirtazapine; Tramadol) – rare but possible serotonin toxicity; monitor closely.	Use extreme caution; very high risk of respiratory depression, confusion, falls; start with lowest possible doses and monitor closely; transdermal patches generally reserved for carefully selected patients.	Transdermal use in children is restricted and specialist-only; IV dosing is weight-based and used in anesthesia/ICU with experienced staff only.	Use in pregnancy only if clearly indicated (e.g., anesthesia); may cause neonatal respiratory depression; avoid in labor unless facilities for neonatal resuscitation are available.	Overdose: life-threatening respiratory depression, pinpoint pupils, coma, chest wall rigidity; immediate airway support and high-dose naloxone with repeated administration may be required due to long fentanyl effect; monitor for recurrence.	Store securely in controlled-drug storage; protect patches from heat and dispose carefully; keep ampoules protected from light and out of reach of children.
3.9.3.4	Fentanyl	Solution for injection 50mcg/ ml/Patches 12mcg/50mcg Patches												
3.9.4	Anti-migraine medicines													
3.9.4.1	Ergotamine tartrate	Tablets 1mg	Potent ergot alkaloid used in the acute treatment of migraine attacks, with strong vasoconstrictive and adrenergic effects on cranial blood vessels. It has historic utility but is largely superseded by safer triptans in many centers. Still included in formularies where cost and clinical familiarity favor continued use.	Ergotamine is a partial agonist and antagonist at various serotonin (5-HT1B/1D), adrenergic (alpha), and dopaminergic receptors. It causes constriction of intracranial blood vessels and inhibition of trigeminal neuropeptide release, reducing neurogenic inflammation associated with migraine. It also constricts peripheral vasculature through alpha-adrenergic stimulation and smooth muscle activation, which explains ischemia and end-organ risk in overdose.	Acute migraine attacks (with or without aura) when used early in the attack; occasionally used for cluster headache under specialist supervision. It is **not for prophylaxis**.	Severe peripheral vascular disease, coronary artery disease, uncontrolled hypertension, severe hepatic or renal impairment, sepsis, pregnancy or breastfeeding, hemiplegic or basilar migraine, hypersensitivity to ergot alkaloids, and concurrent strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (table or non-table) due to risk of prolonged vasospasm. Avoid in patients with severe infection or compromised circulation due to risk of gangrene.	Nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, paresthesia, muscle cramps, weakness, transient hypertension, bradycardia or tachycardia. Serious adverse effects include vasospasm, digital ischemia, Raynaud-like symptoms, myocardial or cerebral ischemia, infarction, gangrene, and ergotism with chronic exposure (vascular insufficiency, hallucinations, convulsions).	Administer at earliest onset of migraine. Typical oral regimen: 1–2 mg initially, then 1 mg every 30 minutes as needed. **Maximum dose**: 6 mg per attack or 10 mg per week (varies by guideline). Do not exceed recommended limits due to cumulative vasoconstrictive toxicity. Use antiemetic (e.g., Metoclopramide) if nausea severe. **Not for daily continuous use**.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Beta-blockers (Propranolol, Atenolol, Carvedilol, Metoprolol, Labetalol) – ergotamine + beta-blockers increases risk of **severe peripheral vasoconstriction and digital ischemia**, because beta-blockers block compensatory vasodilation and unmask alpha-mediated vasospasm. Strictly avoid routine co-administration in migraine patients. CRITICAL-INTRA: Calcium channel blockers used for angina/hypertension (Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) – theoretically reduce vasospastic risk, but in overdose settings may mask early ischemia and allow more ergotamine exposure before symptoms manifest; monitor closely. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol and SSRIs/SNRIs (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine, Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine) – serotonergic overlap increases theoretical serotonin-syndrome risk if used repeatedly; risk is **low**, but clinicians should differentiate arterial vasospasm from serotonin toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl, Tramadol) + ergotamine – additive nausea, vomiting, respiratory depression risk, and risk of aspiration during severe migraine. OUT-OF-TABLE: Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (macrolides not in your dataset such as erythromycin high-dose or clarithromycin, azoles like itraconazole, HIV protease inhibitors, or verapamil/diltiazem externally) – **absolute contraindication** due to prolonged vasospasm, risk of gangrene and fatal ischemia; if these drugs are added to the database, tag as CRITICAL.	Use caution. Elderly have higher risk of peripheral ischemia, coronary vasospasm, confusion, reduced hepatic clearance and drug accumulation. Maximum dose should be lower, and therapy should be **exceptional rather than routine**.	Not recommended. Higher risk of vasospasm and unpredictable toxicity. If used, strict specialist supervision with vascular monitoring is mandatory.	ABSOLUTE CONTRAINDICATION – ergotamine is uterotonic and vasoconstrictive, associated with miscarriage, fetal growth restriction, uterine tetany, placental ischemia and neonatal toxicity. Avoid while breastfeeding because ergotamine suppresses lactation and can cause infant cyanosis.	Toxicity presents with severe vasospasm, limb ischemia, gangrene, abdominal ischemia, myocardial or cerebral infarction, hallucinations, seizures and hypertension. Immediate treatment: discontinue drug, hospitalize urgently, initiate IV vasodilators (nitroprusside, nitroglycerin, nifedipine), aggressive limb perfusion support, anticoagulation when appropriate, vascular surgery consult for severe digital ischemia or infarction.	Store at controlled room temperature, protected from excessive light and humidity. Avoid heat degradation. Keep tablets in blister until use to preserve stability; do not store in bathroom or high-moisture areas.

8	Medicines used in the treatment of malignant diseases: Anti-neoplastic medicines													
8.1	Actinomycin D (Dactinomycin)	Powder for injection 500mcg	Cytotoxic antibiotic used primarily in pediatric and some adult solid tumors; extremely potent with a narrow therapeutic index and strong myelosuppressive and mucosal toxicity.	Intercalates between DNA base pairs and inhibits DNA-dependent RNA synthesis; at higher concentrations it also impairs DNA synthesis, resulting in apoptosis of rapidly dividing cells.	Wilms’ tumor, rhabdomyosarcoma, Ewing sarcoma, gestational trophoblastic neoplasia, testicular tumors and other sarcomas as part of combination regimens.	Pregnancy, severe hepatic or renal impairment, existing severe myelosuppression, active serious infection without control, known hypersensitivity.	Dose-limiting myelosuppression, severe mucositis and stomatitis, nausea/vomiting, diarrhea, hepatotoxicity, alopecia, local tissue necrosis if extravasation, photosensitivity.	Dosed by body surface area as part of specific oncology protocols, typically IV bolus or short infusion; requires specialist oncology supervision and full blood count and liver tests before each cycle.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Dactinomycin + other myelosuppressive agents in this table (Cyclophosphamide, Ifosfamide, Carboplatin, Cisplatin, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Melphalan, Lomustine, Dacarbazine, Fludarabine, Hydroxyurea, Mitomycin) → profound additive bone marrow suppression with severe neutropenia and infection/bleeding risk; schedule spacing and Filgrastim support should be encoded in protocols. [MODERATE-INTRA] Dactinomycin + Hepatotoxic drugs (Methotrexate, Azathioprine, Tacrolimus, high-dose Paracetamol elsewhere in table) → increased liver injury; monitor LFTs. [OUT] Concomitant radiotherapy markedly increases local tissue necrosis and mucositis (standard external-oncology caution).	Older adults have less marrow reserve and are highly susceptible to sepsis and mucosal injury; consider lower doses and aggressive infection prophylaxis, and avoid if frailty is high.	Used extensively in pediatric oncology; dosing strictly BSA-based and protocol-driven with rigorous toxicity monitoring; high risk of severe mucositis and sepsis in children mandates center-based care.	Contraindicated or strongly discouraged due to teratogenicity; if absolutely unavoidable for life-threatening maternal cancer, fetal loss or malformation risk must be accepted and documented.	Overdose presents as profound and prolonged pancytopenia, severe mucositis, GI bleeding, and multi-organ failure; management is purely supportive with broad-spectrum antibiotics, growth factor support (Filgrastim), transfusions and intensive care.	Store refrigerated or at controlled room temperature according to manufacturer, protected from light; reconstituted solutions should be used promptly or discarded per stability guidelines and handled as cytotoxic waste.
8.2	Asparaginase	Powder for injection 1000 IU	Antineoplastic enzyme used mainly in acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL); depletes circulating asparagine, selectively affecting tumor cells dependent on exogenous asparagine.	Catalyzes hydrolysis of circulating L-asparagine to aspartic acid and ammonia, depleting asparagine needed for protein synthesis in leukemic lymphoblasts, which lack sufficient asparagine synthetase.	Acute lymphoblastic leukemia as part of multi-agent chemotherapy protocols.	History of severe hypersensitivity or pancreatitis due to asparaginase, severe hepatic dysfunction, significant coagulopathy, uncontrolled infection.	Hypersensitivity reactions (rash, anaphylaxis), pancreatitis, hepatotoxicity, thrombotic or bleeding events due to coagulation factor changes, hyperglycemia, hyperammonemia, nausea/vomiting.	Dose and schedule are protocol-specific (e.g. IV or IM doses in IU/m2), usually given intermittently with close monitoring of amylase/lipase, liver function, glucose and coagulation parameters.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Asparaginase + hepatotoxic agents (Methotrexate, Dactinomycin, Azathioprine, Tacrolimus) → increased risk of severe hepatic injury; LFTs and coagulation should be monitored intensively. [MODERATE-INTRA] Asparaginase + anticoagulants in this table (Heparin, Enoxaparin, Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) → unpredictable coagulation, bleeding or thrombosis; specialist hematology oversight mandatory. [CLASS] Combination with other myelosuppressive cytotoxics (Cyclophosphamide, Cytarabine, Doxorubicin, etc.) increases infection and bleeding risk and is deliberate but requires protocolized supportive care. [OUT] Live vaccines contraindicated during and after therapy until immune function recovers.	Geriatric ALL is uncommon; toxicity is usually higher, with more hepatotoxicity and thrombosis; often avoided or dose-reduced in older patients.	Widely used in pediatric ALL protocols; toxicities (pancreatitis, thrombosis) must be monitored closely with standardized early intervention algorithms.	Contraindicated or avoided in pregnancy due to teratogenicity and maternal coagulopathy risk; alternative regimens considered if possible.	Overdose may cause severe coagulopathy, pancreatitis, hyperammonemia and hepatic failure; management is intensive supportive care, coagulation factor replacement and treatment of pancreatitis.	Store powder refrigerated if required, protect from light, reconstitute per instructions and use within recommended time-frame; avoid vigorous shaking to preserve enzyme stability.
8.3	Azathioprine	Tablets 50mg, injection 50mg vial	Immunosuppressive antimetabolite used as a steroid-sparing agent in autoimmune disease and as part of transplant regimens; in oncology, historically used in leukemias and as adjunct but now mainly immunosuppressive.	Prodrug converted to 6-mercaptopurine; its metabolites (thioinosinic acid and others) inhibit purine synthesis, impairing DNA replication and proliferation of rapidly dividing cells, particularly lymphocytes.	Autoimmune diseases (e.g. rheumatoid arthritis, IBD), organ transplantation, sometimes as part of leukemia/lymphoma regimens or maintenance immunosuppression in oncology patients.	Previous azathioprine-induced myelotoxicity, TPMT or NUDT15 severe deficiency when known, pregnancy (relative), active serious infection, severe hepatic dysfunction, prior malignancy risk assessment needed for chronic use.	Dose-dependent myelosuppression (leukopenia, thrombocytopenia), hepatotoxicity, GI upset, rash, increased infection risk, long-term risk of secondary malignancy (lymphoma, skin cancers).	Typical doses 1–3mg/kg/day orally, adjusted to WBC and TPMT activity; injection formulation used less commonly.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Azathioprine + Allopurinol (14.2.1 Allopurinol in this table) → severe, potentially fatal myelosuppression due to inhibition of xanthine oxidase; azathioprine dose must be drastically reduced (to ~25%) or avoided; your system should hard-flag this combination. [MODERATE-INTRA] Azathioprine + other myelosuppressive agents here (Cyclophosphamide, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Melphalan, Fludarabine, Hydroxyurea, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine) → additive marrow suppression and infection risk. [MODERATE-INTRA] Azathioprine + Tacrolimus or other strong immunosuppressants → markedly increased infection and malignancy risk. [OUT] Live vaccines generally contraindicated during therapy.	Elderly patients have increased risk of bone marrow suppression, infections and skin cancers; use lower initial doses and strict dermatologic and hematologic monitoring.	Not commonly used as primary oncology agent in children now, but widely used as immunosuppressant; TPMT testing (if available) and close CBC monitoring essential.	Generally avoided in pregnancy; if used (e.g. transplant), risk–benefit must be explicitly documented with fetal monitoring.	Overdose leads to profound pancytopenia and severe infection risk; management is supportive with growth factor support (Filgrastim), transfusions, prophylactic/therapeutic antimicrobials.	Store tablets at room temperature, protect from light and moisture; handle with care as cytotoxic/immunosuppressive agent.
8.4	Bleomycin	Powder for injection 15 000-unit ampoule	Cytotoxic glycopeptide antibiotic mainly used in testicular cancer, Hodgkin lymphoma and some squamous cell carcinomas; notable for pulmonary toxicity and relatively little myelosuppression.	Binds DNA and generates free radicals, causing single- and double-strand breaks; cells in G2 phase are particularly sensitive.	Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphomas, testicular germ cell tumors, squamous cell carcinomas of head/neck, cervix and skin, malignant effusions (intrapleural).	Pre-existing significant pulmonary disease, previous severe bleomycin lung toxicity, severe renal impairment, elderly with poor lung reserve, pregnancy.	Dose-limiting pulmonary fibrosis and pneumonitis, fever, chills, mucocutaneous toxicity (ulceration, hyperpigmentation), Raynaud’s phenomenon, hypersensitivity reactions; relatively mild myelosuppression compared with other cytotoxics.	Dosed IV, IM, SC or intrapleural per protocol, cumulative dose usually limited (e.g. ≤400,000 IU) to reduce pulmonary toxicity; baseline and periodic pulmonary function tests recommended.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Bleomycin + high-concentration supplemental oxygen during anesthesia → increased risk of life-threatening pulmonary toxicity; your perioperative module should warn anesthetists. [MODERATE-INTRA] Bleomycin + other pulmonary toxic agents (Busulphan, Cyclophosphamide at high doses, Methotrexate, radiation to chest) → higher lung damage risk. [CLASS] Combination with other cytotoxics (Cisplatin, Etoposide, Doxorubicin, Vinblastine) is routine but increases infection risk; supportive care with Filgrastim beneficial. [OUT] Smoking and external environmental lung toxins amplify toxicity and should be flagged during counseling.	Older patients are at much higher risk of pulmonary fibrosis; consider alternative regimens or aggressive dose limitation and pulmonary monitoring.	Limited pediatric use in germ cell tumors and Hodgkin lymphoma; careful pulmonary monitoring and avoidance of high O2 also crucial.	Avoid in pregnancy if possible; teratogenic and fetotoxic, particularly in first trimester.	Overdose primarily causes acute inflammatory pneumonitis progressing to fibrosis, plus mucocutaneous ulceration; supportive respiratory care and corticosteroids may be used though evidence is limited.	Store dry powder refrigerated or at controlled room temperature as per manufacturer, protect from light; reconstituted solutions should be used promptly and discarded as cytotoxic waste.
