8.5	Busulphan	Tablets 500mcg	Alkylating agent used mainly in chronic myeloid leukemia (historically) and in high-dose regimens for stem cell transplantation conditioning; strongly myeloablative.	Bifunctional alkylating agent that cross-links DNA strands, inhibiting DNA replication and leading to cell death, particularly in hematopoietic cells.	Chronic myeloid leukemia (less common now with TKIs), conditioning regimens before bone marrow or stem cell transplantation.	Pregnancy, severe myelosuppression, previous busulphan-induced pulmonary fibrosis, uncontrolled infection.	Profound myelosuppression, pulmonary fibrosis (“busulphan lung”), skin hyperpigmentation, seizures at high doses, hepatic veno-occlusive disease, infertility.	Oral or IV dosing is highly protocol specific, often based on mg/kg or mg/m2 with therapeutic drug monitoring in transplant setting.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Busulphan + other alkylators (Cyclophosphamide, Melphalan, Chlorambucil, Ifosfamide, Lomustine) or antimetabolites (Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea, Fludarabine) → extreme marrow suppression and mucositis; protocols require Filgrastim support and strict infection prophylaxis. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Busulphan + Phenytoin (not in current table) used historically for seizure prophylaxis can alter clearance; any antiseizure medicine your DB adds later should be flagged as OUT. [MODERATE-INTRA] Busulphan + hepatotoxic agents (Azathioprine, Tacrolimus, Asparaginase, Methotrexate) increases risk of hepatic veno-occlusive disease.	In elderly, transplant-based high-dose regimens carry very high toxicity; use only after thorough fitness assessment.	Use in children for transplantation conditioning is common; seizure prophylaxis and PK-guided dosing are crucial.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; high teratogenic and mutagenic risk.	Overdose produces prolonged pancytopenia, severe mucositis, hepatic failure and pulmonary fibrosis; treatment is supportive, with potential consideration of stem cell rescue.	Store tablets at room temperature in tightly closed containers; label and handle as cytotoxic.
8.6	Calcium folinate	Tablets 15mg	Reduced folate used as a “rescue” after high-dose methotrexate and as a biochemical modulator to enhance 5-fluorouracil efficacy; not itself cytotoxic at normal doses.	Bypasses dihydrofolate reductase blockade by Methotrexate and replenishes reduced folate pools in normal cells; when combined with Fluorouracil, stabilizes the FdUMP–thymidylate synthase complex, increasing antitumor effect.	Rescue after high-dose Methotrexate; modulation of 5-Fluorouracil in colorectal and other cancers; treatment of folate deficiency and certain toxicities.	Pernicious anemia or other B12 deficiency anemia as sole therapy (may mask neurological progression); hypersensitivity.	Generally well tolerated; high doses can cause GI upset, insomnia, agitation or rarely allergic reactions.	Dosing and timing are protocol-specific (e.g. started 24 hours after Methotrexate and repeated until serum Methotrexate falls to safe levels; or fixed doses when given with 5-FU).	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Calcium folinate + high-dose Methotrexate (both in your table) → deliberate rescue; timing and dose must be encoded in protocols to prevent Methotrexate toxicity without compromising antitumor activity. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Calcium folinate + 5-Fluorouracil → increases 5-FU efficacy but also mucositis and diarrhea; regimen-specific dosing (e.g. “FOLFOX”) should be standardized. [OUT] Concurrent folic acid supplements can confound interpretation of folate status but usually minor.	Older adults benefit from leucovorin rescue but may still clear Methotrexate slowly; monitoring of Methotrexate levels and renal function is critical.	Safe and useful in children in the same contexts, using weight/BSA-based dosing.	Considered safe in pregnancy when used to treat folate deficiency or as part of life-saving maternal regimens.	Overdose is typically benign; may theoretically negate antifolate antitumor efficacy if severely excessive around Methotrexate dosing; no specific antidote required.	Store at room temperature, protect from light; follow manufacturer’s guidance for any injectable formulations you choose to include later.
8.7	Carboplatin	Solution for injection 10mg/ml	Second-generation platinum compound with broad antitumor activity; less nephrotoxic and emetogenic than Cisplatin but more myelosuppressive.	Forms intra- and inter-strand DNA cross-links, inhibiting DNA replication and transcription, leading to apoptosis of rapidly dividing cells.	Ovarian cancer, lung cancer, head and neck cancers, testicular tumors and others, often in combination regimens.	Severe bone marrow suppression, significant bleeding, severe renal or hepatic impairment without adjustment, prior severe hypersensitivity to platinum agents.	Myelosuppression (particularly thrombocytopenia), nausea/vomiting, mild nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, peripheral neuropathy, hypersensitivity reactions, electrolyte abnormalities.	Dosed using Calvert formula based on target AUC and glomerular filtration rate; given IV in cycles every 3–4 weeks.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Carboplatin + other myelosuppressives (Cyclophosphamide, Paclitaxel, Etoposide, Doxorubicin, Vinblastine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, etc.) → profound cytopenias; combination is common but requires Filgrastim and transfusion support. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Carboplatin + other nephrotoxic agents in this table (Cisplatin, Amphotericin B, high-dose Methotrexate, Tacrolimus, Vancomycin, Gentamicin, Amikacin) → increased risk of acute kidney injury; renal function and electrolytes must be closely monitored. [MODERATE-INTRA] Carboplatin + ototoxic agents (e.g. Aminoglycosides Gentamicin/Amikacin) → hearing loss risk. [OUT] Concurrent live vaccines contraindicated due to immunosuppression.	Elderly frequently have reduced renal function; AUC-based dosing must be carefully calculated and toxicity monitored.	Used in pediatric oncology (e.g. brain tumors, neuroblastoma) with similar toxicity; careful dosing using measured GFR preferred.	Teratogenic; avoid in pregnancy unless maternal life-threatening indication exists and multidisciplinary team agrees.	Overdose results in severe, prolonged myelosuppression, renal failure, ototoxicity and potential fatal sepsis; treatment is supportive with intensive care and blood product support.	Store at controlled room temperature, protect from light; discard any unused solution as cytotoxic waste per protocol.
8.8	Carmustine	Powder for injection 100mg vial	Nitrosourea alkylating agent with high lipid solubility, used particularly for brain tumors and as part of conditioning regimens; notable for delayed myelosuppression and pulmonary toxicity.	Alkylates DNA and carbamoylates proteins, inhibiting DNA and RNA synthesis; crosses the blood–brain barrier effectively.	Primary brain tumors (e.g. gliomas), Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphomas, multiple myeloma, high-dose conditioning regimens.	Severe bone marrow suppression, pulmonary fibrosis, hepatic or renal impairment, prior nitrosourea-induced organ toxicity.	Delayed and prolonged myelosuppression (often peaking at 4–6 weeks), pulmonary fibrosis, hepatic and renal dysfunction, nausea/vomiting, facial flushing, phlebitis, secondary malignancies.	IV dosing every 6–8 weeks or as part of transplant-conditioning protocols; maximum cumulative doses limited to reduce pulmonary toxicity.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Carmustine + other pulmonary toxic agents (Bleomycin, Busulphan, high-dose Cyclophosphamide, Methotrexate) → increased risk of interstitial lung disease; baseline and periodic lung function testing strongly advised. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Carmustine + other delayed myelosuppressive drugs (Busulphan, Lomustine, Melphalan) → severe prolonged pancytopenia; your scheduling logic must account for delayed nadirs. [OUT] Combination with external radiotherapy to chest/brain increases lung and marrow toxicity.	Elderly have higher risk of pulmonary and marrow toxicity; careful risk–benefit and dose adjustments are required.	Used cautiously in pediatric brain tumors and Hodgkin lymphoma; long-term risk of secondary malignancies and lung fibrosis must be discussed.	Avoid in pregnancy; strongly teratogenic and mutagenic.	Overdose leads to profound, prolonged marrow aplasia, severe organ toxicity (lung, liver, kidney) and high mortality; only supportive treatment and stem cell support (if available) are possible.	Store vials refrigerated if required, protect from light; reconstituted solutions are unstable and must be used promptly with cytotoxic handling precautions.
8.9	Chlorambucil	Tablets 2mg	Oral alkylating agent used in chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) and indolent lymphomas; relatively slow onset and predictable myelosuppression.	Forms DNA cross-links, inhibiting DNA replication and transcription, leading to apoptosis in lymphoid cells.	Chronic lymphocytic leukemia, low-grade non-Hodgkin lymphomas, sometimes as palliative therapy.	Severe bone marrow suppression, pregnancy, hypersensitivity, history of chlorambucil-induced seizures (rare), severe infection.	Myelosuppression (leukopenia, thrombocytopenia), GI upset, rash, rare seizures, long-term risk of secondary malignancy and infertility.	Oral dosing typically 0.1–0.2mg/kg/day for several days per cycle or continuous low dosing depending on protocol.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Chlorambucil + other myelosuppressive drugs (Cyclophosphamide, Busulphan, Fludarabine, Azathioprine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea) → additive marrow suppression; avoid overlapping cycles unless strictly protocolized. [OUT] Live vaccines are contraindicated during treatment due to immunosuppression.	Elderly CLL patients often receive chlorambucil; careful monitoring for cytopenias and secondary malignancies is required, with dose reduction as needed.	Limited pediatric use; long-term infertility and leukemogenic potential must be considered.	Contraindicated in pregnancy unless no alternative; significant risk of fetal toxicity.	Overdose results in severe myelosuppression, mucositis and sepsis; only supportive care and transfusions available.	Store tablets in a refrigerator or as per manufacturer, protected from moisture; handle and dispense with cytotoxic precautions.
8.1	Cisplatin	Solution for injection 1mg/ml	First-generation platinum compound with broad-spectrum antitumor activity; highly emetogenic and nephrotoxic, used in many curative regimens.	Forms inter- and intra-strand DNA cross-links and DNA–protein cross-links, distorting the DNA helix and triggering apoptosis.	Testicular cancer, ovarian cancer, lung cancers, head and neck cancers, bladder cancer and many other solid tumors.	Pre-existing significant renal impairment, hearing impairment, severe myelosuppression, pregnancy, prior severe hypersensitivity to platinum, uncontrolled dehydration.	Severe nausea and vomiting, nephrotoxicity (tubular damage, electrolyte wasting), ototoxicity (high-frequency hearing loss), peripheral neuropathy, myelosuppression, electrolyte disturbances (hypomagnesemia, hypokalemia), hypersensitivity reactions.	IV infusion with aggressive pre- and post-hydration and antiemetics; dosing is mg/m2 and schedule strongly protocolized; cumulative dose limited due to neuropathy and ototoxicity.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Cisplatin + other nephrotoxic drugs in this table (Carboplatin, Amphotericin B, Vancomycin, Gentamicin, Amikacin, Tacrolimus, high-dose Methotrexate) → significantly increased risk of acute kidney injury and chronic renal damage; renal monitoring and avoidance of unnecessary nephrotoxins is mandatory. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Cisplatin + loop/thiazide diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorothiazide, Bendrofluazide, Amiloride/HCTZ) used for forced diuresis can aggravate electrolyte loss; continuous electrolyte replacement protocols needed. [MODERATE-INTRA] Cisplatin + ototoxic Aminoglycosides (Gentamicin, Amikacin) → additive hearing loss risk. [CLASS] Cisplatin + all other myelosuppressive cytotoxics increases cytopenias and infection risk; Filgrastim support recommended in intensive regimens.	Elderly frequently have reduced renal reserve and more baseline hearing loss; cisplatin may be substituted with Carboplatin in some regimens to reduce toxicity, and dosing must be conservative with close monitoring.	Used in pediatric oncology (e.g. neuroblastoma, osteosarcoma, hepatoblastoma); particular attention is required to protect hearing and kidney function with long-term follow-up for ototoxicity.	Highly teratogenic; avoid in pregnancy except for life-threatening indication after multidisciplinary discussion; timing in gestation significantly affects fetal risk.	Overdose or accidental rapid infusion can cause catastrophic nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, neurotoxicity and prolonged myelosuppression; management includes aggressive hydration, electrolyte management, renal replacement therapy and ICU support.	Store vials at recommended temperature (often room temperature, protected from light); once diluted, use promptly and discard unused portions as cytotoxic waste.
8.11	Cyclophosphamide	ablets 50mg, Powder for injection 100mg	Widely used alkylating agent for many cancers and autoimmune conditions; prodrug activated in the liver with key toxicities of myelosuppression and hemorrhagic cystitis.	Hepatic activation yields phosphoramide mustard, which alkylates DNA and cross-links strands, inhibiting replication; acrolein metabolite is responsible for urothelial toxicity.	Lymphomas, leukemias, breast cancer, ovarian cancer, sarcomas, conditioning for transplantation, and numerous autoimmune diseases.	Severe bone marrow suppression, urinary tract obstruction, active urinary tract infection, severe hepatic impairment, pregnancy, previous serious hemorrhagic cystitis from the drug.	Dose-related myelosuppression, nausea/vomiting, hemorrhagic cystitis, alopecia, SIADH, secondary malignancy risk, infertility; high doses can cause cardiotoxicity and pulmonary toxicity.	Oral or IV dosing is protocol-specific; high-dose regimens require Mesna uroprotection and vigorous hydration; regular urinalysis is recommended.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Cyclophosphamide + other myelosuppressive agents (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Doxorubicin, Daunorubicin, Etoposide, Busulphan, Melphalan, Lomustine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Fludarabine, Hydroxyurea, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Procarbazine, Vinblastine, Vincristine, Paclitaxel) → severe pancytopenia; protocolized Filgrastim and prophylactic antibiotics are essential. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Cyclophosphamide + nephrotoxins (Cisplatin, Amphotericin B, Tacrolimus, Gentamicin, Amikacin, Vancomycin) → increased renal injury risk. [MODERATE-INTRA] Cyclophosphamide + other bladder irritants (e.g. high-dose Ifosfamide) → cumulative hemorrhagic cystitis risk; Mesna scheduling should be standardized. [OUT] Concomitant live vaccines contraindicated.	Elderly at higher risk of cardiotoxicity, myelosuppression and secondary malignancies; lower doses and careful monitoring are required.	Extensively used in pediatric leukemias and solid tumors; aggressive hydration and Mesna (in high-dose regimens) are mandatory.	Teratogenic and fetotoxic; generally avoided in pregnancy except in life-threatening maternal disease, usually after first trimester if absolutely necessary.	Overdose results in profound marrow aplasia, severe hemorrhagic cystitis, cardiotoxicity and multi-organ failure; management is supportive with possible stem cell support if available.	Store tablets at room temperature in original blister; vials stored according to manufacturer’s instructions and handled with cytotoxic precautions.
8.12	Irinotecan	Solution for injection 20mg/ml	Topoisomerase I inhibitor used mainly in colorectal and other GI cancers; characterized by acute and delayed diarrhea and myelosuppression.	Prodrug converted to active metabolite SN-38, which inhibits topoisomerase I, preventing religation of single-strand DNA breaks during replication, leading to double-strand breaks and cell death.	Metastatic colorectal cancer (often in FOLFIRI regimens), small cell lung cancer and other tumors.	Severe bone marrow suppression, chronic inflammatory bowel disease, baseline severe diarrhea, known UGT1A1*28 homozygosity (high toxicity risk), pregnancy.	Dose-limiting diarrhea (early cholinergic and late secretory forms), neutropenia, nausea/vomiting, alopecia, abdominal pain, cholinergic syndrome (flushing, diaphoresis, cramping) during infusion.	IV infusion dosed by BSA, usually every 2–3 weeks; prophylactic Atropine may be given for acute cholinergic symptoms; loperamide used for delayed diarrhea under strict protocols.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Irinotecan + other myelosuppressive agents (5-Fluorouracil, Oxaliplatin if added later, Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, Etoposide) → increased neutropenia and infection risk; Filgrastim support may be needed. [MODERATE-INTRA] Irinotecan + antidiarrheals in this table (Loperamide, Codeine) is deliberate in delayed diarrhea management but must not mask severe colitis; your system should show red-flag symptoms (fever, abdominal pain) for urgent evaluation. [OUT] Strong CYP3A4 modulators and UGT1A1 interactions (e.g. some azoles, macrolides) will be encoded as OUT when full list is expanded.	Elderly have higher risk of neutropenia, diarrhea and dehydration; dose reduction and close monitoring are recommended.	Not routinely used in children outside specialist protocols; toxicity profile demands experienced pediatric oncology oversight.	Avoid in pregnancy due to teratogenicity and risk of severe maternal diarrhea and myelosuppression.	Overdose causes severe, prolonged neutropenia and intractable diarrhea with risk of sepsis, renal failure and death; management is aggressive fluid/electrolyte replacement, Filgrastim, broad-spectrum antibiotics and ICU care.	Store vials at 2–8°C or as per manufacturer, protected from light; dilute immediately before use and discard unused portions as cytotoxic waste.
8.13	Cytarabine (Cytosine arabinoside)	Powder for injection 100mg, 500mg, 1g vial	Key antimetabolite for acute leukemias; at high dose causes significant CNS and ocular toxicity in addition to myelosuppression.	Pyrimidine analog that is phosphorylated to Ara-CTP, which competes with deoxycytidine triphosphate for incorporation into DNA, leading to chain termination and inhibition of DNA polymerase.	Acute myeloid leukemia (AML), acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL), lymphomas (e.g. high-dose regimens), intrathecal CNS prophylaxis or treatment.	Existing severe bone marrow suppression not due to leukemia, active serious infection without control, severe hepatic impairment, pregnancy unless life-saving.	Myelosuppression, mucositis, nausea/vomiting, high-dose cerebellar toxicity (ataxia, dysarthria), conjunctivitis/keratitis (high-dose), hepatic dysfunction, rash, “cytarabine syndrome” (fever, myalgia, chest pain).	Dosed IV or SC in low, intermediate or high-dose regimens by BSA; intrathecal dosing used for CNS prophylaxis; requires strict protocol adherence and monitoring of neurologic status, eyes and CBC.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Cytarabine + other strong myelosuppressors (Cyclophosphamide, Ifosfamide, Busulphan, Melphalan, Fludarabine, Daunorubicin, Doxorubicin, Etoposide, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea) → severe pancytopenia with sepsis and bleeding risk; Filgrastim support and sterile precautions mandatory. [MODERATE-INTRA] High-dose Cytarabine + other neurotoxic agents (Vincristine, Ifosfamide, high-dose Methotrexate) → risk of additive neurotoxicity. [OUT] Concomitant intrathecal Methotrexate or other CNS-directed therapies must follow strict spacing rules to avoid neurotoxicity.	Older adults have increased risk of cerebellar toxicity and prolonged myelosuppression; consider dose reductions and pre-treatment neurologic assessment.	Pediatric AML/ALL protocols commonly use Cytarabine; meticulous dosing and supportive care have large impact on outcomes.	Teratogenic; generally avoided in pregnancy except when maternal leukemia treatment cannot be delayed.	Overdose particularly via intrathecal route or high-dose schedules causes severe neurotoxicity, persistent pancytopenia and sepsis; management is supportive, with no specific antidote.	Store vials at recommended temperature, protect from light; prepared solutions have limited stability and must be handled as cytotoxic.
8.14	Cyproteron acetate	Tablets 50mg, 100mg	Synthetic steroidal anti-androgen and progestin used in prostate cancer and certain androgen-dependent conditions.	Competes with dihydrotestosterone at androgen receptors and strongly suppresses gonadotropin release, lowering testosterone levels; also has progestogenic activity.	Advanced prostate cancer, palliative treatment of hypersexuality, anti-androgen therapy in other androgen-driven disorders.	Severe hepatic disease, history of liver tumors, thromboembolic disease, uncontrolled diabetes with vascular complications, meningioma history, pregnancy and breastfeeding in females.	Hepatotoxicity, decreased libido, gynecomastia, weight gain, fatigue, hot flushes, mood changes, thromboembolism risk, meningioma with long-term high-dose use.	Dose regimens vary; in prostate cancer often 50–100mg 2–3 times daily or as part of combined androgen blockade; long-term use requires periodic LFTs and monitoring for thrombotic events.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Cyproteron acetate + anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Enoxaparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) → complex effects on clotting and bleeding; monitor INR and clinical bleeding/clotting signs. [MODERATE-INTRA] Cyproteron acetate + other hepatotoxic agents (Methotrexate, Azathioprine, Asparaginase, Tacrolimus, Dactinomycin, Busulphan) → increased liver injury risk. [CLASS] Anti-androgen effect overlaps with Stilboestrol and GNRH analogs (if added later) requiring endocrine monitoring. [OUT] Concomitant use with external hormonal contraceptives/estrogens increases thrombotic risk.	Geriatric male prostate cancer patients require careful thromboembolic risk assessment and liver monitoring; falls in libido and energy can increase frailty.	Not used in children for oncology indications except rarely under specialist endocrine/oncology guidance.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; risk of feminization of male fetus and other hormone-related malformations.	Overdose may cause nausea, vomiting, dizziness, and can accentuate hepatotoxicity and coagulation abnormalities; management is supportive with monitoring of liver function and coagulation.	Store at room temperature, protect from light and moisture; treat as a hormone with special handling for staff of childbearing potential.
8.15	Dacarbazine	Powder for injection 200mg vial	Alkylating-like agent used primarily in Hodgkin lymphoma and melanoma; requires hepatic activation and is markedly emetogenic.	Converted in the liver to active methylating metabolites that damage DNA by methylation at the O6 position of guanine, causing mispairing and strand breaks.	Hodgkin lymphoma (e.g. ABVD regimen), metastatic melanoma, sarcomas.	Severe bone marrow suppression, hepatic or renal impairment, pregnancy, severe infection.	Myelosuppression, severe nausea and vomiting, hepatotoxicity (including veno-occlusive disease), flu-like symptoms, photosensitivity, local tissue damage with extravasation.	IV infusion given by BSA-based dose as part of combination regimens, usually every 2–4 weeks with intensive antiemetic prophylaxis and monitoring of blood counts and liver function.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Dacarbazine + other myelosuppressants (Bleomycin, Doxorubicin, Cyclophosphamide, Cisplatin, Etoposide, Busulphan, Melphalan, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Fludarabine, Hydroxyurea, Procarbazine, Vinblastine) → enhanced risk of severe cytopenias and sepsis; Filgrastim may be needed. [MODERATE-INTRA] Dacarbazine + hepatotoxins (Azathioprine, Tacrolimus, Asparaginase, Methotrexate, Busulphan) → increased liver injury risk. [OUT] Concomitant alcohol exacerbates hepatotoxicity.	Elderly more vulnerable to myelosuppression, nausea, and hepatic complications; lower starting doses and aggressive supportive care may be required.	Less frequently used in pediatric regimens, but where used, antiemetic and hepatic monitoring support is essential.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; teratogenic potential high with alkylators.	Overdose causes profound marrow aplasia, intractable vomiting, hepatotoxicity and potentially fatal sepsis; only supportive measures including blood product support and antiemetics are available.	Store powder refrigerated or at room temperature per manufacturer, protect from light; reconstituted solution is photosensitive and must be used promptly or protected from light until infusion.
8.16	Daunorubicin	Powder for injection 20mg vial	Anthracycline antibiotic used mainly in acute leukemias; strongly myelosuppressive and cardiotoxic with lifetime cumulative dose limits.	Intercalates into DNA, inhibits topoisomerase II and generates free radicals, leading to DNA strand breaks and apoptosis; cardiotoxicity stems from free-radical damage in cardiac myocytes.	Induction therapy for acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL), sometimes other hematologic malignancies.	Severe cardiac dysfunction (low LVEF, prior anthracycline-induced cardiomyopathy), prior maximal cumulative anthracycline dose, severe myelosuppression, active serious infection, pregnancy.	Dose-limiting myelosuppression, cardiotoxicity (acute arrhythmias, chronic cardiomyopathy), mucositis, alopecia, nausea/vomiting, red discoloration of urine, severe local tissue necrosis with extravasation.	IV infusion given via central line whenever possible, BSA-based dosing typically combined with Cytarabine or other agents; cumulative dose tracked to limit cardiotoxicity.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Daunorubicin + other anthracyclines (Doxorubicin, Liposomal Doxorubicin) → cumulative cardiotoxicity; your system must track total lifetime anthracycline dose. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Daunorubicin + other myelosuppressants (Cytarabine, Cyclophosphamide, Busulphan, Melphalan, Fludarabine, Methotrexate, Hydroxyurea, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine) → severe pancytopenia and infection risk. [OUT] Concomitant external trastuzumab or mediastinal radiotherapy significantly increase cardiotoxicity and should be flagged when added.	Older adults with baseline cardiac compromise are particularly vulnerable; pre- and post-treatment echocardiography is crucial; consider alternative regimens or liposomal formulations where available.	Used in pediatric leukemia protocols; strict lifetime dose limits and long-term cardiac follow-up are mandatory.	Avoid in pregnancy; anthracyclines are teratogenic, especially in first trimester; if unavoidable, second/third trimester use requires fetal monitoring and maternal cardiac surveillance.	Overdose leads to severe myelosuppression, mucositis, acute cardiac damage and sepsis; ICU support with growth factors, transfusions and cardiac management is required.	Store vials refrigerated or at room temperature depending on formulation, protect from light; reconstituted solution is stable only for limited time and must be handled as a vesicant.
8.17	Doxorubicin	Powder for injection 10mg,50mg	Prototype anthracycline used in many curative regimens (e.g. breast cancer, lymphomas); highly effective but with significant cardiotoxicity and myelosuppression.	Intercalates into DNA, inhibits topoisomerase II and forms free radicals, causing DNA breaks and apoptosis; cardiotoxicity arises from oxidative injury to cardiac myocytes.	Breast cancer, lymphomas (Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin), sarcomas, ovarian cancer and many other tumors as part of combination chemotherapy.	Severe cardiac disease (low LVEF, recent MI), prior high cumulative anthracycline exposure, severe myelosuppression, active serious infection, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression, mucositis, alopecia, severe nausea/vomiting, cardiomyopathy (dose-related), arrhythmias, secondary leukemia, red discoloration of urine, extravasation necrosis.	IV infusion through central venous access, BSA-based dosing with strict lifetime cumulative dose limit (often ~450–550mg/m2 depending on risk); routine cardiac imaging before and during therapy.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Doxorubicin + other anthracyclines (Daunorubicin, Liposomal Doxorubicin) or high-dose Cyclophosphamide and external trastuzumab/mediastinal radiotherapy → greatly increased cardiomyopathy risk; your DB should aggregate anthracycline doses and generate alerts. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Doxorubicin + strong myelosuppressants (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Cytarabine, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine, Methotrexate, Fludarabine, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea, Busulphan) → severe neutropenia and infection. [MODERATE-INTRA] Doxorubicin + other vesicants (Vincristine, Vinblastine) → extravasation risk; central line recommended. [OUT] Concomitant use with external cardiotoxins should be flagged when added.	Older adults experience more cardiac and marrow toxicity; aggressive cardiac and hematologic monitoring and lower dosing may be necessary.	Widely used in pediatric oncology; strict lifetime dose limits and long-term cardiac surveillance (including into adulthood) are standard.	Avoid in pregnancy when possible; if use is unavoidable for curative intent, second/third trimester regimens may be considered with close fetal and maternal monitoring.	Overdose causes acute cardiac failure, severe pancytopenia and mucositis; no antidote exists, management is intensive cardiac and supportive care; dexrazoxane (if available) may mitigate cardiotoxicity at high-risk doses.	Store vials at room temperature or refrigerated as labeled, protected from light; prepared solutions must be handled as vesicants and discarded appropriately.
8.18	Liposomal Doxorubin	20mg/ml	Pegylated liposomal formulation of Doxorubicin designed to alter pharmacokinetics, reduce peak cardiac exposure and change toxicity profile (more hand–foot syndrome, less acute cardiotoxicity).	Encapsulation of Doxorubicin in liposomes prolongs circulation time and alters tissue distribution, allowing preferential tumor accumulation via enhanced permeability and retention while reducing free-drug peaks.	Ovarian cancer, AIDS-related Kaposi’s sarcoma, metastatic breast cancer and other tumors where anthracyclines are indicated and reduced cardiotoxicity is desired.	Severe cardiac dysfunction, prior high-dose anthracycline therapy nearing maximum lifetime dose, pregnancy, severe myelosuppression.	Myelosuppression, palmar–plantar erythrodysesthesia (hand–foot syndrome), mucositis, alopecia, mild-to-moderate cardiotoxicity (still cumulative), infusion reactions, rash.	Dose and schedule vary by indication (e.g. every 4 weeks IV); administered as prolonged infusion to reduce acute reactions; cumulative anthracycline dose still tracked.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Liposomal Doxorubicin + conventional Doxorubicin or Daunorubicin → total anthracycline burden still contributes to cardiomyopathy; your system should aggregate all anthracycline doses regardless of formulation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Liposomal Doxorubicin + other myelosuppressive drugs (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Paclitaxel, Etoposide, Methotrexate, Cytarabine, etc.) → increased cytopenias and infection risk. [OUT] Concomitant external cardiotoxic therapies must be flagged when added.	Elderly may particularly benefit from reduced peak cardiotoxicity but remain vulnerable to cumulative cardiac damage and hand–foot syndrome; frequent cardiac assessment and skin checks needed.	Used with caution in adolescents and young adults where lifetime cardiac risk is a major concern; pediatric use is less established.	Avoid in pregnancy; anthracycline teratogenic risk remains.	Overdose presents with features similar to conventional Doxorubicin (severe myelosuppression, mucositis, cardiac toxicity); management is supportive.	Store in refrigerator or as specified by manufacturer, protect from light; do not freeze and discard if precipitated or after recommended in-use period.
8.19	Etoposide	Solution for IV infusion 20mg/ml	Topoisomerase II inhibitor used widely in lung cancer, germ cell tumors, lymphomas and leukemias; causes dose-limiting myelosuppression and mucositis.	Stabilizes the transient DNA double-strand break complex formed by topoisomerase II, preventing religation and causing accumulation of DNA breaks, leading to apoptosis.	Small cell lung cancer, testicular cancer, lymphomas, leukemias, other solid tumors as part of combination regimens.	Severe myelosuppression, pregnancy, severe hepatic impairment without dose adjustment, uncontrolled infection.	Myelosuppression (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia), alopecia, nausea/vomiting, mucositis, hypotension with rapid infusion, rare secondary leukemia.	IV infusion or oral dosing (oral bioavailability variable) based on BSA, usually given over days 1–3 or 1–5 in cycles; dose adjusted for renal and hepatic function.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Etoposide + Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, Daunorubicin, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Busulphan, Melphalan, Fludarabine, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine → profound myelosuppression with high febrile neutropenia risk; Filgrastim strongly recommended in intensive regimens. [MODERATE-INTRA] Etoposide + anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Enoxaparin, DOACs) increases bleeding risk via thrombocytopenia; monitoring required. [OUT] CYP3A4 modulators from outside the list may alter etoposide levels and should be flagged when added.	Elderly patients require dose reductions and careful monitoring of counts and renal function.	Used in pediatric oncology protocols (e.g. neuroblastoma, brain tumors, leukemias); myelosuppression and secondary leukemia risk must be discussed with families.	Avoid in pregnancy; teratogenic and fetotoxic.	Overdose results in profound prolonged myelosuppression, mucositis, possible hepatic and renal impairment; management is supportive with prolonged hospitalization.	Store at room temperature, protect from light; diluted solutions stable for limited periods and must be handled as cytotoxic.
8.2	Fludarabine	Tablets 10mg, Solution for injection, infusion 40mg/m2	Purine analog used primarily in chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) and indolent lymphomas; profoundly immunosuppressive, particularly affecting T-cells.	Phosphorylated to active triphosphate form, which inhibits DNA polymerase, ribonucleotide reductase and DNA primase, leading to inhibition of DNA synthesis and apoptosis in lymphocytes.	Chronic lymphocytic leukemia, low-grade non-Hodgkin lymphoma, conditioning regimens (e.g. for stem cell transplantation).	Existing severe immunodeficiency, active severe infection, decompensated renal failure, pregnancy, recent live vaccination.	Severe myelosuppression, profound T-cell suppression with opportunistic infections (Pneumocystis, CMV), autoimmune hemolytic anemia, neurotoxicity at high doses, nausea/vomiting, edema.	IV or oral dosing by BSA, typically in 5-day cycles; requires prophylaxis for opportunistic infections and regular CBC and renal monitoring.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Fludarabine + other myelo- and lymphotoxic agents (Cyclophosphamide, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Melphalan, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea, Etoposide, Tacrolimus, Azathioprine) → severe, prolonged pancytopenia and immunodeficiency; your system should recommend Pneumocystis and antiviral prophylaxis where appropriate. [OUT] Live vaccines absolutely contraindicated during and after therapy until immune reconstitution; encode as OUT-critical.	Older adults with CLL often receive Fludarabine; frailty and infection risk must be rigorously assessed with aggressive prophylaxis.	Pediatric use is limited mainly to transplant conditioning or refractory leukemias under specialist care; extreme immunosuppression requires high-level surveillance.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; strong teratogen with long-lasting immunologic impact on the fetus.	Overdose leads to irreversible central neurotoxicity (blindness, coma), severe marrow failure and fatal infections; no antidote exists and prognosis is poor despite intensive care.	Store tablets at room temperature; injection/infusion solutions stored as per manufacturer and handled as high-risk cytotoxic.
8.21	Filgrastim	Solution for injection 300mcg/ml	Recombinant granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) used to prevent and treat chemotherapy-induced neutropenia and to mobilize stem cells; not itself cytotoxic.	Binds G-CSF receptors on hematopoietic progenitors, stimulating proliferation and differentiation into neutrophils and enhancing neutrophil function.	Prevention and treatment of neutropenia due to cytotoxic chemotherapy, bone marrow transplantation, severe chronic neutropenia; mobilization of peripheral blood stem cells.	Hypersensitivity to E. coli–derived proteins or Filgrastim; caution in myeloid malignancies where stimulation of malignant clones is possible.	Bone pain, leukocytosis, splenomegaly and rare splenic rupture, mild injection site reactions, transient LFT abnormalities; very rare ARDS.	Typically 5mcg/kg/day SC starting 24–72 hours after chemotherapy until neutrophil recovery; dosing and duration vary with regimen and indication.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Filgrastim + all myelosuppressive chemotherapy in this table (practically every cytotoxic listed) is deliberate and beneficial, reducing neutropenia duration; your system should suggest Filgrastim automatically when regimens exceed defined FN risk thresholds. [OUT] G-CSF in patients with myelodysplastic syndromes or leukemia may theoretically increase blast counts; flagged as OUT-specialist decision.	Elderly benefit significantly from FN risk reduction but may be more prone to bone pain and splenic enlargement; consider imaging if LUQ pain occurs.	Used widely in pediatric oncology; dosing is weight-based with similar side-effect profile; essential in intensive regimens.	Considered relatively safe in pregnancy when maternal benefit is compelling, but data are limited; use under specialist hematology/oncology advice only.	Overdose is rare; may cause extreme leukocytosis and splenomegaly; generally reversible after dose cessation.	Store refrigerated at 2–8°C, do not freeze; protect from light and avoid vigorous shaking; allow to reach room temperature before injection.
8.22	Fluorouracil	Solution for injection 25mg/ml	Pyrimidine analog antimetabolite central to many solid tumor regimens (e.g. colorectal, breast, head and neck); toxicity profile dominated by myelosuppression, mucositis and diarrhea.	Converted intracellularly to FdUMP, which inhibits thymidylate synthase and blocks DNA synthesis, and to FUTP, which is incorporated into RNA, disrupting RNA processing and function.	Colorectal cancer, breast cancer, gastric and pancreatic cancers, head and neck cancers, other GI malignancies in combination regimens.	Severe myelosuppression, DPD (dihydropyrimidine dehydrogenase) deficiency, severe malnutrition, serious infection, poor performance status, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression, mucositis, diarrhea, hand–foot syndrome (especially with continuous infusion), photosensitivity, cardiotoxicity (coronary vasospasm, ischemia), hyperpigmentation.	IV bolus or continuous infusion as BSA-based dosing, often modulated with Calcium folinate; dose and schedule strictly protocol-specific; DPD testing recommended where available.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] 5-FU + Calcium folinate (both in this table) → deliberate biochemical modulation that increases efficacy but also mucositis and diarrhea; regimens must standardize doses and toxicity monitoring. [CRITICAL-INTRA] 5-FU + other myelosuppressives (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Methotrexate, Cytarabine, Irinotecan) → severe neutropenia and risk of sepsis; Filgrastim support advisable in high-risk protocols. [MODERATE-INTRA] 5-FU + cardiotoxic agents (Anthracyclines, high-dose Cyclophosphamide) → increased cardiac ischemia risk; baseline CV assessment recommended. [OUT] Concomitant warfarin (10.1.3) significantly increases INR; tag as OUT–critical monitoring.	Elderly have higher risk of diarrhea, mucositis, cardiotoxicity and myelosuppression; dose reductions and careful monitoring are routine.	Used in pediatric oncology less frequently but may appear in selected sarcoma or GI protocols; toxicity surveillance particularly important.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; strong teratogen, especially in first trimester.	Overdose leads to severe mucositis, intractable diarrhea, profound myelosuppression and multiorgan failure; uridine triacetate (if available) is an antidote when given early; otherwise treatment is supportive.	Store vials at room temperature or refrigerated per manufacturer, protect from light; diluted solutions are stable for limited periods and must be handled as cytotoxic.
8.23	Hydroxyurea	Capsules 500mg	Oral antimetabolite with cytoreductive activity; used in myeloproliferative neoplasms, sickle cell disease and as adjunct in some leukemias; radiation-sensitizing agent.	Inhibits ribonucleotide reductase, decreasing deoxyribonucleotide pools needed for DNA synthesis, causing S-phase arrest and apoptosis.	Chronic myeloid leukemia (historically), essential thrombocythemia, polycythemia vera, sickle cell disease (to increase HbF), adjunct in some leukemias or head and neck cancers.	Severe bone marrow suppression, pregnancy, severe anemia without transfusion support, hypersensitivity.	Myelosuppression, GI upset, mucocutaneous ulcers, skin and nail changes, hyperpigmentation, rare leg ulcers and secondary malignancy with long-term use.	Dosed orally, adjusted to blood counts and indication (e.g. 15–30mg/kg/day in myeloproliferative disease); regular CBC monitoring essential.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Hydroxyurea + other myelosuppressives (Cyclophosphamide, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Fludarabine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine) → additive marrow suppression; avoid overlapping unless protocolized. [OUT] Concurrent radiotherapy increases mucocutaneous toxicity and should be managed as OUT–sensitizer interaction.	Elderly are at higher risk of leg ulcers, cytopenias and secondary malignancies; dosing should be conservative with frequent CBCs.	Used in children (e.g. sickle cell disease) with close monitoring; concerns about long-term mutagenicity must be balanced against benefits.	Teratogenic; avoid in pregnancy and in those planning conception; effective contraception required.	Overdose causes severe cytopenias and mucocutaneous toxicity; manage with drug cessation, transfusion support and infection prophylaxis.	Store capsules at room temperature in a tightly closed container; handle as a cytotoxic drug.
8.24	Ifosfamide	Powder for injection 1g, 2g vial	Alkylating agent related to Cyclophosphamide, used in sarcomas and germ cell tumors; notable for hemorrhagic cystitis and neurotoxicity.	Requires hepatic activation to form cytotoxic alkylating metabolites that cross-link DNA; chloroacetaldehyde metabolite is implicated in neurotoxicity.	Soft tissue sarcomas, osteosarcoma, germ cell tumors, lymphomas and other cancers as part of combination regimens.	Severe bone marrow suppression, urinary outflow obstruction, untreated urinary tract infection, prior severe Ifosfamide neurotoxicity, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression, hemorrhagic cystitis, nephrotoxicity (especially proximal tubular dysfunction), neurotoxicity (confusion, encephalopathy), nausea/vomiting, alopecia, SIADH.	Given IV in high doses by BSA, usually with mandatory Mesna uroprotection and vigorous hydration; dose adjustments required for renal dysfunction.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Ifosfamide + other urotoxic or nephrotoxic agents (Cyclophosphamide, Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Amphotericin B, Tacrolimus, Aminoglycosides, Vancomycin) → increase risk of cystitis and kidney injury; Mesna and renal monitoring mandatory. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Ifosfamide + CNS depressants (benzodiazepines, opioids) may mask or worsen encephalopathy; your system should flag neurotoxicity risk and demand documentation of neurologic status. [MODERATE-INTRA] Ifosfamide + other myelosuppressants (Doxorubicin, Etoposide, Vinblastine, Vincristine, Paclitaxel, Cytarabine, Methotrexate) → severe cytopenias and infection risk; Filgrastim often required.	Elderly at heightened risk of encephalopathy and renal dysfunction; consider alternative regimens or lower doses with aggressive monitoring.	Used in pediatric sarcoma and germ cell protocols; neurotoxicity and nephrotoxicity must be specifically monitored with early intervention algorithms.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; significant teratogenic and fetotoxic risks.	Overdose causes severe encephalopathy, refractory hemorrhagic cystitis, renal failure and marrow aplasia; management includes cessation, Mesna, dialysis if needed and intensive supportive care.	Store vials at room temperature, protect from light; reconstituted solutions used promptly and discarded as cytotoxic.
8.25	Imatinib	Tablets 100mg	Targeted tyrosine kinase inhibitor (TKI) that revolutionized therapy for CML and some KIT/PDGFRA-driven tumors.	Competitively inhibits BCR-ABL tyrosine kinase and also c-KIT and PDGFRA kinases, blocking downstream proliferative signaling and inducing apoptosis in malignant cells.	Chronic myeloid leukemia (Philadelphia chromosome-positive), Ph+ ALL, gastrointestinal stromal tumors (GIST), certain PDGFRA-driven disorders.	Severe hepatic impairment, pregnancy, significant cardiac failure, known hypersensitivity.	Fluid retention (periorbital and peripheral edema), nausea, diarrhea, rash, myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, muscle cramps, rare cardiotoxicity.	Oral dosing usually 400–800mg daily depending on indication; taken with food to reduce GI upset; regular CBC and LFT monitoring.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Imatinib + other myelosuppressants (Hydroxyurea, Cytarabine, Doxorubicin, Cyclophosphamide, Fludarabine, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine) → additive cytopenias; your DB should prompt more frequent CBCs. [MODERATE-INTRA] Imatinib + hepatotoxic agents (Azathioprine, Methotrexate, Asparaginase, Tacrolimus, Dactinomycin, Dacarbazine, Busulphan) increases risk of liver injury; monitor LFTs. [OUT] Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g. Azole antifungals Fluconazole, Itraconazole in your list; Macrolides like Clarithromycin, Azithromycin) increase Imatinib levels; your system should tag these as OUT–critical and suggest dose review.	Older adults often tolerate Imatinib reasonably but may have more edema and cardiac comorbidities; monitoring of weight, edema and heart function is advised.	Used in pediatric CML and Ph+ ALL with weight-based dosing; growth and bone health monitoring required with long-term therapy.	Avoid during pregnancy if possible due to teratogenicity; if needed, careful risk–benefit assessment and fetal monitoring required.	Overdose may cause severe GI upset, edema, cytopenias and liver injury; treatment is supportive with monitoring and temporary cessation.	Store tablets at room temperature, protect from moisture and light; advise patients to take with a large glass of water and food.
8.26	Interferon	Solution for injection 300mg	Immunomodulatory and antiproliferative cytokine used in certain malignancies and viral infections; formulation details vary (e.g. IFN-α, IFN-β).	Binds specific cell-surface receptors, activating JAK-STAT pathways and inducing expression of genes that have antiviral, antiproliferative and immunomodulatory effects.	Hairy cell leukemia, chronic myeloid leukemia (historically), melanoma, Kaposi’s sarcoma, viral hepatitis (HBV/HCV) and others depending on interferon subtype.	Severe depression or psychiatric illness, autoimmune disease, severe cardiac disease, decompensated liver disease (in some hepatitis settings), pregnancy (relative contraindication).	Flu-like syndrome (fever, chills, myalgia), fatigue, depression, anorexia, weight loss, myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, thyroid dysfunction, injection-site reactions.	Dose, schedule and route (SC or IM) depend on indication and interferon subtype; often administered several times weekly for months.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Interferon + myelosuppressive chemo (Cytarabine, Hydroxyurea, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Doxorubicin, etc.) → increased cytopenias and infection risk. [MODERATE-INTRA] Interferon + antidepressants (SSRIs in your list: Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine) may be intentionally combined to manage depression but can complicate side-effect profiles; monitor closely. [OUT] Co-administration with external immunosuppressants (e.g. high-dose steroids) may blunt efficacy.	Elderly particularly susceptible to fatigue, depression and weight loss; interferon is now rarely used in this population for oncology due to tolerability.	Pediatric use is limited to specific indications and requires growth, thyroid, and psychological monitoring.	Avoid in pregnancy unless benefit outweighs risk; some subtypes may be used cautiously but oncology indications are rare in this group.	Overdose primarily amplifies known toxicities: profound fatigue, myelosuppression, liver dysfunction and neuropsychiatric effects; management is supportive with drug discontinuation.	Store refrigerated, do not freeze, protect from light; allow to reach room temperature before injection and discard any unused portion after single use.
8.27	Lomustine	Capsules 40mg	Oral nitrosourea used in brain tumors and Hodgkin lymphoma; characterized by delayed and prolonged myelosuppression and potential pulmonary toxicity.	Alkylates DNA and carbamoylates proteins, inhibiting DNA and RNA synthesis; highly lipophilic and crosses blood–brain barrier.	Recurrent or primary brain tumors, combination regimens for Hodgkin lymphoma, some off-label uses in other refractory cancers.	Severe bone marrow suppression, prior nitrosourea-induced pulmonary toxicity, pregnancy, severe hepatic or renal dysfunction.	Delayed myelosuppression (neutropenia and thrombocytopenia peaking 4–6 weeks post-dose), pulmonary fibrosis (with cumulative dosing), hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, nausea/vomiting, secondary malignancies.	Administered orally as a single dose every 6–8 weeks with strict attention to blood counts for many weeks post-dose; cumulative dose limits applied for lung toxicity.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Lomustine + other delayed myelosuppressive agents (Carmustine, Busulphan, Melphalan, Fludarabine, Cytarabine) → severe prolonged bone marrow failure; spacing and Filgrastim support critical. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Lomustine + pulmonary-toxic drugs (Bleomycin, Busulphan, high-dose Cyclophosphamide, Methotrexate) → additive risk of interstitial lung disease and fibrosis. [OUT] Combined with chest radiotherapy dramatically increases pulmonary risk and should be flagged.	Older adults with diminished organ reserves are poor candidates for Lomustine; if used, consider lower dosing and intensive monitoring of lung, liver and kidney function.	Used in pediatric brain tumor protocols; the delayed marrow suppression requires long observation and caregiver education.	Avoid in pregnancy; teratogenic and fetotoxic risks high.	Overdose may not be immediately apparent but leads to catastrophic delayed pancytopenia and organ toxicity; no antidote exists, management is supportive over several weeks.	Store capsules at room temperature in tightly closed containers, protected from moisture and heat; handle as a cytotoxic drug.
8.28	Melphalan	Tablets 2mg, Solution for injection 100mg	Alkylating agent used in multiple myeloma, ovarian cancer and as high-dose conditioning before transplantation; strongly myelosuppressive.	Bifunctional alkylating agent that cross-links DNA strands, inhibiting DNA and RNA synthesis leading to apoptosis; particularly active against plasma cells.	Multiple myeloma, ovarian carcinoma, high-dose conditioning regimens with stem cell support.	Severe myelosuppression, pregnancy, prior melphalan hypersensitivity, severe renal impairment (relative, requires adjustment).	Myelosuppression, mucositis, nausea/vomiting, alopecia, infertility, secondary leukemia risk, pulmonary fibrosis (rare).	Oral low-dose regimens or high-dose IV regimens with stem cell support; dosing individualized based on indication and renal function.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Melphalan + other alkylators and antimetabolites (Busulphan, Cyclophosphamide, Chlorambucil, Lomustine, Cytarabine, Fludarabine, Hydroxyurea, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine) → severe marrow aplasia; Filgrastim and stem cell rescue often necessary in high-dose regimens. [OUT] Combined with external radiotherapy amplifies marrow toxicity and secondary malignancy risk; flagged as OUT.	Elderly myeloma patients may receive dose-reduced oral melphalan; frailty and renal function must guide dosing and supportive care.	Used less in children; when used for high-dose therapy, long-term infertility and secondary malignancy risks must be discussed explicitly.	Contraindicated in pregnancy unless life-threatening indication; high teratogenic and mutagenic potential.	Overdose results in profound marrow aplasia, mucositis and high infection risk; supportive management with transfusion and infection control is essential.	Store tablets at room temperature, away from moisture; injection powder stored refrigerated or as specified and handled with cytotoxic precautions.
8.29	Mercaptopurine	Tablets 50mg	Purine analog antimetabolite used primarily in acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) maintenance therapy; hepatotoxic and myelosuppressive.	Converted intracellularly to thioguanine nucleotides that inhibit purine synthesis and get incorporated into DNA and RNA, leading to cytotoxicity in rapidly dividing cells.	Maintenance therapy of ALL and some other leukemias; sometimes used in inflammatory bowel disease (non-oncology).	TPMT or NUDT15 deficiency, prior severe myelotoxicity on thiopurines, pregnancy (relative), severe hepatic impairment.	Myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity (cholestatic and hepatocellular), GI upset, rash, increased infection risk, rare pancreatitis.	Oral dosing by BSA, usually once daily in maintenance regimens, adjusted to blood counts and TPMT/NUDT15 status where available.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Mercaptopurine + Allopurinol (in your list) → greatly increased mercaptopurine levels and life-threatening myelosuppression; dosage must be drastically reduced and closely monitored or combination avoided; your system should hard-flag this. [MODERATE-INTRA] Mercaptopurine + other myelosuppressives (Cyclophosphamide, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Busulphan, Melphalan, Chlorambucil, Fludarabine, Thioguanine, Hydroxyurea) → additive bone marrow suppression. [OUT] Live vaccines contraindicated during therapy; avoid concomitant azathioprine (same metabolic pathway) unless exceptional specialist indication.	Elderly rarely receive mercaptopurine for leukemia; if they do, TPMT/NUDT15 testing and frequent labs are essential.	Widely used in pediatric ALL maintenance; long-term hepatotoxicity and secondary malignancy risks are relevant and monitored.	Avoid in pregnancy where possible; if used, teratogenic risk and neonatal myelosuppression must be considered.	Overdose results in severe prolonged pancytopenia and hepatic injury; management is supportive, with transfusions and broad-spectrum antibiotics.	Store tablets at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; handle as cytotoxic.
8.3	Methotrexate	Tablets 2.5mg, Solution for injection 50mg	Folate antagonist used in oncology, rheumatology and dermatology; at high doses, used for leukemia, lymphoma and osteosarcoma; toxicity dominated by myelosuppression, mucositis, nephrotoxicity and hepatotoxicity.	Inhibits dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR), depleting reduced folates needed for thymidylate and purine synthesis, thereby inhibiting DNA synthesis in rapidly dividing cells; also has anti-inflammatory mechanisms at low doses.	Acute lymphoblastic leukemia, lymphomas, osteosarcoma (high-dose), head and neck cancers, breast cancer regimens, and numerous autoimmune diseases at low dose.	Severe renal or hepatic impairment, pre-existing significant bone marrow suppression, active serious infection, pregnancy, pleural effusions/ascites (for high doses unless managed), alcoholism.	Myelosuppression, mucositis, hepatotoxicity (fibrosis and cirrhosis with chronic low doses), nephrotoxicity (especially with high-dose), pulmonary toxicity (pneumonitis), photosensitivity, neurotoxicity with intrathecal/high-dose use.	Oncology: high doses given IV by BSA with mandatory Calcium folinate rescue and therapeutic drug monitoring; low-dose weekly oral or parenteral regimens used in rheumatology; adequate hydration and alkaline urine are critical in high-dose therapy.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Methotrexate + Calcium folinate (in your table) → deliberate rescue after high-dose MTX; rescue timing and dosing must be encoded precisely in protocols. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Methotrexate + nephrotoxic agents (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Amphotericin B, Tacrolimus, Aminoglycosides, Vancomycin) → impaired clearance and enhanced toxicity; your DB should warn and suggest dose adjustment or avoidance. [MODERATE-INTRA] Methotrexate + other myelosuppressives (Cytarabine, Fludarabine, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Busulphan, Melphalan, Cyclophosphamide, Etoposide) → severe marrow suppression; Filgrastim support often required. [OUT] NSAIDs and PPIs (when later added) reduce renal elimination and significantly elevate MTX levels; these should be flagged as OUT-critical.	Elderly have reduced renal function and are highly susceptible to MTX toxicity even at low doses; lower dosing and very close monitoring are mandatory.	In pediatric oncology, MTX is a backbone drug; strict adherence to hydration, rescue and monitoring protocols is critical to avoid life-threatening toxicity.	Contraindicated in pregnancy at any dose; highly teratogenic and abortifacient; effective contraception is mandatory for both sexes around treatment.	Overdose or delayed clearance causes life-threatening mucositis, nephrotoxicity, hepatotoxicity and pancytopenia; high-dose Calcium folinate rescue, aggressive hydration, urine alkalinization and possibly glucarpidase (if available) are required.	Store tablets at room temperature; injectable formulations refrigerated or as per label; protect from light and handle as cytotoxic.
8.31	Mitomycin	Solution for injection 40mg	Bioreductive alkylating agent with antibiotic origin; used in gastric, pancreatic, breast and bladder cancers; also used intravesically and intraoperatively in some settings.	Reduced intracellularly to generate alkylating species that cross-link DNA, particularly in hypoxic tumor cells, inhibiting DNA synthesis and function.	Advanced gastric and pancreatic cancers, breast cancer (older regimens), superficial bladder cancer (intravesical), some intraoperative uses (e.g. ophthalmic/airway scarring).	Severe myelosuppression, renal or hepatic impairment, history of hemolytic–uremic syndrome due to Mitomycin, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression (often delayed and cumulative), hemolytic–uremic syndrome (microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, renal failure), pulmonary fibrosis, mucositis, alopecia, severe tissue necrosis with extravasation.	IV dosing by BSA in cycles, with careful monitoring for marrow and renal toxicity; intravesical doses are much lower and largely local.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Mitomycin + other marrow and renal toxins (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Busulphan, Cyclophosphamide, Fludarabine, Methotrexate, Cytarabine, Melphalan, Tacrolimus, Aminoglycosides, Amphotericin B) → heightened risk of HUS, severe pancytopenia and renal failure. [MODERATE-INTRA] Mitomycin + pulmonary-toxic agents (Bleomycin, Busulphan, Carmustine) → increased risk of lung injury. [OUT] Concurrent radiotherapy enhances toxicity in exposed fields.	Elderly with reduced renal reserve are at high risk for HUS and fatal complications; Mitomycin use must be very carefully justified.	Intravesical use in children is rare; systemic use is limited due to toxicity; only highly specialized centers should consider it.	Avoid in pregnancy due to strong teratogenic and fetotoxic potential.	Overdose can cause delayed catastrophic marrow aplasia, renal failure (HUS) and pulmonary fibrosis; no antidote exists; supportive care and dialysis may be required.	Store vials refrigerated or at room temperature as recommended, protect from light; reconstituted solutions are unstable and must be used promptly with vesicant precautions.
8.32	Mustine	Solution for injection 10mg	One of the earliest nitrogen mustard alkylating agents, now mainly used in topical or limited systemic regimens for lymphomas; extremely vesicant.	Forms highly reactive aziridinium ions that alkylate DNA, causing cross-links and strand breaks, leading to cell death; non-specific to cell cycle phase.	Hodgkin lymphoma (in some classical regimens), cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (topical formulations), palliative regimens.	Severe bone marrow suppression, pregnancy, active infection, severe hepatic or renal impairment, prior hypersensitivity.	Severe nausea/vomiting, myelosuppression, secondary leukemia, infertility, alopecia, local severe tissue necrosis on extravasation.	IV dosing is highly protocol-specific with careful vesicant handling; topical gel/solution used for cutaneous lymphoma is applied carefully to affected skin.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Mustine + other alkylators and myelosuppressants (Cyclophosphamide, Melphalan, Busulphan, Chlorambucil, Fludarabine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Etoposide, Paclitaxel, Vinblastine, Vincristine) → profound marrow suppression; rarely used now for systemic therapy because of this. [OUT] Any concurrent external radiotherapy increases marrow and skin toxicity.	Elderly are poor candidates for systemic Mustine due to intolerance and secondary malignancy risk; topical use may still be considered for cutaneous lymphoma.	Pediatric systemic use is now essentially obsolete, replaced by safer agents; topical preparations rarely considered.	Contraindicated in pregnancy.	Overdose causes massive myelosuppression and severe GI and mucosal toxicity; treatment is supportive with transfusions and infection control.	Store at 2–8°C or per manufacturer, protect from light; reconstituted solution is extremely unstable and must be used immediately; handle as powerful vesicant.
8.33	Paclitaxel	Solution for IV infusion 6mg/ml	Taxane microtubule-stabilizing agent used broadly in solid tumors (breast, ovarian, lung, Kaposi’s); hallmark toxicities are neuropathy and myelosuppression.	Promotes and stabilizes microtubule assembly by binding β-tubulin, preventing depolymerization, leading to mitotic arrest and apoptosis.	Breast cancer, ovarian cancer, lung cancer, Kaposi’s sarcoma, other solid tumors in various combinations.	Baseline neutrophil count <1500/μL (for most indications), severe peripheral neuropathy, severe hypersensitivity to paclitaxel or its solvent, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression (neutropenia), peripheral neuropathy, alopecia, myalgia/arthralgia, mucositis, hypersensitivity reactions (flushing, hypotension, bronchospasm), mild nausea/vomiting, bradycardia or arrhythmias.	IV infusion over 3 or 24 hours after premedication with corticosteroids, H1 and H2 blockers to reduce hypersensitivity; BSA-based dosing every 3 weeks or weekly schedules.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Paclitaxel + other neurotoxic drugs (Cisplatin, Vincristine, Ifosfamide, high-dose Cytarabine) → additive neuropathy; your DB should suggest neuropathy assessment modules. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Paclitaxel + other myelosuppressants (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, Etoposide, Fludarabine, Methotrexate, Cytarabine, Vinblastine) → severe cytopenias; Filgrastim advised in high-risk regimens. [OUT] Solvent (Cremophor EL) interactions with PVC tubing and external drugs must be encoded when you add more infusion products.	Elderly are more prone to neuropathy, myelosuppression and cardiotoxic events; dose reductions and weekly regimens may be better tolerated.	Paclitaxel is used in adolescents and young adults for selected tumors; neuropathy monitoring is essential due to long-term functional impact.	Contraindicated in pregnancy due to teratogenic and fetotoxic effects.	Overdose leads to severe neutropenia, peripheral neuropathy and mucositis; management is supportive with growth factor support and symptomatic care.	Store vials at controlled room temperature, protect from light; diluted solutions stable for limited periods and must be filtered and infused using non-PVC materials if required.
8.34	Procarbazine	Capsules 50mg	Oral alkylating agent and weak monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) used mainly in Hodgkin lymphoma regimens (e.g. MOPP); has significant interaction profile.	Metabolized to reactive species that methylate DNA, causing strand breaks; also inhibits MAO, leading to potential tyramine and drug interactions.	Hodgkin lymphoma (older regimens), certain brain tumors, off-label uses in combination chemotherapy.	Severe bone marrow suppression, pregnancy, concurrent MAOI or serotonergic agents, uncontrolled psychiatric illness, severe hepatic or renal impairment.	Myelosuppression, nausea/vomiting, anorexia, neurotoxicity (drowsiness, confusion), peripheral neuropathy, secondary malignancies, disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol, hypertensive crisis with tyramine-rich foods.	Oral dosing usually daily for 1–2 weeks within combination regimens, with careful dietary counseling and monitoring of counts.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Procarbazine + serotonergic antidepressants (SSRIs: Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Paroxetine, Mirtazapine, Tricyclics like Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine) → risk of serotonin syndrome and hypertensive crises; your system should hard-flag and generally consider this combination contraindicated. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Procarbazine + sympathomimetics (e.g. some decongestants you may add later) → hypertensive crises; OUT-coded. [MODERATE-INTRA] Procarbazine + other myelosuppressives (Cyclophosphamide, Dacarbazine, Chlorambucil, Busulphan, Fludarabine, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Thioguanine, Etoposide, Vinblastine, Vincristine) → additive marrow suppression.	Elderly at extreme risk of CNS toxicity, falls, and hypertensive crises from inadvertent dietary interactions; rarely used now in this population.	Pediatric use is rare and mostly historical; if used, dietary and drug interaction education is challenging and must be carefully managed.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; mutagenic and teratogenic.	Overdose can result in severe marrow suppression, neurotoxicity, seizures and hypertensive crises; supportive management with blood pressure control and ICU monitoring is required.	Store capsules at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; counsel on strict dietary (low-tyramine) and alcohol avoidance.
8.35	Stilboestrol (Diethylstilbestrol)	Tablets 1mg	Synthetic nonsteroidal estrogen historically used in prostate cancer and some gynecologic indications; now largely obsolete due to thrombotic and carcinogenic risks.	Binds estrogen receptors, exerting strong estrogenic effects including feedback suppression of gonadotropins and reduction of androgen production in prostate cancer.	Advanced prostate cancer (palliative), historical use in certain gynecologic conditions; now rarely used due to safer options.	History of thromboembolic disease, estrogen-dependent malignancy in women, severe hepatic dysfunction, pregnancy.	High risk of thromboembolism, gynecomastia, fluid retention, nausea, hypertension, increased risk of cardiovascular events and secondary cancers (vaginal clear cell carcinoma in exposed offspring).	Oral doses (e.g. 1–5mg daily) historically used; now much less common and generally replaced by safer hormonal therapies.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Stilboestrol + anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Enoxaparin, DOACs) → unpredictable coagulation and markedly increased thrombotic/bleeding risk; careful monitoring or avoidance required. [MODERATE-INTRA] Stilboestrol + other hormonal agents (Cyproteron acetate, Tamoxifen, Oestrogens/Progestogens in Section 5) → complex endocrine and thrombotic effects; your system should flag cumulative estrogenic burden. [OUT] Smoking and external cardiovascular risk factors further increase thrombotic risk.	Elderly men with prostate cancer have high baseline cardiovascular risk; diethylstilbestrol is generally avoided in favor of safer hormonal options; if used, strict thrombotic risk management is required.	Not used in children due to strong carcinogenic legacy and endocrine disruption.	Absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy due to transplacental carcinogenesis and reproductive tract malformations in offspring.	Overdose may cause severe nausea, gynecomastia, fluid overload, hypertension and thromboembolism; management is supportive with aggressive VTE surveillance.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; treat as a high-risk hormonal agent with caution in dispensing.
8.36	Tamoxifen	Tablets 20mg	Selective estrogen receptor modulator (SERM) used primarily in hormone receptor–positive breast cancer and for risk reduction; has mixed agonist/antagonist actions.	Competitive antagonist of estrogen at breast tissue ER, while acting as partial agonist in endometrium and bone, thereby reducing breast cancer cell proliferation.	Estrogen receptor–positive breast cancer (adjuvant and metastatic), ductal carcinoma in situ, breast cancer risk reduction in high-risk women.	History of venous thromboembolism, endometrial carcinoma, severe hepatic impairment, pregnancy.	Hot flushes, vaginal discharge, menstrual irregularities, endometrial hyperplasia/cancer, venous thromboembolism, stroke risk, cataracts, mild myelosuppression (rare).	Typically 20mg orally once daily for 5–10 years in adjuvant setting; adherence monitoring is critical.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Tamoxifen + anticoagulants (Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran, Heparin, Enoxaparin) → altered coagulation and increased bleeding/thrombosis risk; INR and clinical monitoring essential. [MODERATE-INTRA] Tamoxifen + other estrogenic agents (Conjugated Oestrogens, Micronized Progestogen, Hormonal IUCD, Ethinylestradiol/Levonorgestrel) can blunt therapeutic effect and increase VTE and endometrial risk; should be avoided or justified in your system. [OUT] CYP2D6 inhibitors (e.g. some SSRIs not in your list yet) reduce formation of active metabolites and may reduce efficacy; encode as OUT-high-impact when you add them.	Elderly women often receive Tamoxifen; VTE and stroke risk must be weighed against benefits; regular gynecological assessment for endometrial pathology is important.	Not routinely used in prepubertal children; in adolescents with breast cancer, same precautions apply with specific fertility counseling.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; teratogenic and may harm fetus; effective contraception required during and for some time after therapy.	Overdose is usually mild, presenting with tremor, dizziness, and GI upset; supportive management is usually sufficient.	Store tablets at room temperature, protected from moisture and light.
8.37	5% Fluorouracil Cream	Topical Cream 15g - 30g	Topical formulation of 5-Fluorouracil for actinic keratoses and superficial skin cancers; works locally with minimal systemic absorption when used appropriately.	Inhibits thymidylate synthase and incorporates into RNA locally in skin lesions, leading to selective destruction of dysplastic and neoplastic keratinocytes.	Actinic keratoses, superficial basal cell carcinoma, Bowen’s disease and other superficial premalignant or malignant skin lesions.	Hypersensitivity to 5-FU, dihydropyrimidine dehydrogenase (DPD) deficiency (systemic toxicity risk), pregnancy, breastfeeding, use on eroded or inflamed large areas in frail patients.	Local inflammation, erythema, crusting, ulceration, pain and photosensitivity at application site; rarely systemic toxicity if applied to large areas, especially with occlusion.	Applied once or twice daily to lesions for several weeks as directed, often until significant inflammatory response occurs; sun protection is critical.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Topical 5-FU + systemic 5-Fluorouracil in this table, or other systemic antimetabolites (Capecitabine if added later) → increased risk of systemic toxicity; your system should flag cumulative 5-FU exposure if combined. [OUT] Concomitant use with external topical retinoids or irritants exacerbates skin reactions and should be flagged as OUT-synergistic irritation.	Elderly with thin skin are more prone to intense reactions and secondary infection; close follow-up and dose adjustment (frequency/area) are important.	Used sparingly in older children/adolescents with extensive sun damage; extreme caution on body surface area and parental education required.	Contraindicated in pregnancy due to potential systemic absorption and teratogenic risk.	Excessive application over large areas could theoretically cause systemic 5-FU toxicity (myelosuppression, GI upset); management is cessation, supportive care and specialist input.	Store at room temperature, tightly closed, away from heat and light; advise patients to wash hands after application and avoid contact with eyes and mucous membranes.
8.38	Sun screen protector	Topical lotion with SPF 30, 40, 50	Topical photoprotective preparation used to prevent sunburn, photoaging and reduce risk of skin cancers; essential supportive agent in oncology patients receiving photosensitizing drugs or radiation.	Contains organic (chemical) and/or inorganic (physical) UV filters that absorb, reflect or scatter UV radiation (UVA/UVB), reducing DNA damage in skin cells.	Prevention of sunburn and photodamage; adjunctive protection in patients on photosensitizing chemotherapeutics (e.g. Dactinomycin, Dacarbazine, 5-FU, Methotrexate, Tetracyclines) and in those with skin cancers or precancerous lesions.	Severe reactions to ingredients, extensive open wounds or active dermatitis at application sites (relative).	Generally mild: local irritation, allergic or photoallergic contact dermatitis, acneiform eruptions; very rare systemic allergy.	Applied liberally (2mg/cm²) 15–30 minutes before sun exposure and reapplied every 2 hours or after swimming/sweating; daily use advised in oncology patients with photosensitizing treatment.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Sunscreen + photosensitizing agents in this table (Dactinomycin, Dacarbazine, 5-Fluorouracil, Methotrexate, Tetracycline eye ointment, some antibiotics like Ciprofloxacin) is strongly beneficial and should be recommended; your system can generate counseling prompts. [OUT] Cosmetic or external topical therapies can interact (e.g. additional irritants) but are low risk.	Highly recommended for elderly on cytotoxic or immunosuppressive therapies, as they are at high risk of actinic damage and skin cancers.	Safe in children; a cornerstone of UV protection, especially in those on photosensitizing drugs.	Safe in pregnancy; non-systemic and beneficial for preventing UV damage.	Overuse is unlikely to cause systemic harm, but might exacerbate acne or contact dermatitis; manage by switching formulation or reducing frequency.	Store at room temperature, avoid excessive heat; ensure cap is tightly closed to prevent degradation of active ingredients.
8.39	Tacrolimus	Tablet 1mg	Potent calcineurin inhibitor used as an immunosuppressant in organ transplantation and some autoimmune conditions; occasionally relevant in oncology patients undergoing transplant or GVHD prophylaxis.	Binds FKBP-12 and the complex inhibits calcineurin phosphatase, preventing dephosphorylation of NFAT and subsequent transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines, thereby suppressing T-cell activation.	Prevention and treatment of graft-versus-host disease, solid organ transplant rejection, severe autoimmune diseases; in your context, primarily in patients undergoing stem cell transplant for malignancy.	Hypersensitivity, uncontrolled infection, severe hepatic impairment without specialist management, concomitant use with other strong nephrotoxins without monitoring.	Nephrotoxicity, hypertension, neurotoxicity (tremor, headache, seizures), hyperglycemia, hyperkalemia, infection and malignancy risk, GI upset, alopecia or hirsutism.	Dosed orally (and IV in some settings) based on target trough blood levels, with doses adjusted for hepatic function, concomitant interacting drugs and transplant protocol.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Tacrolimus + nephrotoxic agents in this table (Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Ifosfamide, Cyclophosphamide at high dose, Amphotericin B, Gentamicin, Amikacin, Vancomycin, Cisplatin) → markedly increased risk of renal failure; renal function and drug levels must be monitored closely. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Tacrolimus + Azole antifungals (Fluconazole, Itraconazole), Macrolides (Azithromycin, Clarithromycin) → strong CYP3A4 inhibition and major elevation of Tacrolimus levels; your DB should tag these as high-priority alerts requiring Tacrolimus dose adjustment and trough monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Tacrolimus + other immunosuppressants (Azathioprine, Fludarabine, Cyclophosphamide) → profound immunosuppression and infection/malignancy risk.	Older adults are particularly susceptible to nephrotoxicity, neurotoxicity and infection; target trough levels may be set lower with more frequent monitoring.	Used in pediatric transplant and GVHD prophylaxis with weight-based dosing and intensive level monitoring; neurotoxicity and nephrotoxicity are key concerns.	Generally contraindicated or used with extreme caution in pregnancy; if required (e.g. transplant), maternal–fetal monitoring must be intense.	Overdose leads to severe nephrotoxicity, neurotoxicity (seizures, encephalopathy), hypertension and electrolyte abnormalities; treatment is supportive with cessation and possibly enhanced elimination if feasible.	Store tablets at room temperature, in original blister or container, protected from moisture and light; advise patients to take consistently with regard to food and to avoid grapefruit products.
8.4	Thioguanine	Tablets 40mg	Purine analog antimetabolite used in acute leukemias; similar to Mercaptopurine but with different toxicity profile (notably hepatic veno-occlusive disease risk).	Converted to thioguanine nucleotides, incorporated into DNA and RNA, inhibiting purine synthesis and cell proliferation.	Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and some ALL regimens, particularly as part of combination chemotherapy.	Previous thioguanine-induced VOD, severe hepatic impairment, TPMT/NUDT15 deficiency, pregnancy.	Myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity including nodular regenerative hyperplasia and veno-occlusive disease, GI upset, mucositis, increased infection risk.	Oral dosing by BSA, usually in short cycles or maintenance regimens; careful monitoring of liver function and blood counts is essential.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Thioguanine + Allopurinol (like other thiopurines) increases risk of severe myelosuppression; combination should be avoided or dosing heavily reduced with close CBC monitoring. [MODERATE-INTRA] Thioguanine + other hepatotoxic/myelosuppressive agents (Methotrexate, Mercaptopurine, Azathioprine, Fludarabine, Cytarabine, Busulphan, Melphalan, Dacarbazine) → increased risk of VOD and pancytopenia; LFTs and clinical assessment for portal hypertension are required. [OUT] Live vaccines contraindicated during therapy.	Older adults with leukemias rarely receive Thioguanine due to better-tolerated alternatives; if used, risk of VOD is particularly concerning.
