Used in pediatric protocols historically; due to hepatic toxicity, many protocols now limit or avoid long-term use; where used, long-term liver follow-up is needed.	Avoid Thioguanine in pregnancy due to teratogenic and mutagenic effects.	Overdose causes severe marrow suppression and potentially irreversible hepatic VOD; management is supportive with transfusions and intensive hepatology care.	Store at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; handle as a cytotoxic antimetabolite.
8.41	Vinblastine	Solution for injection 1mg/ml	Vinca alkaloid that inhibits microtubule assembly; used in lymphomas, germ cell tumors and Kaposi’s sarcoma; strongly myelosuppressive.	Binds to tubulin and inhibits microtubule polymerization, arresting cells in metaphase and leading to apoptosis.	Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphomas, testicular cancer, Kaposi’s sarcoma and other tumors in combination regimens.	Severe bone marrow suppression, active severe infection, pregnancy, significant neurodegenerative disease (relative; neuropathy risk).	Myelosuppression (dose-limiting), alopecia, mild peripheral neuropathy (less than Vincristine), GI upset, stomatitis, jaw pain, local tissue necrosis with extravasation.	IV administration only; must never be given intrathecally (fatal); dosing is BSA-based and given in cycles.	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Vinblastine + other myelosuppressants (Doxorubicin, Daunorubicin, Cyclophosphamide, Cisplatin, Carboplatin, Etoposide, Cytarabine, Methotrexate, Fludarabine, Busulphan, Melphalan, Procarbazine, Bleomycin) → severe marrow suppression; Filgrastim support is often necessary. [CRITICAL-INTRA] Vinblastine + other neurotoxic agents (Cisplatin, Paclitaxel, Ifosfamide, Vincristine) → additive neuropathy. [OUT] Intrathecal administration (even alone) is uniformly fatal and must be hard-blocked in your administration workflows.	Elderly have increased risk of myelosuppression, neuropathy and falls; dose reductions and careful neurological monitoring are advised.	Used extensively in pediatric Hodgkin lymphoma and germ cell tumor regimens; safety depends on strict adherence to IV-only and vesicant handling precautions.	Contraindicated in pregnancy due to teratogenic risk.	Overdose leads to severe myelosuppression, paralytic ileus, neurotoxicity and potentially fatal infections; management is supportive with transfusions, antibiotics and intensive care.	Store vials refrigerated or at room temperature as specified, protected from light; label clearly as “IV use only – fatal if given intrathecally”.
8.42	Vincristine	Solution for injection 1mg, 5mg	Vinca alkaloid with high neurotoxicity and relatively less myelosuppression compared with Vinblastine; cornerstone of many pediatric and adult regimens.	Inhibits microtubule polymerization by binding tubulin, arresting cells in metaphase; neurons are particularly sensitive, leading to peripheral neuropathy.	ALL, lymphomas, Wilms’ tumor, rhabdomyosarcoma, neuroblastoma and many other pediatric and adult malignancies in combination regimens.	Demyelinating Charcot–Marie–Tooth disease, pre-existing severe peripheral neuropathy, pregnancy, intrathecal administration (absolute contraindication).	Dose-limiting peripheral neuropathy (sensory and motor), autonomic neuropathy (constipation, ileus, urinary retention), alopecia, jaw pain, SIADH, mild myelosuppression, severe tissue necrosis with extravasation.	IV administration only, via minibag or slow push; NEVER intrathecal; BSA-based dosing with strict maximum doses (often 2mg per cycle).	[CRITICAL-INTRA] Vincristine + other neurotoxic drugs (Cisplatin, Ifosfamide, high-dose Cytarabine, Paclitaxel) → severe neuropathy; your system should prompt regular neuropathy assessments. [MODERATE-INTRA] Vincristine + severe constipation-inducing agents (Opioids: Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Codeine; Anticholinergics: Hyoscine, Procyclidine) → paralytic ileus risk; bowel regimen and monitoring required. [OUT] Intrathecal injection (even alone) is uniformly fatal; administration route check must be hard-coded into medication administration safety layers.	Older adults are highly susceptible to debilitating neuropathy and constipation; dose reductions and rigorous monitoring of bowel function and gait are mandatory.	Vincristine is a workhorse drug in pediatric oncology; long-term neuropathy and learning impacts require close neurodevelopmental follow-up.	Contraindicated in pregnancy due to teratogenicity and fetal neurotoxicity.	Overdose causes profound neurotoxicity including paralysis, cranial neuropathies, seizures and ileus; treatment is largely supportive and prognosis may be poor in severe cases.	Store vials at 2–8°C or as specified, protected from light; label with bold warnings: “FOR IV USE ONLY – FATAL IF GIVEN INTRATHECALLY”.
8.43	Letrozole	Tablets 2.5mg	Nonsteroidal aromatase inhibitor used as adjuvant and metastatic therapy in hormone receptor–positive breast cancer in postmenopausal women; markedly reduces estrogen levels.	Selectively inhibits aromatase enzyme (CYP19), blocking conversion of androgens to estrogens in peripheral tissues, leading to profound suppression of circulating estrogen in postmenopausal women.	Adjuvant treatment of hormone receptor–positive early breast cancer, extended adjuvant therapy, first-line therapy in metastatic HR+ breast cancer in postmenopausal women.	Premenopausal women without ovarian suppression, pregnancy, breastfeeding, severe hepatic impairment.	Hot flushes, arthralgia/myalgia, osteoporosis and fractures, hypercholesterolemia, fatigue, headache, vaginal dryness, cardiovascular risk increase.	2.5mg orally once daily, often for 5 years or longer depending on protocol.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Letrozole + other agents affecting bone density (Glucocorticoids: Prednisolone, Dexamethasone; GnRH analogues if added; proton pump inhibitors later) → cumulative osteoporosis risk; your DB should prompt bone density monitoring and calcium/vitamin D supplementation. [MODERATE-INTRA] Letrozole + Tamoxifen (in table) is usually avoided as concurrent therapy; if combined or sequenced, endocrine effects and clotting profile become complex and should be supervised by oncology. [OUT] Co-administration with hormone replacement therapy (external estrogens) negates therapeutic effect and should be flagged.	Older postmenopausal women are the main users; fracture risk, hyperlipidemia and cardiovascular risk must be actively managed.	Not indicated in children; off-label use (e.g. for growth disorders) is outside oncology scope and requires specialist endocrinology.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; aromatase inhibitors can cause fetal harm and are only indicated in postmenopausal women.	Overdose usually causes exacerbation of known adverse effects (nausea, vomiting, dizziness, hot flushes); no specific antidote; treatment is supportive.	Store tablets at room temperature, protected from moisture and light.
8.44	Anastrozole	Tablet 1mg; oral solution 1mg/ml	Nonsteroidal aromatase inhibitor similar to Letrozole, used in postmenopausal HR+ breast cancer; reduces estrogen production and recurrence risk.	Reversibly inhibits aromatase enzyme, blocking peripheral conversion of androgens to estrogens, leading to marked estrogen depletion in postmenopausal women.	Adjuvant treatment of hormone receptor–positive early breast cancer in postmenopausal women, first-line treatment in metastatic HR+ breast cancer, sequential therapy after Tamoxifen.	Premenopausal women without appropriate ovarian suppression, pregnancy, breastfeeding, severe hepatic impairment.	Hot flushes, arthralgia, osteoporosis and fracture risk, hypercholesterolemia, fatigue, mood changes, vaginal dryness, possible cardiovascular risk increase.	1mg orally once daily, typically for 5 years or part of a sequential endocrine approach; requires adherence tracking.	[MODERATE-INTRA] Anastrozole + bone-depleting agents (Glucocorticoids, heparin at high doses, other AIs like Letrozole when sequenced) → increased fracture risk; your DB should recommend baseline and periodic bone mineral density testing. [MODERATE-INTRA] Anastrozole + Tamoxifen or systemic estrogens counteract each other’s pharmacodynamics and should generally not be combined; your system should block overlapping adjuvant prescriptions unless specifically justified. [OUT] Lipid-lowering therapy interactions (e.g. statins already in your list – Simvastatin, Atorvastatin) are minor but monitoring of lipids is recommended.	Commonly used in elderly women; bone health, cardiovascular risk and polypharmacy must be actively addressed.	Not indicated in children; not used in pediatric oncology.	Contraindicated in pregnancy; must not be used in women who are or may become pregnant; effective contraception required in perimenopausal women.	Overdose usually leads to enhanced class adverse effects; no specific antidote exists; management is supportive.	Store at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; dispense in original blister packs where possible to maintain stability.

4.2.28	Atropine	Injection 1mg/ml (1ml)	Tertiary amine antimuscarinic (anticholinergic) agent used in bradycardia, pre-anesthesia and as an antidote in organophosphate/carbamate poisoning.	Competitively blocks muscarinic acetylcholine receptors (M1–M5) in peripheral organs and CNS. In organophosphate/carbamate poisoning, it reverses muscarinic effects of excess acetylcholine (bronchorrhea, bronchospasm, bradycardia, hypotension). In bradycardia, it blocks vagal tone on the SA and AV nodes, increasing heart rate.	Symptomatic bradycardia; advanced life support algorithms; pre-anesthetic to reduce secretions and vagal reflexes; organophosphate and carbamate insecticide poisoning; cholinergic crisis from anticholinesterase overdose (e.g., Neostigmine).	Narrow-angle glaucoma; obstructive uropathy (e.g., prostatic hypertrophy with retention); paralytic ileus or severe ulcerative colitis; myasthenia gravis (except with cholinesterase inhibitor overdose); tachyarrhythmias; known hypersensitivity to atropine.	Dry mouth, blurred vision, photophobia, tachycardia, urinary retention, constipation, flushing, confusion, agitation; in high doses or sensitive patients (especially elderly): delirium, hallucinations, hyperthermia.	ACLS bradycardia (adult): 0.5 mg IV bolus every 3–5 minutes up to 3 mg total. Organophosphate poisoning: large, repeated IV doses (e.g., 1–3 mg IV every 5–10 minutes) until bronchial secretions dry and adequate ventilation achieved, then infusion as needed. Dose adjustment based on clinical response and poisoning severity. Use IM/IV/IO per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Neostigmine – atropine is co-administered to block muscarinic effects of Neostigmine (bradycardia, secretions) while preserving its nicotinic action at neuromuscular junction; failure to balance doses can lead to bradycardia or excessive tachycardia. CRITICAL-INTRA: Other anticholinergics (Hyoscine butyl bromide, Bezhexol, Procyclidine, Glycopyrronium bromide, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Miconazole oral gel in high doses) – additive anticholinergic burden (urinary retention, ileus, confusion). MODERATE-INTRA: CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – atropine may mask signs of hypoxia/tachycardia, while sedatives depress respiration; close monitoring needed in poisoned patients. MODERATE-INTRA: Antiarrhythmics (Amiodarone, Digoxin, Quinidine, beta-blockers, calcium channel blockers such as Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) – complex interactions on heart rate and conduction; atropine may transiently increase heart rate and oxygen demand.	Highly sensitive to central anticholinergic toxicity (confusion, hallucinations, urinary retention, constipation, hyperthermia); use lowest effective dose and avoid unnecessary repeated dosing; close monitoring of mental state and bowel/bladder function.	Use with caution; dosing is age/weight-based (e.g., bradycardia 0.02 mg/kg IV, minimum 0.1 mg, maximum single dose 0.5 mg child, 1 mg adolescent); children can develop paradoxical bradycardia with tiny doses – avoid sub-therapeutic boluses.	Avoid unless clearly indicated; generally considered relatively low teratogenic risk but should only be used in acute, life-saving scenarios (e.g., bradycardia, poisoning) with careful fetal monitoring if applicable.	Severe atropine toxicity: anticholinergic syndrome (hyperthermia, dry skin and mucosa, mydriasis, tachycardia, ileus, urinary retention, agitation, delirium, seizures, coma). Management: supportive care, cooling, benzodiazepines for agitation/seizures, and in severe central toxicity, physostigmine under specialist supervision with ECG monitoring.	Store below 25°C, protected from light in original ampoule; do not freeze; keep out of reach of children.
4.2.29	Naloxone	Injection 400mcg/ml	Competitive opioid receptor antagonist used to reverse opioid-induced respiratory and CNS depression.	High-affinity competitive antagonist at μ-opioid receptors (also κ and δ to lesser extent), displacing opioids like Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol and Fentanyl from receptors, rapidly reversing their effects on respiration and consciousness.	Reversal of known or suspected opioid overdose (e.g., Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl); reversal of opioid-induced respiratory depression after anesthesia or procedural sedation.	Hypersensitivity to naloxone (rare); use with caution in opioid-dependent patients because of risk of acute withdrawal; not contraindicated in true overdose emergencies.	Acute opioid withdrawal (agitation, tachycardia, hypertension, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, pain, sweating) in dependent patients; rarely pulmonary edema, arrhythmias, hypertension, seizures, especially in mixed overdoses or patients with cardiovascular disease.	Adult opioid overdose: initial 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/SC; repeat every 2–3 minutes as needed up to 10 mg total, or start infusion based on effective bolus. Post-anesthesia: smaller titrated IV doses (e.g., 0.1–0.2 mg) to improve respiration without complete analgesia reversal. Pediatric: 0.01–0.1 mg/kg IV/IM/SC, repeated as needed per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – naloxone reverses opioid effects; duration of naloxone (30–90 minutes) may be shorter than that of Morphine and Pethidine, and much shorter than Fentanyl patches, requiring repeated dosing or infusion and prolonged monitoring for re-sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – naloxone will not reverse non-opioid CNS depression; residual sedation may persist even when opioid effect reversed. OUT-OF-TABLE: Tramadol + antidepressants (SSRIs, Mirtazapine) – naloxone may partially reverse respiratory depression but has limited impact on seizure or serotonin-syndrome risk; monitor closely.	No specific dose adjustment but start at lower titrated doses in frail or cardiovascular-compromised elderly to avoid abrupt sympathetic surge; cardiac monitoring advisable.	Use standard pediatric weight-based protocols; relatively safe and life-saving; monitor for recurrent respiratory depression as naloxone effect wears off.	Category is generally acceptable for emergency use in pregnancy because maternal and fetal opioid toxicity is life-threatening; monitor fetus for signs of distress and neonatal withdrawal if mother is opioid-dependent.	High doses in non-opioid-dependent subjects are usually well tolerated; in opioid-dependent patients may precipitate severe acute withdrawal with cardiovascular stress. Management of “overdose” essentially supportive and focused on cardiovascular stability; dose can be titrated lower if excessive withdrawal observed.	Store at 15–25°C, protected from light; do not freeze; keep in original carton until use; check visually for discoloration/particulate matter prior to administration.
4.2.30	Pralidoxime mesylate	Injection 200mg/ml, (5ml)	Oxime cholinesterase reactivator used as antidote in organophosphate poisoning, usually together with Atropine.	Nucleophilic oxime group binds to phosphorylated acetylcholinesterase (AChE) and can remove the organophosphate moiety if “aging” has not occurred, thereby reactivating AChE at neuromuscular junctions and autonomic synapses; reverses nicotinic effects (muscle weakness, paralysis) more than muscarinic effects.	Organophosphate insecticide and nerve agent poisoning (in combination with Atropine and supportive care); sometimes in severe carbamate poisoning where benefits outweigh risks.	Hypersensitivity to pralidoxime; caution in myasthenia gravis maintained on anticholinesterases; use with caution in renal impairment due to reduced clearance.	Transient dizziness, blurred vision, diplopia, headache, nausea, tachycardia, muscle rigidity or weakness; rapid IV injection may cause hypertension, tachycardia, laryngospasm, or chest tightness.	Adults: commonly 1–2 g pralidoxime IV over at least 15–30 minutes, repeated every 4–6 hours or continuous infusion (e.g., 500 mg/hour) depending on severity and local protocols. Must be given as early as possible in organophosphate poisoning. Pediatric: 20–50 mg/kg IV over 15–30 minutes, repeated as needed under specialist guidance.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Atropine – always used together in significant organophosphate poisoning. Atropine treats muscarinic symptoms (secretions, bronchospasm, bradycardia), while Pralidoxime treats nicotinic neuromuscular weakness; inadequate atropinization risks death from secretions even when pralidoxime given. CRITICAL-INTRA: Neostigmine – pralidoxime antagonizes therapeutic anticholinesterase effects and may provoke myasthenic symptoms or loss of neuromuscular reversal; avoid unless treating genuine poisoning. MODERATE-INTRA: Other drugs causing tachycardia or hypertension (Atropine, beta-agonists like Salbutamol, vasopressors like Adrenaline, Dopamine, Dobutamine) – additive cardiovascular stimulation; monitor BP/ECG.	Use with caution; elderly may be more susceptible to BP/HR changes; monitor hemodynamics closely, especially with repeated dosing or infusion.	Use is life-saving in pediatric organophosphate poisoning; weight-based dosing; monitor for hypertension, tachycardia, and neuromuscular response; ensure concurrent appropriate Atropine dosing and supportive care.	Use only for life-threatening organophosphate poisoning in pregnancy; benefit usually outweighs risk of untreated poisoning for mother and fetus; fetal monitoring recommended when feasible.	High doses can cause muscle weakness, respiratory depression, tachycardia, hypertension, vision disturbances; management is supportive with airway and cardiovascular monitoring, dose reduction or interruption; no specific antidote.	Store below 25°C, protect from light; reconstitute/dilute according to manufacturer’s instructions; use solution soon after preparation; discard unused portion.
4.2.31	Glycopyrronium bromide	Injection 500mcg/ml	Quaternary ammonium antimuscarinic agent with minimal CNS penetration used to reduce secretions and block muscarinic side effects of anticholinesterases.	Competitive antagonist at peripheral muscarinic receptors, especially M1 and M3 in salivary glands, airways, and GI tract; limited ability to cross blood–brain barrier, so central anticholinergic effects are reduced compared with Atropine.	Pre-anesthetic reduction of salivary and respiratory secretions; prevention/treatment of bradycardia during anesthesia; reversal of muscarinic side effects during neuromuscular blockade reversal with Neostigmine; adjunct in peptic ulcer disease and sialorrhea (other formulations).	Hypersensitivity; glaucoma (especially narrow-angle); urinary retention; severe ulcerative colitis or toxic megacolon; obstructive uropathy; caution in coronary artery disease and arrhythmias.	Dry mouth, reduced sweating, tachycardia, blurred vision, urinary retention, constipation; less CNS toxicity than Atropine but confusion can still occur, particularly in frail elderly; potential for bronchial mucus inspissation.	Pre-anesthetic: adults 0.2–0.4 mg IV/IM 30–60 minutes before induction. With Neostigmine for neuromuscular blockade reversal: commonly 0.2 mg glycopyrronium per 1 mg Neostigmine IV, given concurrently. Doses adjusted per local anesthesia protocols; pediatric dosing is weight-based (e.g., 4–9 mcg/kg).	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Neostigmine – routinely co-administered to blunt Neostigmine-induced bradycardia and secretions during reversal of atracurium, cisatracurium, rocuronium, suxamethonium residual block; inappropriate imbalance can cause bradycardia (too little glycopyrronium) or excessive tachycardia (too much). MODERATE-INTRA: Other anticholinergics (Atropine, Hyoscine butyl bromide, Bezhexol, Procyclidine, Promethazine, Cyclizine) – additive peripheral anticholinergic burden (urinary retention, ileus, tachycardia). MODERATE-INTRA: Drugs causing tachycardia (Adrenaline, Dopamine, Dobutamine, Salbutamol, beta-blocker withdrawal) – synergistic HR increase; monitor hemodynamics.	Safer than Atropine regarding CNS effects, but elderly still at increased risk for urinary retention, constipation, and confusion; use lowest effective dose and monitor.	Commonly used IV/IM as premedication and during neuromuscular reversal; weight-based dosing; monitor for tachycardia, urinary retention, and constipation.	Generally avoid routine use in pregnancy unless required for anesthesia or neuromuscular reversal; limited systemic exposure with single doses; benefits in peri-operative setting often outweigh risks.	Excessive dosing may cause pronounced anticholinergic syndrome (tachycardia, urinary retention, ileus, hyperthermia, confusion); management supportive with monitoring; physostigmine rarely considered if severe central signs develop, under specialist guidance.	Store at room temperature (<25°C), protected from light; do not freeze; keep in original carton until use.
4.2.32	N-acetylcysteine	Solution 20%	Acetylated cysteine derivative used as antidote for paracetamol poisoning and as a mucolytic (other formulations).	Restores hepatic glutathione by providing cysteine precursor, enhancing detoxification of the toxic paracetamol metabolite NAPQI; also has direct free-radical scavenging and vasodilatory effects. As mucolytic (inhaled/oral forms), breaks disulfide bonds in mucus glycoproteins.	Primary antidote for Paracetamol (Acetaminophen) overdose (oral and IV protocols); adjunct in some other drug-induced liver injuries and in certain critical care indications (e.g., contrast nephropathy or ARDS in some protocols, off-label).	Known hypersensitivity or prior anaphylactoid reaction (relative; may still be used with precautions); caution in asthma or bronchospasm when using inhaled forms; caution in severe volume overload (IV infusion).	IV: anaphylactoid reactions (flushing, rash, pruritus, bronchospasm, hypotension), usually early in infusion; nausea, vomiting; oral: nausea, vomiting, unpleasant odor/taste. Generally hepatoprotective rather than hepatotoxic.	Standard IV paracetamol-overdose protocols: e.g., 150 mg/kg over 60 minutes, then 50 mg/kg over 4 hours, then 100 mg/kg over 16 hours (exact regimen may vary by guideline); dose based on actual body weight. Oral regimens also exist (e.g., 140 mg/kg loading, then 70 mg/kg every 4 hours for 17 doses) where IV not feasible.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Paracetamol (oral and injection) – N-acetylcysteine is the specific antidote for overdose; timing of NAC relative to ingestion is critical (best within 8–10 hours). MODERATE-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate and other hepatotoxic drugs (e.g., Methotrexate, some anti-TB such as Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Pyrazinamide; some antineoplastics like Doxorubicin, Cyclophosphamide) – NAC may offer hepatoprotective benefit in overdose or high-risk situations but also adds fluid load when given IV; balance needed in heart failure patients. OUT: Activated charcoal (if given orally together) – charcoal can adsorb oral NAC and reduce its bioavailability; separate doses.	Generally safe; dose is the same but monitor more closely for volume overload and anaphylactoid reactions; adjust infusion rate or dilute more if cardiovascular compromise present.	Same weight-based protocols; important in pediatric paracetamol overdose due to narrow margin for error; monitor for anaphylactoid reactions especially with rapid IV infusion; consider slower infusion rate if reactions occur.	Considered safe and life-saving in pregnancy for paracetamol overdose; use full weight-based dosing; early administration prevents maternal and fetal liver injury; benefits clearly outweigh risks.	NAC itself is rarely “overdosed” in a toxic sense; excessive dosing may cause fluid overload, hyponatremia, and more intense anaphylactoid reactions; management is supportive, including slowing or stopping the infusion, antihistamines, bronchodilators, and vasopressors if needed.	Store at controlled room temperature; protect from light; once opened or diluted, use within manufacturer-specified period; inhalation/oral preparations have similar storage requirements and should be kept tightly closed.
4.2.33	Protamine sulphate	Injection 10mg/ml	Strongly basic peptide antidote used to neutralize heparin (and partially low-molecular-weight heparins).	Forms stable ion pairs with acidic heparin molecules, creating inactive complexes that lack anticoagulant activity; neutralizes unfractionated heparin almost completely and low-molecular-weight heparins (e.g., Enoxaparin) partially.	Reversal of heparin anticoagulation after surgery or in cases of bleeding; partial reversal of low-molecular-weight heparin (Enoxaparin) effect; management of heparin overdose.	History of hypersensitivity to protamine; previous exposure to protamine (e.g., prior protamine, NPH insulin), fish allergy, or vasectomy (higher risk of anaphylactoid reactions); severe hypotension or pulmonary hypertension risk with rapid IV administration.	Hypotension, bradycardia, flushing, dyspnea, pulmonary hypertension, bronchospasm, anaphylactoid reactions; in excessive doses, can exert anticoagulant or antiplatelet effects and paradoxically increase bleeding.	Heparin reversal: approximately 1 mg protamine neutralizes 100 units of unfractionated heparin remaining in circulation (heparin dose in last 2 hours). Maximum single dose often limited (e.g., 50 mg) and given slowly IV over 10 minutes. For Enoxaparin, ~1 mg protamine per 1 mg Enoxaparin given within previous 8 hours neutralizes about 60–80% of activity; follow local protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Heparin (Unfractionated) – direct antidote; dose based on heparin amount and timing. CRITICAL-INTRA: Enoxaparin – partial neutralization only; some anticoagulant effect may persist; monitor anti-Xa level or clinical bleeding. MODERATE-INTRA: Other anticoagulants (Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) and antiplatelet agents (Aspirin) – protamine does not reverse their effects; combined therapy requires broader hemostatic strategy (vitamin K, PCCs, etc.). OUT: Vasodilators or agents causing hypotension (e.g., high-dose anesthetics, Dopamine/Dobutamine shifts, calcium channel blockers) – additive hypotension when protamine infused rapidly; careful BP/ECG monitoring needed.	Elderly cardiac/vascular patients are at higher risk of hemodynamic instability and anaphylactoid reactions; administer in monitored setting, slow infusion, and minimal effective dose.	Used in pediatric cardiac and surgical patients according to weight and heparin dose; careful hemodynamic and coagulation monitoring essential.	Use when necessary to reverse heparin after cardiopulmonary bypass or other procedures in pregnant patients; benefit usually outweighs risk; administer in a fully monitored environment due to risk of severe reactions.	Excess protamine can itself lead to anticoagulant effects, bleeding, and hemodynamic collapse; there is no specific antidote; management is supportive with fluids, vasopressors, oxygenation, and blood products as needed.	Store at 2–25°C as per manufacturer; do not freeze; protect from excessive heat and light; use opened vials promptly and discard unused portion.


Medicine	Presentation	Description	Mechanism Of Action	Indications	Contraindications	Adverse Effects	Dosage And Administration	Drug Interactions	Geriatric Use	Pediatric Use	Pregnancy	Overdose	Storage	
Divalproex sodium	Tablet 500mg	Enteric-coated valproate formulation that dissociates to valproate ion; antiepileptic and mood stabilizer.	Increases brain gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) by inhibiting GABA transaminase and possibly enhancing GABA synthesis; blocks voltage-gated sodium channels and T-type calcium channels at higher concentrations, stabilizing neuronal membranes and reducing high-frequency firing.	Focal and generalized seizures (absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic); prophylaxis of migraine; acute and maintenance treatment of bipolar manic episodes.	Hepatic impairment or significant liver disease; urea cycle disorders; known mitochondrial disorders due to POLG mutations; hypersensitivity to valproate; pregnancy when used for migraine or where safer alternatives exist for epilepsy/bipolar (per many guidelines).	Dose-related: nausea, vomiting, dyspepsia, tremor, weight gain, hair loss, sedation; serious: hepatotoxicity (especially in young children), pancreatitis, thrombocytopenia, hyperammonemic encephalopathy, teratogenicity (neural tube defects), menstrual disturbances/PCOS-like features.	Usual adult: 10–15 mg/kg/day PO in divided doses, titrated by 5–10 mg/kg/week to effect; typical maintenance 15–60 mg/kg/day depending on indication and response. Swallow enteric-coated tablets whole; adjust dose in hepatic impairment (often avoid).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Carbamazepine – increased risk of hepatotoxicity and altered levels (mutual induction/inhibition). CRITICAL-INTRA: Phenytoin – complex bidirectional kinetic interaction; may increase free phenytoin and alter valproate levels. CRITICAL-INTRA: Phenobarbitone – valproate inhibits metabolism, increasing barbiturate levels and sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: Lamotrigine – valproate markedly reduces lamotrigine clearance, increasing risk of serious rash (dose of lamotrigine must be reduced). MODERATE-INTRA: Aspirin – displaces valproate from protein binding and inhibits metabolism, increasing free levels and toxicity. CLASS/OUT: Other hepatotoxic drugs (e.g., isoniazid, methotrexate) – additive liver injury. OUT: Carbapenem antibiotics (e.g., meropenem) – can markedly reduce valproate concentrations and efficacy.	Use lower initial doses and slower titration; monitor liver function, platelets, and ammonia periodically; heightened risk of sedation, falls, and hepatotoxicity; consider dose reduction in frail patients.	Use with caution; higher risk of hepatotoxicity in children under 2 years, particularly with polytherapy; use lowest effective dose and close monitoring.	Generally contraindicated or avoided unless no suitable alternative; major teratogen (neural tube defects, craniofacial anomalies, cognitive impairment); use effective contraception if of childbearing potential.	Symptoms: CNS depression, confusion, coma, respiratory depression, hypotension, metabolic acidosis, hyperammonemia, hepatotoxicity; management: supportive care, airway protection, consideration of L-carnitine in severe toxicity, hemodialysis in massive overdose or severe hyperammonemia.	Store below 25°C, in a dry place, protected from light; keep in original blister/container and out of reach of children.	
Carbamazepine	Tablets 200mg, injection 5mg/ml	Dibenzazepine tricyclic antiepileptic with mood-stabilizing and analgesic (neuropathic pain) properties.	Use-dependent blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels, stabilizing hyperexcited neuronal membranes and reducing repetitive firing and synaptic transmission.	Focal (partial) seizures with or without secondary generalization; trigeminal neuralgia; neuropathic pain; bipolar disorder (as a mood stabilizer) in some protocols.	History of bone marrow depression; hepatic porphyria; AV block; concomitant use of monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs); hypersensitivity to carbamazepine or tricyclics; caution in severe hepatic impairment.	Common: dizziness, ataxia, diplopia, nystagmus, nausea, vomiting, hyponatremia (SIADH); serious: agranulocytosis, aplastic anemia, Stevens–Johnson syndrome/toxic epidermal necrolysis (HLA-associated), hepatic dysfunction, cardiac conduction disturbances.	Adult epilepsy: initial 100–200 mg 1–2 times daily, titrated to 800–1,200 mg/day in divided doses; max ~1,600–2,000 mg/day. Trigeminal neuralgia: start 100 mg 1–2 times daily; titrate up to 600–800 mg/day as needed. For IV (where available), use under specialist guidance with careful monitoring.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex sodium – mutual hepatic metabolism interactions and additive hepatotoxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Phenobarbitone, Phenytoin – strong enzyme-inducing combination; may alter levels of each drug and increase CNS depression. MODERATE-INTRA: Sodium valproate (antiepileptic entry) – see above. CLASS/OUT: Other enzyme inducers (e.g., rifampicin) – reduced carbamazepine levels. CLASS/OUT: CYP3A4 substrates (e.g., many statins, oral contraceptives) – reduced efficacy due to induction.	Start at lower doses and titrate slowly; increased risk of hyponatremia, gait disturbance, and cognitive impairment; monitor sodium, CBC, and liver function more closely.	Use with caution; children may clear carbamazepine faster and require higher mg/kg doses; monitor for rash and hematologic toxicity.	Generally category of risk in pregnancy is unfavorable but can be used if benefits outweigh risks; risk of neural tube defects and other malformations – use folic acid and aim for monotherapy at lowest effective dose.	Symptoms: CNS depression, seizures, cardiac conduction abnormalities, hypotension, coma; management: supportive care, cardiac monitoring, activated charcoal if early, consider multiple-dose charcoal in large ingestions.	Store below 25°C, protected from moisture and light; keep in tightly closed container.	
Lamotrigine	Tablets 25mg, 50mg	Phenyltriazine antiepileptic and mood stabilizer.	Blocks voltage-sensitive sodium channels, stabilizing neuronal membranes and modulating presynaptic release of excitatory amino acids (e.g., glutamate, aspartate).	Focal and generalized seizures including Lennox–Gastaut syndrome; bipolar disorder (maintenance to delay mood episodes).	Hypersensitivity to lamotrigine; history of serious rash (e.g., Stevens–Johnson syndrome) with prior lamotrigine therapy.	Common: headache, dizziness, diplopia, ataxia, nausea; serious: Stevens–Johnson syndrome/TEN, hypersensitivity reactions with multi-organ involvement, blood dyscrasias, aseptic meningitis.	Adult epilepsy: initial doses depend strongly on whether coadministered with valproate or enzyme inducers. With valproate: start 25 mg every other day, titrating gradually (max 200 mg/day). Without valproate/enzyme inducers: start 25 mg once daily for 2 weeks, then 50 mg once daily for 2 weeks, then increase by 50–100 mg/day every 1–2 weeks (usual 100–500 mg/day). Follow local guidelines for bipolar disorder dosing.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex sodium and Sodium valproate – valproate significantly inhibits lamotrigine metabolism, increasing levels and risk of serious rash; lamotrigine dose must be reduced. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone – enzyme induction increases lamotrigine clearance, reducing levels. OUT: Valproate + other antiepileptic combinations – additive CNS effects; adjust per clinical response.	Elderly may require slower dose titration; monitor for dizziness, gait instability, and rash; no major specific adjustment but consider renal/hepatic status.	Approved for pediatric epilepsy in many age groups; dosing is weight-based and influenced by concomitant valproate/inducers; increased risk of rash, especially with rapid titration – use strict titration schedules.	Pregnancy: use only if benefits outweigh risks; some data suggest modest teratogenic risk; lamotrigine clearance increases in pregnancy, so dose adjustment may be required.	Overdose may cause dizziness, ataxia, nystagmus, seizures, coma, and cardiac conduction disturbances; management is supportive with airway, seizure control, and cardiac monitoring.	Store at room temperature (below 25°C), in a dry place, protected from light; keep in original blister.	
Ethosuximide	Capsules 250mg	Succinimide antiepileptic specifically for absence seizures.	Reduces low-threshold T-type calcium currents in thalamic neurons, dampening the thalamocortical oscillations associated with absence seizures.	Absence (petit mal) seizures – especially when other drugs (e.g., valproate) are not suitable or as adjunct.	Hypersensitivity to ethosuximide or succinimides; caution in severe hepatic or renal impairment.	Common: GI upset (nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain), fatigue, dizziness, headache; serious: blood dyscrasias, lupus-like syndrome, Stevens–Johnson syndrome, behavioral changes.	Adult and adolescent: usual starting dose 250–500 mg once daily; titrate in increments of 250 mg at 4–7 day intervals to typical maintenance 1–1.5 g/day in divided doses; children require weight-based dosing (e.g., 20 mg/kg/day).	MODERATE-INTRA: Valproate (Divalproex/Sodium valproate) – valproate may increase ethosuximide levels; additive CNS depression; monitor clinically. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone – enzyme induction may lower ethosuximide levels. OUT: Other CNS depressants – additive sedation.	Use cautiously with lower starting doses; monitor CBC and liver function periodically; watch for CNS adverse effects and falls.	Used in children for absence seizures with weight-based dosing; younger patients may be more prone to behavioral changes and GI intolerance.	Use only if benefits outweigh risks; limited but generally not strongly teratogenic data; absence seizure control may require continuation during pregnancy.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, CNS depression, respiratory depression, coma; treatment is supportive with airway protection and monitoring.	Store at room temperature, tightly closed, protected from moisture and light.	
Phenobarbitone	Tablets 30mg, Solution for injection 200mg/ml	Long-acting barbiturate with antiepileptic and sedative properties.	Enhances GABA-mediated chloride influx by prolonging opening of GABA_A receptor channels; at higher doses, may directly activate GABA_A; also decreases excitatory neurotransmission, raising seizure threshold.	Generalized and partial seizures (especially in resource-limited settings); status epilepticus (IV) where first-line benzodiazepines are unavailable; sedation in some contexts.	Severe respiratory depression; significant hepatic impairment; history of barbiturate dependence; acute intermittent porphyria; uncontrolled severe obstructive pulmonary disease.	Common: sedation, cognitive impairment, ataxia, depression, behavioral disturbance (especially in children); chronic: dependence, tolerance; serious: respiratory depression, hypotension (IV), rash, megaloblastic anemia, osteomalacia.	Adult oral: 60–180 mg/day in divided doses for epilepsy; status epilepticus: 10–20 mg/kg IV at a max rate per local protocol with cardiorespiratory monitoring. Dose adjustments required in hepatic impairment.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – valproate inhibits phenobarbital metabolism, increasing sedation and toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin – mutual enzyme induction; alters serum levels of each; monitor concentrations and clinical response. OUT: Other CNS depressants (e.g., opioids like Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl) – additive respiratory depression and sedation. CLASS/OUT: Oral anticoagulants, many other drugs – marked CYP induction reduces their effect.	Greater risk of sedation, cognitive decline, and falls; use lowest effective dose and consider alternative antiepileptics when possible.	Generally avoided if alternatives available due to behavioral and cognitive effects; if used, doses are weight-based and require close monitoring.	Use in pregnancy only if necessary; risk of neonatal sedation, withdrawal, and possible congenital malformations; supplement maternal folate and vitamin K according to guidelines.	Overdose leads to profound CNS and respiratory depression, hypotension, coma, potentially death; management: airway support, vasopressors, enhanced elimination (e.g., urinary alkalinization or hemodialysis in some settings), close monitoring.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; IV solution as per manufacturer’s recommendations (avoid freezing).	
Phenytoin	Tablets 100mg, Solution for injection 250mg	Hydantoin antiepileptic with non-sedative profile at therapeutic doses.	Use-dependent blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels, stabilizing neuronal membranes and reducing repetitive firing; exhibits saturable (zero-order) kinetics at higher concentrations.	Tonic-clonic and partial seizures; status epilepticus (IV phenytoin/fosphenytoin) after benzodiazepines.	Sinus bradycardia, sinoatrial block, second- or third-degree AV block (for IV use); history of phenytoin hypersensitivity; pregnancy use with caution due to teratogenic risk.	Common: nystagmus, ataxia, diplopia, gingival hyperplasia, hirsutism, coarsening of facial features; long-term: osteomalacia, peripheral neuropathy; serious: Stevens–Johnson syndrome, blood dyscrasias, hepatotoxicity, arrhythmias (IV), purple glove syndrome.	Oral adult: 3–5 mg/kg/day in 1–2 divided doses; adjust based on levels and clinical response. IV loading for status epilepticus ~15–20 mg/kg at a controlled rate (e.g., ≤50 mg/min) with ECG and BP monitoring; maintenance 4–6 mg/kg/day IV or PO.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – valproate can displace phenytoin from protein binding and inhibit metabolism, increasing free phenytoin; monitor levels and toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Carbamazepine, Phenobarbitone – mutual enzyme induction; adjust doses based on clinical response and levels. OUT: Many CYP-metabolized drugs (warfarin, corticosteroids, oral contraceptives, some antiretrovirals) – reduced effect due to induction; monitor and adjust.	Use lower initial doses and monitor serum levels, ECG (if IV), and for CNS adverse effects; high risk of falls and cognitive decline in frail patients.	Use with caution; weight-based dosing; higher risk of cosmetic adverse effects and behavioral issues; monitor carefully.	Teratogenic (fetal hydantoin syndrome, cleft palate, growth deficiency); use only if necessary and preferably as monotherapy at lowest effective dose with folate supplementation.	Overdose causes nystagmus, ataxia, slurred speech, CNS depression, coma, hypotension, arrhythmias; treatment is supportive with cardiac monitoring, airway management; hemodialysis can be considered in severe toxicity.	Store at room temperature, protected from light and moisture; IV formulations per manufacturer guidelines.	
Sodium valproate	Tablets 200mg, Syrup 200mg/5ml	Simple valproate salt antiepileptic; formulation with rapid systemic availability; same active moiety as divalproex sodium.	Increases GABA levels (inhibits GABA transaminase, enhances synthesis) and blocks voltage-gated sodium and T-type calcium channels, stabilizing neuronal firing.	Focal and generalized seizures, including absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic; sometimes used for bipolar disorder and migraine prophylaxis (depending on local practice).	As for divalproex sodium: hepatic disease or dysfunction; urea cycle disorders; mitochondrial POLG-related disease; hypersensitivity to valproate; strong pregnancy restrictions where alternatives exist.	Adverse effects profile essentially identical to divalproex: GI disturbance, tremor, weight gain, alopecia; serious hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis, hyperammonemia, thrombocytopenia, teratogenicity.	Adult: 10–15 mg/kg/day PO in divided doses, titrated to 20–30 mg/kg/day (max ~60 mg/kg/day) depending on response; syrup useful for pediatrics and titration; avoid abrupt withdrawal.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Same as for Divalproex – Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone (mutual hepatic interactions, increased toxicity risk); MODERATE-INTRA: Lamotrigine (reduced clearance, higher rash risk). OUT: Carbapenems (e.g., Meropenem), other hepatotoxic drugs – either marked reduction in valproate levels or additive liver injury.	Use cautiously with regular monitoring of LFTs, CBC, and ammonia; consider lower doses in frail or hepatic-compromised elderly.	Widely used in pediatric epilepsy but high risk of hepatotoxicity in young children (<2 years) and severe teratogenic risk later in life; use lowest effective dose and close monitoring.	Same concerns as divalproex: strong teratogen; generally avoided for women of child-bearing potential unless no alternative; effective contraception mandatory if used.	Overdose as for valproate: CNS depression, metabolic acidosis, hyperammonemia; treat supportively, consider L-carnitine, and hemodialysis in severe cases.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; syrup tightly closed; keep out of reach of children.	
Gabapentin	Tablets 600mg, Oral solution 250mg/5ml	Structural analogue of GABA used as antiepileptic and for neuropathic pain.	Binds to the α2δ subunit of voltage-gated calcium channels in the CNS, reducing excitatory neurotransmitter release; does not act directly on GABA receptors.	Adjunctive therapy for partial seizures; neuropathic pain (e.g., postherpetic neuralgia, diabetic neuropathy); sometimes used off-label for other pain/anxiety states.	Hypersensitivity to gabapentin; caution in severe renal impairment and in history of substance misuse.	Common: dizziness, somnolence, peripheral edema, weight gain, ataxia; less common: mood changes, visual disturbances; rare: respiratory depression in combination with CNS depressants.	Adult epilepsy/neuropathic pain: initial 300 mg once daily, then titrated to 300 mg three times daily; usual effective range 900–3,600 mg/day in divided doses. Adjust dose in renal impairment. Pediatric dosing is weight-based where indicated.	MODERATE-INTRA: Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – additive CNS and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other sedatives (e.g., benzodiazepines like Diazepam, Midazolam) – increased sedation. OUT: Antacids containing aluminium/magnesium – may reduce gabapentin bioavailability; separate dosing.	Elderly more prone to dizziness, somnolence, and falls; start low, titrate slowly; dose reduction may be required due to reduced renal function.	Approved for pediatric partial seizures in many regions; dosing is weight-based; monitor for behavioral changes and sedation.	Limited but generally not strongly teratogenic data; use only if benefits outweigh risks; adjust dose in pregnancy if needed.	Overdose manifestations: marked somnolence, dizziness, ataxia, hypotension; management is supportive; hemodialysis can remove gabapentin in severe renal impairment.	Store at room temperature, protected from moisture; oral solution refrigerated or as per manufacturer’s recommendations.	
Levetiracetam	Tablets 250mg, suspension 100mg/ml, injection 5mg/ml	Pyrrolidone derivative antiepileptic with broad spectrum and favorable interaction profile.	Binds to synaptic vesicle protein SV2A, modulating neurotransmitter release and reducing neuronal excitability; exact mechanism not fully defined.	Adjunctive or monotherapy for focal seizures and some generalized seizures (e.g., myoclonic, tonic-clonic) depending on guidelines.	Hypersensitivity to levetiracetam; caution in severe renal impairment and in patients with significant psychiatric history.	Common: somnolence, asthenia, dizziness; behavioral changes (irritability, aggression, mood swings); rare: psychosis, severe dermatologic reactions.	Adult: initial 500 mg twice daily; may increase by 500 mg twice daily every 2 weeks to a maximum of about 1,500 mg twice daily. Adjust dose according to renal function. IV dosing mirrors oral total daily dose.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (e.g., Morphine, Tramadol, Diazepam) – additive sedation. Advantage: minimal clinically significant CYP interactions with other antiepileptics (Carbamazepine, Valproate, Phenytoin) though additive CNS effects may occur. OUT: Alcohol – increased CNS depression.	Generally well tolerated in elderly; adjust dose based on renal function; monitor for mood or behavioral changes.	Approved for various pediatric seizure syndromes; dosing is weight-based; monitor for behavioral changes.	No major teratogenic signal compared to older antiepileptics, but use only if benefits outweigh risks; adjust dose in pregnancy due to altered clearance.	Overdose may cause somnolence, agitation, respiratory depression; treat supportively; hemodialysis can enhance elimination.	Store at room temperature; protect oral solution from light; use IV solution as per manufacturer guidelines.	
Bezhexol	Tablets 5mg	Anticholinergic antiparkinsonian agent (likely trihexyphenidyl under local brand name).	Competitive antagonist at central muscarinic receptors, restoring dopaminergic–cholinergic balance in basal ganglia; also has peripheral anticholinergic effects.	Parkinsonism (idiopathic and drug-induced); adjunct in extrapyramidal symptoms due to antipsychotics such as Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine.	Narrow-angle glaucoma; GI obstruction; urinary retention; myasthenia gravis; uncontrolled tachyarrhythmias; hypersensitivity.	Common: dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, tachycardia; CNS: confusion, agitation, hallucinations (especially at higher doses or in elderly).	Adults: start 1 mg/day, increase by 1–2 mg every few days to typical 6–15 mg/day in divided doses; use lowest effective dose, particularly for antipsychotic-induced extrapyramidal symptoms.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics on list (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine) – therapeutic use to counteract extrapyramidal side effects but increases anticholinergic load (confusion, constipation, urinary retention). MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (e.g., Hyoscine butyl bromide, Atropine) – additive anticholinergic adverse effects. OUT: CNS depressants – increased risk of confusion/sedation in combination.	Avoid or use extreme caution in elderly due to high risk of confusion, delirium, urinary retention, constipation, and falls; favor lowest possible dose or alternatives.	Use generally avoided in young children; if used for dystonia or EPS, dosing is weight-based with close monitoring.	Use only if clearly indicated; potential for fetal anticholinergic effects is poorly defined; avoid in pregnancy where possible.	Overdose: severe anticholinergic syndrome (hyperthermia, mydriasis, delirium, tachycardia, urinary retention, seizures); management includes supportive care, physostigmine in selected severe cases under specialist supervision.	Store below 25°C, protected from light and moisture; keep out of reach of children.	
Bromocriptine	Tablets 2.5mg	Ergot-derived dopamine agonist acting on D2 receptors.	Stimulates D2 receptors and inhibits prolactin secretion; in Parkinson’s disease, augments dopaminergic activity in the striatum.	Parkinsonism (as adjunct to levodopa or other therapy); hyperprolactinemia, prolactinomas; suppression of lactation where indicated.	Uncontrolled hypertension; severe cardiovascular disease; history of ergot-induced vasospasm; uncontrolled psychosis; hypersensitivity to ergot derivatives.	Common: nausea, vomiting, orthostatic hypotension, headache, dizziness; serious: cardiac valvulopathy (mainly at higher doses), fibrosis (retroperitoneal, pleural), psychosis, impulse-control disorders.	Parkinsonism: start 1.25 mg once daily with food, increase gradually to typical 10–30 mg/day in divided doses; hyperprolactinemia usually needs lower doses (e.g., 1.25–2.5 mg 2–3 times daily).	MODERATE-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – functional antagonism (antipsychotics block dopamine, bromocriptine stimulates), reducing efficacy of both and increasing risk of EPS/psychosis. OUT: Antihypertensives (e.g., Atenolol, Amlodipine, etc.) – additive hypotension; adjust monitoring. CLASS/OUT: Other ergot derivatives – increased risk of vasospasm and fibrosis.	Elderly are at higher risk of orthostatic hypotension, hallucinations, and confusion; start very low and titrate slowly.	Use with caution; not first-line in pediatric patients; if used for prolactinomas, dose is weight and response based.	Avoid in pregnancy for Parkinsonism unless essential; may be used for prolactinomas in some settings with specialist guidance; monitor blood pressure closely.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, hypotension, hallucinations, confusion; treatment is supportive, including gastric decontamination if early and cardiovascular monitoring.	Store in tightly closed container at room temperature, protected from light and moisture.	
Procyclidine	Tablets 5mg, injection 5mg/ml (2ml)	Synthetic anticholinergic antiparkinsonian and anti-EPS agent.	Central muscarinic receptor antagonist, reducing cholinergic tone in the basal ganglia and helping correct dopamine–acetylcholine imbalance; also peripheral anticholinergic effects.	Parkinsonism; treatment of extrapyramidal symptoms (acute dystonia, drug-induced parkinsonism) due to antipsychotics like Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, etc.	Narrow-angle glaucoma; GI obstruction; urinary retention; myasthenia gravis; tachyarrhythmias; hypersensitivity to procyclidine.	Dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, tachycardia; CNS effects including confusion, agitation, hallucinations especially at higher doses or in the elderly.	Oral: usually 2.5–5 mg three times daily, adjusted according to response; in acute dystonic reactions, 5–10 mg IM/IV may be used, repeated as needed per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics on list (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Quetiapine, Olanzapine, Clozapine) – used to treat their EPS but increases cumulative anticholinergic load and risk of confusion, constipation, urinary retention. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Bezhexol, Hyoscine butyl bromide, Atropine) – additive adverse effects. OUT: CNS depressants – additive confusion and sedation.	Avoid where possible in elderly due to high risk of delirium and falls; if used, lowest possible dose and close monitoring.	Not usually first-line in children; may be used for acute dystonia under specialist supervision with weight-based dosing.	Avoid in pregnancy unless clearly needed; data are limited; theoretical risk of fetal anticholinergic effects.	Overdose causes anticholinergic syndrome: hyperthermia, mydriasis, flushed skin, tachycardia, delirium, seizures; supportive management, benzodiazepines for agitation, physostigmine in carefully selected severe cases.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; keep injection away from extremes of temperature.	
Cyclizine	Tablets 50mg, Solution for injection 50mg/ml	First-generation antihistamine with antiemetic and anticholinergic properties.	H1 receptor antagonist with central anticholinergic and antiserotonergic effects in the vomiting center and vestibular pathways, reducing motion sickness and nausea.	Prevention and treatment of nausea and vomiting including postoperative, motion sickness, and in some vertigo conditions.	Severe heart failure; neonates; hypersensitivity; caution in glaucoma, urinary retention, severe hepatic impairment.	Drowsiness, dry mouth, blurred vision, urinary retention, constipation; less common: headache, palpitations, hypotension, confusion (especially in elderly).	Adults: 50 mg PO up to 3 times daily (max 150 mg/day); IM/IV 50 mg up to 3 times daily as needed, according to local protocols.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Promethazine) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Hyoscine butyl bromide, Procyclidine, Bezhexol) – additive anticholinergic effects. OUT: Alcohol – increased sedation.	Use cautiously; increased sensitivity to anticholinergic and sedative effects; higher risk of confusion and falls.	Use with caution; avoid in neonates; dosing in children is weight/age-based per local guidelines; increased risk of paradoxical excitation.	Avoid in late pregnancy unless clearly indicated; may cause neonatal sedation if used near delivery.	Overdose: anticholinergic and antihistaminic toxicity – agitation, hallucinations, seizures, tachycardia, arrhythmias; manage supportively with cardiac monitoring and benzodiazepines for seizures.	Store at room temperature away from light; keep ampoules in outer carton.	
Domperidone	Tablets 10mg	Peripheral dopamine D2 receptor antagonist with antiemetic and prokinetic effects.	Blocks D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone (outside the BBB) and in the GI tract, enhancing gastric motility and accelerating gastric emptying.	Nausea and vomiting of various causes; symptomatic relief of upper GI motility disorders such as gastroparesis (subject to regulatory restrictions).	Prolactin-releasing pituitary tumor; known QT prolongation, significant cardiac disease, or concomitant QT-prolonging drugs; moderate to severe hepatic impairment.	Headache, dry mouth, abdominal cramps, diarrhea; increased prolactin leading to galactorrhea or gynecomastia; rare: QT prolongation and serious arrhythmias.	Adult: 10 mg up to three times daily, 15–30 minutes before meals; maximum dose and duration may be restricted by local regulatory guidance (e.g., shortest duration at lowest dose).	MODERATE-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – antagonism at dopamine receptors may oppose domperidone’s effect and increase risk of extrapyramidal symptoms with some agents. OUT: QT-prolonging drugs (e.g., some macrolides like Clarithromycin, fluoroquinolones like Ciprofloxacin, antipsychotics) – additive QT risk. OUT: Potent CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., Azole antifungals like Itraconazole, macrolides) – increased domperidone levels and arrhythmia risk.	Increased risk of cardiac arrhythmias and sudden death, particularly >60 years; use with great caution, at lowest dose and shortest duration or avoid.	Not recommended in very young infants; in older children use weight-based dosing with caution and cardiac risk assessment if prolonged use anticipated.	Avoid during pregnancy unless benefit clearly outweighs risk; limited human data; avoid high doses or prolonged use.	Overdose: extrapyramidal symptoms (rare), agitation, arrhythmias; management is supportive with ECG monitoring; anticholinergics or benzodiazepines for dystonia if present.	Store below 25°C, protected from moisture and light.	
Metoclopramide	Tablets 10mg, Solution for injection 5mg/ml (2ml)	Dopamine D2 antagonist with antiemetic and prokinetic activity.	Blocks D2 receptors centrally in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and peripherally in GI tract; at higher doses also antagonizes 5-HT3 receptors and agonizes 5-HT4 receptors, enhancing gastric emptying and upper GI motility.	Nausea and vomiting (postoperative, chemotherapy-related, migraine-associated); diabetic gastroparesis; to facilitate gastric emptying and procedures (e.g., small bowel intubation).	Pheochromocytoma, GI hemorrhage or obstruction, perforation, epilepsy, history of tardive dyskinesia with dopamine antagonists; children <1 year (high risk of extrapyramidal reactions).	Common: drowsiness, fatigue, diarrhea, restlessness; serious: acute dystonic reactions (especially in young adults), Parkinsonian symptoms, tardive dyskinesia with long-term use, hyperprolactinemia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome (rare).	Adult: 10 mg up to three times daily PO/IM/IV, 30 minutes before meals; for IV, give slowly; limit duration (often ≤5 days) to reduce risk of extrapyramidal adverse effects.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Antipsychotics (Haloperidol, Chlorpromazine, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – additive dopamine blockade, greatly increased risk of extrapyramidal symptoms and tardive dyskinesia. MODERATE-INTRA: Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – may reduce opioid-induced nausea but adds CNS depression; monitor. OUT: Other QT-prolonging drugs – additive risk of arrhythmia.	Use cautiously in elderly due to higher risk of extrapyramidal effects and tardive dyskinesia; use lowest effective dose and shortest duration.	Avoid in very young children; use with extreme caution in older children with strict adherence to weight-based dosing and maximum duration (often maximum 5 days).	Avoid in pregnancy unless clearly needed, particularly near term (risk of extrapyramidal effects in neonate); however, often considered relatively safe if short term and at lowest dose when benefits outweigh risks.	Overdose: drowsiness, confusion, extrapyramidal reactions, cardiac conduction abnormalities; treat with supportive care, consider anticholinergic or benzodiazepine for dystonia, and ECG monitoring.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; do not freeze injection.	
Prochlorperazine	Tablets 5mg	Phenothiazine antipsychotic used primarily at low dose as antiemetic.	Blocks dopamine D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and limbic system; also has anticholinergic and antihistaminic properties.	Nausea and vomiting; vertigo; at higher doses for psychotic disorders.	Severe CNS depression, coma; bone marrow suppression; pheochromocytoma; hepatic failure; children <2 years or <10 kg (formulation-specific).	Sedation, orthostatic hypotension, anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, blurred vision), extrapyramidal symptoms, QT prolongation, rare agranulocytosis, neuroleptic malignant syndrome.	Antiemetic: 5–10 mg two or three times daily PO; maximum dose per local guidelines; higher doses for psychiatric indications under specialist supervision.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other antipsychotics (Chlorpromazine, Haloperidol, Fluphenazine, Risperidone, Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Clozapine) – additive dopamine blockade and EPS risk. MODERATE-INTRA: Metoclopramide – markedly increased risk of dystonia/EPS when combined. OUT: QT-prolonging agents (e.g., Ciprofloxacin, Macrolides, Domperidone) – additive arrhythmia risk.	Elderly have higher risk of orthostatic hypotension, sedation, EPS, and falls; use lowest dose and consider alternatives in dementia.	Avoid in very young children; in older pediatric patients, restrict to specific indications with strict weight-based dosing and monitoring for EPS.	Avoid or restrict in pregnancy; phenothiazines have some historical use for hyperemesis but should be used only when benefits clearly outweigh risks and at low doses.	Overdose: marked sedation, hypotension, EPS, arrhythmias; treat with supportive measures, airway management, fluids/vasopressors, and ECG monitoring; consider anticholinergics for dystonia.	Store below 25°C, protected from light; keep in well-closed containers.	
Promethazine	Tablet 25mg, Solution for injection 25mg/ml (2ml)	First-generation antihistamine with potent sedative and antiemetic properties.	H1 receptor antagonist with anticholinergic and antidopaminergic actions; depresses the labyrinthine function and chemoreceptor trigger zone, reducing nausea and vomiting; strong sedative effects via central H1 blockade.	Allergic conditions (rhinitis, urticaria); nausea and vomiting; premedication and sedation; motion sickness.	Children <2 years (risk of severe respiratory depression); comatose states; hypersensitivity; caution in asthma, sleep apnea, glaucoma, urinary retention.	Sedation, dizziness, confusion, dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention; serious: respiratory depression, especially in young children; neuroleptic malignant syndrome (rare), leukopenia. MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: other anticholinergics (Hyoscine, Procyclidine, Bezhexol) – increased anticholinergic adverse effects. OUT: Alcohol – greatly increased sedation.	High risk of confusion, falls, urinary retention, and anticholinergic adverse effects in elderly; avoid or use minimal doses with close monitoring.
