Overdose: pronounced sedation to coma, respiratory depression, anticholinergic signs, seizures; treatment is supportive with airway protection, activated charcoal if early, benzodiazepines for seizures, and monitoring.	Store at room temperature, protected from light; injections in original packaging.	
Ondansetron	Tablets 4mg, Oral suspension 2mg, Solution for injection 8mg/2ml	Selective 5-HT3 receptor antagonist antiemetic.	Blocks serotonin 5-HT3 receptors centrally in the chemoreceptor trigger zone and peripherally on vagal nerve terminals, preventing chemotherapy- and surgery-induced nausea and vomiting.	Prevention and treatment of nausea and vomiting associated with chemotherapy, radiotherapy, and postoperative state.	Hypersensitivity to ondansetron or other 5-HT3 antagonists; caution in congenital long QT syndrome, severe hepatic impairment.	Headache, constipation, flushing; rare: QT interval prolongation, arrhythmias, serotonin syndrome when combined with other serotonergic drugs.	Adult: 4–8 mg PO 1–2 times daily for chemotherapy or postoperative nausea; IV 4–8 mg slowly before chemotherapy or surgery as per guideline; adjust dose in hepatic impairment.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other QT-prolonging medicines (e.g., Prochlorperazine, Metoclopramide, Domperidone, some antipsychotics like Haloperidol) – additive QT risk. OUT: Serotonergic agents (SSRIs like Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram; Tramadol) – rare risk of serotonin syndrome; monitor clinically.	Generally well tolerated; consider ECG monitoring if multiple QT-prolonging agents or cardiac risk; adjust dose if hepatic function is impaired.	Commonly used in children with weight-based dosing; good safety profile when dosed appropriately; monitor for constipation and QT issues in high-risk groups.	Generally considered relatively safe in pregnancy (especially for hyperemesis gravidarum) but use only when clearly indicated; follow local guidelines.	Overdose may cause visual disturbances, severe constipation, hypotension, QT prolongation, and arrhythmias; management is supportive with ECG monitoring.	Store at room temperature, away from light and moisture; protect injection from light.	
Paracetamol (Acetaminophen) oral	Tablets 100mg, 500mg, Suppository 125mg, 250mg, Syrup 120mg/5ml	Analgesic and antipyretic with minimal anti-inflammatory activity.	Inhibits central cyclooxygenase (COX) enzymes and prostaglandin synthesis, raising pain threshold and reducing fever; negligible peripheral anti-inflammatory effect at therapeutic doses.	Mild to moderate pain and fever in adults and children; adjunct to opioid analgesics for moderate to severe pain.	Severe hepatic impairment, acute liver failure; hypersensitivity to paracetamol; caution in chronic alcohol use and malnutrition.	Generally well tolerated at therapeutic doses; rash, rare thrombocytopenia; serious: dose-dependent hepatotoxicity in overdose or chronic excessive dosing.	Adults: 500–1,000 mg every 4–6 hours as needed (max 3–4 g/day depending on guideline). Children: 10–15 mg/kg per dose every 4–6 hours (maximum daily dose according to age/weight). Rectal doses similar on mg/kg basis with longer dosing intervals.	MODERATE-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – additive hepatotoxic risk; monitor liver function if used chronically together. OUT: Chronic alcohol use and enzyme-inducing antiepileptics (Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone) – increased formation of hepatotoxic metabolites with overdose risk. OUT: Warfarin and other vitamin K antagonists – prolonged regular high-dose use may enhance anticoagulant effect.	Elderly usually tolerate standard doses but maximum daily dose often limited to 3 g/day; monitor liver function with chronic use or polypharmacy.	Widely used and first-line antipyretic/analgesic; doses strictly weight-based; educate caregivers to avoid unintentional overdose from multiple paracetamol-containing products.	Generally considered safe in pregnancy at recommended doses; analgesic/antipyretic of choice when needed, using lowest effective dose for shortest duration.	Early overdose: nausea, vomiting, diaphoresis; later: right upper quadrant pain, elevated liver enzymes, hepatic failure; management: rapid assessment of amount/time, measurement of serum levels where available, and administration of N-acetylcysteine as soon as possible.	Store below 25°C, in a dry place; protect syrup from light; keep out of reach of children.	
Paracetamol (Acetaminophen) injection	Solution for injection 10mg/ml	IV formulation of paracetamol for analgesia and antipyresis when oral/rectal routes are unsuitable.	Same as oral paracetamol: central inhibition of prostaglandin synthesis via COX inhibition, providing analgesic and antipyretic effects.	Short-term treatment of moderate pain and fever when IV route is clinically appropriate (e.g., perioperative, critically ill patients).	Severe hepatic impairment or active liver disease; hypersensitivity to paracetamol; severe volume depletion or hemodynamic instability without careful monitoring.	Same as oral but IV route may cause local pain, injection-site reactions, hypotension (especially with rapid infusion), and rare anaphylactoid reactions.	Adult: typically 1 g IV every 6 hours (or 650 mg every 4 hours) with maximum daily dose 4 g; infused over ≥15 minutes. Pediatric doses are weight-based per guideline; maximum daily totals not to be exceeded.	MODERATE-INTRA: As with oral paracetamol – additive hepatotoxic risk with Divalproex/Sodium valproate and enzyme inducers (Carbamazepine, Phenytoin, Phenobarbitone). OUT: Concurrent multiple paracetamol-containing products – cumulative dose toxicity; ensure total daily dose within limit.	Use with caution in elderly with hepatic impairment, malnutrition, or polypharmacy; consider lower maximum daily doses.	Dose is strictly weight-based; avoid exceeding recommended daily total; monitor liver function in prolonged use or in malnourished/chronically ill children.	Preferred antipyretic/analgesic IV option in pregnancy when needed; use lowest effective dose for shortest duration; same hepatotoxicity concerns as oral.	Overdose: identical hepatic toxicity pattern as oral but can occur more quickly with IV misdosing; treat promptly with N-acetylcysteine and supportive measures.	Store below 25°C; do not refrigerate or freeze; protect from light; use immediately after opening in line with manufacturer’s instructions.	
Aspirin	Tablets 75mg	 300mg	Salicylate with antiplatelet, analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory effects.	Irreversibly inhibits cyclooxygenase (COX-1 and COX-2), reducing prostaglandin and thromboxane synthesis; at low doses, selectively blocks platelet COX-1, inhibiting thromboxane A2 and platelet aggregation for the platelet lifespan.	Low-dose: secondary prevention of myocardial infarction, stroke, and other thrombotic events; high-dose: mild to moderate pain, fever, and inflammatory conditions (e.g., rheumatic disease) depending on guidelines.	Active peptic ulcer; bleeding disorders; history of aspirin-induced asthma or NSAID hypersensitivity; severe renal or hepatic impairment; children with viral infections (risk of Reye’s syndrome).	Dyspepsia, GI bleeding, bruising; tinnitus at high doses; serious: GI ulceration, hemorrhage, hypersensitivity reactions (bronchospasm, anaphylaxis), Reye’s syndrome in children.	Low-dose antiplatelet: 75–150 mg once daily. Analgesic/anti-inflammatory: 300–900 mg every 4–6 hours up to max daily dose per guideline (often 4 g).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate – displaces valproate from protein binding and may inhibit its metabolism, increasing free valproate levels and hepatotoxic risk. MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Ibuprofen, Diclofenac, Mefenamic acid) – increased GI bleeding risk. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Enoxaparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk. OUT: Corticosteroids (Prednisolone, Dexamethasone) – additive GI ulceration risk.	Use cautiously due to high risk of GI bleeding and renal impairment; co-prescribe gastroprotection where indicated; consider lower doses or alternative agents.	Avoid for analgesia/antipyresis in children due to Reye’s syndrome risk; may be used in specific cardiac conditions under specialist care.	Use in pregnancy near term is generally avoided (risk of premature closure of ductus arteriosus and maternal bleeding); low-dose aspirin may be used in selected high-risk obstetric conditions under specialist guidance.	Acute overdose: tinnitus, vomiting, hyperventilation, metabolic acidosis, hyperthermia, confusion, pulmonary edema, coma; management includes activated charcoal, correction of acid–base disturbances, and hemodialysis in severe poisoning.	Store in a cool, dry place, protected from moisture; keep out of reach of children.
Ibuprofen	Tablets 200mg	 400mg	Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) with analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory actions.	Non-selective inhibitor of COX-1 and COX-2, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis, thus reducing inflammation, pain, and fever.	Mild to moderate pain, dysmenorrhea, inflammatory conditions (e.g., arthritis), and fever.	Active or history of GI ulceration/bleeding, severe heart failure, severe renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID-induced asthma or hypersensitivity.	Dyspepsia, nausea, abdominal pain; serious: GI bleeding/ulceration, renal impairment, fluid retention, increased cardiovascular risk with long-term use, bronchospasm in susceptible individuals.	Adults: 200–400 mg every 6–8 hours as needed; maximum daily dose typically 1,200–2,400 mg depending on indication and guideline. Lower doses are used OTC.	MODERATE-INTRA: Aspirin, Diclofenac, Mefenamic acid – additive GI bleeding and renal risk with other NSAIDs. OUT: ACE inhibitors (Captopril, Lisinopril, Enalapril), ARBs (Losartan, Telmisartan), Diuretics (Frusemide, Hydrochlorthiazide, Amiloride) – reduced antihypertensive/diuretic effect and increased nephrotoxicity risk. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk.	Elderly at higher risk of GI bleeding, renal impairment, and cardiovascular events; use lowest effective dose for shortest duration with gastroprotection where indicated.	Use short-term and at lowest effective dose in children, strictly weight-based; avoid in dehydration due to renal risk.	Avoid in third trimester (risk of premature closure of ductus arteriosus, fetal renal impairment); use with caution in earlier pregnancy if benefit outweighs risk.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, epigastric pain, drowsiness; severe cases may cause metabolic acidosis, renal failure, seizures; management is mainly supportive, activated charcoal if early, monitoring of renal function.	Store below 25°C, in a dry place; protect from light; keep out of reach of children.
Diclofenac	Tablets 50mg, 100mg, Solution for injection 75mg, spray	NSAID of phenylacetic acid class with strong anti-inflammatory and analgesic effects.	Non-selective COX inhibitor with some preference for COX-2; reduces prostaglandin synthesis and inflammatory mediators.	Acute pain, postoperative pain, musculoskeletal and rheumatic disorders, dysmenorrhea.	Active peptic ulcer or GI bleeding, severe heart failure, significant renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID hypersensitivity, third-trimester pregnancy.	Dyspepsia, abdominal pain, nausea; serious: GI bleeding and ulceration, renal impairment, edema, increased cardiovascular risk (MI, stroke), hepatotoxicity, injection-site reactions (for IM).	Adults: orally 50 mg 2–3 times daily or 100 mg modified-release once/twice daily; IM 75 mg as a deep injection, often once daily for short durations; follow local protocols.	MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Aspirin, Ibuprofen, Mefenamic acid) – significant additive GI and renal toxicity. OUT: ACE inhibitors/ARBs and Diuretics (Captopril, Lisinopril, Losartan, Frusemide, Hydrochlorthiazide, Spironolactone) – decreased antihypertensive effect, increased risk of renal impairment. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Heparin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk.	Avoid or use with great caution in elderly; high risk of GI and cardiovascular adverse effects; use minimal effective dose for shortest duration with gastroprotection.	Use in children is limited and formulation-dependent; follow weight-based dosing where approved; monitor GI and renal function.	Avoid in third trimester; use in first and second trimester only if essential and at lowest effective dose; increased risk of miscarriage and cardiac malformations has been reported with NSAIDs.	Overdose: GI irritation, renal failure, hypotension, CNS depression, rarely liver injury; management is supportive, activated charcoal early, monitoring of renal and hepatic function.	Store at room temperature in a dry place; protect from light; injection stored as per manufacturer guidance.	
Mefenamic acid	Tablets 250mg, 500mg, Suppository 125mg	NSAID of anthranilic acid (fenamate) class used mainly for dysmenorrhea and pain.	Non-selective inhibition of COX-1 and COX-2, decreasing prostaglandin synthesis and thus pain and inflammation.	Mild to moderate pain, primary dysmenorrhea, menorrhagia (depending on guideline).	Active peptic ulcer disease, inflammatory bowel disease, severe renal or hepatic impairment, NSAID-induced asthma or hypersensitivity.	GI upset (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea), abdominal pain; serious: GI bleeding/ulceration, renal impairment, rare hemolytic anemia and blood dyscrasias, hypersensitivity reactions.	Adults: 500 mg initially, then 250–500 mg every 6–8 hours as needed for short-term use; limit duration (often ≤7 days).	MODERATE-INTRA: Other NSAIDs (Aspirin, Ibuprofen, Diclofenac) – additive GI and renal toxicity. OUT: Anticoagulants (Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) – increased bleeding risk. OUT: ACE inhibitors/ARBs and Diuretics – decreased antihypertensive effect and increased risk of renal dysfunction.	Use cautiously and for shortest possible duration; elderly have increased risk of GI and renal adverse effects; consider alternative analgesics.	Use only with caution in adolescents; avoid prolonged use; monitor for GI and renal issues.	Avoid especially in third trimester; use earlier in pregnancy only if absolutely necessary and short term.	Overdose: nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, drowsiness; serious cases can cause seizures, renal failure, metabolic acidosis; treatment is supportive; consider activated charcoal early.	Store at room temperature in a dry, cool place; protect from moisture.	
Morphine	Powder 5g, Solution for injection 10mg/ml ampoule	Strong opioid analgesic; prototype μ-opioid receptor agonist.	Binds to μ-opioid receptors in CNS and GI tract, inhibiting ascending pain pathways, altering perception and response to pain; causes generalized CNS depression and reduced GI motility.	Moderate to severe acute and chronic pain (e.g., postoperative, cancer pain); pulmonary edema in some settings.	Significant respiratory depression; acute asthma or severe COPD; paralytic ileus; raised intracranial pressure in uncontrolled head injury; hypersensitivity to morphine; caution in elderly, renal/hepatic impairment.	Common: constipation, nausea, vomiting, sedation, pruritus, urinary retention; serious: respiratory depression, hypotension, bradycardia, dependence and tolerance, confusion, myoclonus at high doses.	Dosing must be individualized. Parenteral adult starting doses: 2.5–10 mg IM/IV/SC every 4 hours as needed, titrated to pain and respiratory status. Oral doses depend on formulation (immediate vs. sustained release) and prior opioid exposure. Use standard opioid titration protocols.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – additive sedation and respiratory depression; monitor closely. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol – additive opioid effects and risk of serotonin syndrome/seizures. OUT: Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (not in list) – risk of severe CNS and respiratory depression or excitation.	Elderly particularly sensitive to respiratory depression and confusion; start very low and titrate cautiously; monitor for constipation and falls.	Use with great caution; dosing is weight-based; monitor for respiratory depression, especially in opioid-naïve children; avoid codeine-like combinations confusion, ensure safe dosing.	Use only when benefits outweigh fetal and neonatal risks; may cause neonatal respiratory depression and withdrawal; prolonged use in pregnancy requires neonatal monitoring.	Overdose: pinpoint pupils, profound respiratory depression, coma, hypotension; treatment with airway support and IV naloxone titrated to adequate ventilation; continuous monitoring needed due to shorter naloxone half-life than morphine.	Store in a secure, locked cabinet in accordance with controlled-drug regulations; protect solution from light; discard opened ampoules as per policy.	
Pethidine	Tablets 50mg, Solution for injection 50mg/ml	Synthetic opioid analgesic (also known as meperidine).	μ-opioid receptor agonist with additional local anesthetic-like properties; metabolized to normeperidine, which has CNS excitatory effects (proconvulsant).	Moderate to severe pain, including obstetric analgesia, although use has declined in many guidelines due to safety concerns.	Significant respiratory depression; MAOI use within 14 days; severe hepatic or renal impairment (due to metabolite accumulation); history of seizures; hypersensitivity to pethidine.	Sedation, nausea, vomiting, constipation, orthostatic hypotension; serious: respiratory depression, seizures (due to normeperidine accumulation), serotonin syndrome with serotonergic agents.	Adult: 25–100 mg IM/SC/slow IV every 3–4 hours as required, maximum daily dose per local guidance; avoid prolonged repeated dosing, especially in renal or hepatic impairment.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (not in list) – risk of life-threatening serotonin syndrome and excitation. MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Fentanyl, Tramadol, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive sedation and respiratory depression. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol and serotonergic antidepressants (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine) – increased serotonin syndrome risk.	Use with great caution; high risk of confusion, falls, and metabolite accumulation leading to seizures; often avoided in elderly in favor of safer opioids.	Avoid in neonates and infants; in older children use with caution and weight-based dosing; risk of respiratory depression and seizures.	Use with caution in labor; crosses placenta and may cause neonatal respiratory depression; avoid in late pregnancy unless adequate neonatal resuscitation is available.	Overdose: respiratory depression, CNS depression, seizures, hypotension; treat with airway support, naloxone for opioid effect, benzodiazepines for seizures, and monitoring for recurrent toxicity due to metabolite.	Store securely as controlled drug, protected from light; avoid extreme temperatures; follow local narcotic storage regulations.	
Tramadol	Tablets/Capsule 50mg; Solution for injection 50mg/ml	Atypical centrally acting analgesic with weak opioid and monoaminergic activity.	Weak μ-opioid receptor agonist and inhibitor of serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake in the CNS, modulating pain pathways.	Moderate pain where non-opioid analgesics are inadequate; sometimes as step-2 analgesic in WHO pain ladder.	Acute intoxication with alcohol, hypnotics, opioids, or psychotropics; uncontrolled epilepsy; concomitant or recent (14 days) MAOI use; severe respiratory depression.	Nausea, vomiting, dizziness, sedation, dry mouth, sweating, constipation; serious: seizures (especially at high doses or with predisposition), serotonin syndrome with serotonergic drugs, respiratory depression, dependence.	Adults: 50–100 mg PO/IM/IV every 4–6 hours as needed (max 400 mg/day; lower in elderly or hepatic/renal impairment).	CRITICAL-INTRA: Serotonergic antidepressants (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine) – increased risk of serotonin syndrome and seizures. MODERATE-INTRA: Other opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl) and CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin) – additive respiratory and CNS depression. OUT: Carbamazepine, Phenytoin – induce metabolism, reducing tramadol efficacy and potentially increasing neurotoxicity.	Use reduced doses and longer dosing intervals; increased risk of confusion, falls, and seizures; avoid or use cautiously in patients with dementia or seizure history.	Not recommended in children under some ages per evolving guidance (e.g., <12 years) due to variable metabolism and risk of respiratory depression; avoid in post-tonsillectomy/adenoidectomy patients.	Avoid in late pregnancy due to neonatal respiratory depression and withdrawal; use only if benefits outweigh risks; avoid during breastfeeding in some guidelines due to infant toxicity risk.	Overdose: CNS depression, seizures, respiratory depression, hypotension, serotonin syndrome; treatment includes airway support, benzodiazepines for seizures, consideration of naloxone (may only partially reverse and potentially precipitate seizures).	Store at room temperature, in tightly closed container; keep out of reach of children; handle as controlled substance where applicable.	
Fentanyl	Solution for injection 50mcg/ml, Patches 12mcg/50mcg Patches	Highly potent synthetic opioid analgesic.	Selective μ-opioid receptor agonist with rapid onset and short duration (IV); transdermal patches provide sustained systemic delivery; 50–100 times more potent than morphine.	Severe acute pain (perioperative, ICU) and chronic cancer pain (especially with transdermal patches in opioid-tolerant patients).	Significant respiratory depression; acute severe asthma or respiratory compromise; patients not opioid-tolerant (for high-dose patches); hypersensitivity to fentanyl or adhesives; caution in increased intracranial pressure and bradyarrhythmias.	Profound respiratory depression, bradycardia, chest wall rigidity (with rapid IV infusion), nausea, vomiting, constipation, sedation, dependence and tolerance.	IV: 25–100 mcg given slowly for analgesia or as part of anesthesia, titrated to response; higher doses used in anesthesia protocols. Transdermal: apply patch delivering 12–100 mcg/hour every 72 hours; only for opioid-tolerant patients; dose conversion from prior opioids must follow established guidelines.	CRITICAL-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – high risk of additive respiratory depression and sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: CYP3A4 inhibitors (not in table but may include Azole antifungals, macrolides like Clarithromycin, some antiretrovirals) – can increase fentanyl levels and prolong toxicity. OUT: Serotonergic agents (SSRIs such as Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram; Mirtazapine; Tramadol) – rare but possible serotonin toxicity; monitor closely.	Use extreme caution; very high risk of respiratory depression, confusion, falls; start with lowest possible doses and monitor closely; transdermal patches generally reserved for carefully selected patients.	Transdermal use in children is restricted and specialist-only; IV dosing is weight-based and used in anesthesia/ICU with experienced staff only.	Use in pregnancy only if clearly indicated (e.g., anesthesia); may cause neonatal respiratory depression; avoid in labor unless facilities for neonatal resuscitation are available.	Overdose: life-threatening respiratory depression, pinpoint pupils, coma, chest wall rigidity; immediate airway support and high-dose naloxone with repeated administration may be required due to long fentanyl effect; monitor for recurrence.	Store securely in controlled-drug storage; protect patches from heat and dispose carefully; keep ampoules protected from light and out of reach of children.	
                                                        
                                                        
Medicine	Presentation	Description	Mechanism Of Action	Indications	Contraindications	Adverse Effects	Dosage And Administration	Drug Interactions	Geriatric Use	Pediatric Use	Pregnancy	Overdose	Storage	
Atropine	Injection 1mg/ml (1ml)	Tertiary amine antimuscarinic (anticholinergic) agent used in bradycardia, pre-anesthesia and as an antidote in organophosphate/carbamate poisoning.	Competitively blocks muscarinic acetylcholine receptors (M1–M5) in peripheral organs and CNS. In organophosphate/carbamate poisoning, it reverses muscarinic effects of excess acetylcholine (bronchorrhea, bronchospasm, bradycardia, hypotension). In bradycardia, it blocks vagal tone on the SA and AV nodes, increasing heart rate.	Symptomatic bradycardia; advanced life support algorithms; pre-anesthetic to reduce secretions and vagal reflexes; organophosphate and carbamate insecticide poisoning; cholinergic crisis from anticholinesterase overdose (e.g., Neostigmine).	Narrow-angle glaucoma; obstructive uropathy (e.g., prostatic hypertrophy with retention); paralytic ileus or severe ulcerative colitis; myasthenia gravis (except with cholinesterase inhibitor overdose); tachyarrhythmias; known hypersensitivity to atropine.	Dry mouth, blurred vision, photophobia, tachycardia, urinary retention, constipation, flushing, confusion, agitation; in high doses or sensitive patients (especially elderly): delirium, hallucinations, hyperthermia.	ACLS bradycardia (adult): 0.5 mg IV bolus every 3–5 minutes up to 3 mg total. Organophosphate poisoning: large, repeated IV doses (e.g., 1–3 mg IV every 5–10 minutes) until bronchial secretions dry and adequate ventilation achieved, then infusion as needed. Dose adjustment based on clinical response and poisoning severity. Use IM/IV/IO per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Neostigmine – atropine is co-administered to block muscarinic effects of Neostigmine (bradycardia, secretions) while preserving its nicotinic action at neuromuscular junction; failure to balance doses can lead to bradycardia or excessive tachycardia. CRITICAL-INTRA: Other anticholinergics (Hyoscine butyl bromide, Bezhexol, Procyclidine, Glycopyrronium bromide, Promethazine, Cyclizine, Miconazole oral gel in high doses) – additive anticholinergic burden (urinary retention, ileus, confusion). MODERATE-INTRA: CNS depressants (Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – atropine may mask signs of hypoxia/tachycardia, while sedatives depress respiration; close monitoring needed in poisoned patients. MODERATE-INTRA: Antiarrhythmics (Amiodarone, Digoxin, Quinidine, beta-blockers, calcium channel blockers such as Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) – complex interactions on heart rate and conduction; atropine may transiently increase heart rate and oxygen demand.	Highly sensitive to central anticholinergic toxicity (confusion, hallucinations, urinary retention, constipation, hyperthermia); use lowest effective dose and avoid unnecessary repeated dosing; close monitoring of mental state and bowel/bladder function.	Use with caution; dosing is age/weight-based (e.g., bradycardia 0.02 mg/kg IV, minimum 0.1 mg, maximum single dose 0.5 mg child, 1 mg adolescent); children can develop paradoxical bradycardia with tiny doses – avoid sub-therapeutic boluses.	Avoid unless clearly indicated; generally considered relatively low teratogenic risk but should only be used in acute, life-saving scenarios (e.g., bradycardia, poisoning) with careful fetal monitoring if applicable.	Severe atropine toxicity: anticholinergic syndrome (hyperthermia, dry skin and mucosa, mydriasis, tachycardia, ileus, urinary retention, agitation, delirium, seizures, coma). Management: supportive care, cooling, benzodiazepines for agitation/seizures, and in severe central toxicity, physostigmine under specialist supervision with ECG monitoring.	Store below 25°C, protected from light in original ampoule; do not freeze; keep out of reach of children.	
Naloxone	Injection 400mcg/ml	Competitive opioid receptor antagonist used to reverse opioid-induced respiratory and CNS depression.	High-affinity competitive antagonist at μ-opioid receptors (also κ and δ to lesser extent), displacing opioids like Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol and Fentanyl from receptors, rapidly reversing their effects on respiration and consciousness.	Reversal of known or suspected opioid overdose (e.g., Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl); reversal of opioid-induced respiratory depression after anesthesia or procedural sedation.	Hypersensitivity to naloxone (rare); use with caution in opioid-dependent patients because of risk of acute withdrawal; not contraindicated in true overdose emergencies.	Acute opioid withdrawal (agitation, tachycardia, hypertension, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, pain, sweating) in dependent patients; rarely pulmonary edema, arrhythmias, hypertension, seizures, especially in mixed overdoses or patients with cardiovascular disease.	Adult opioid overdose: initial 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/SC; repeat every 2–3 minutes as needed up to 10 mg total, or start infusion based on effective bolus. Post-anesthesia: smaller titrated IV doses (e.g., 0.1–0.2 mg) to improve respiration without complete analgesia reversal. Pediatric: 0.01–0.1 mg/kg IV/IM/SC, repeated as needed per protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Morphine, Pethidine, Tramadol, Fentanyl – naloxone reverses opioid effects; duration of naloxone (30–90 minutes) may be shorter than that of Morphine and Pethidine, and much shorter than Fentanyl patches, requiring repeated dosing or infusion and prolonged monitoring for re-sedation. MODERATE-INTRA: Other CNS depressants (Promethazine, Cyclizine, Diazepam, Midazolam, Gabapentin, Levetiracetam) – naloxone will not reverse non-opioid CNS depression; residual sedation may persist even when opioid effect reversed. OUT-OF-TABLE: Tramadol + antidepressants (SSRIs, Mirtazapine) – naloxone may partially reverse respiratory depression but has limited impact on seizure or serotonin-syndrome risk; monitor closely.	No specific dose adjustment but start at lower titrated doses in frail or cardiovascular-compromised elderly to avoid abrupt sympathetic surge; cardiac monitoring advisable.	Use standard pediatric weight-based protocols; relatively safe and life-saving; monitor for recurrent respiratory depression as naloxone effect wears off.	Category is generally acceptable for emergency use in pregnancy because maternal and fetal opioid toxicity is life-threatening; monitor fetus for signs of distress and neonatal withdrawal if mother is opioid-dependent.	High doses in non-opioid-dependent subjects are usually well tolerated; in opioid-dependent patients may precipitate severe acute withdrawal with cardiovascular stress. Management of “overdose” essentially supportive and focused on cardiovascular stability; dose can be titrated lower if excessive withdrawal observed.	Store at 15–25°C, protected from light; do not freeze; keep in original carton until use; check visually for discoloration/particulate matter prior to administration.	
Pralidoxime mesylate	Injection 200mg/ml (5ml)	Oxime cholinesterase reactivator used as antidote in organophosphate poisoning, usually together with Atropine.	Nucleophilic oxime group binds to phosphorylated acetylcholinesterase (AChE) and can remove the organophosphate moiety if “aging” has not occurred, thereby reactivating AChE at neuromuscular junctions and autonomic synapses; reverses nicotinic effects (muscle weakness, paralysis) more than muscarinic effects.	Organophosphate insecticide and nerve agent poisoning (in combination with Atropine and supportive care); sometimes in severe carbamate poisoning where benefits outweigh risks.	Hypersensitivity to pralidoxime; caution in myasthenia gravis maintained on anticholinesterases; use with caution in renal impairment due to reduced clearance.	Transient dizziness, blurred vision, diplopia, headache, nausea, tachycardia, muscle rigidity or weakness; rapid IV injection may cause hypertension, tachycardia, laryngospasm, or chest tightness.	Adults: commonly 1–2 g pralidoxime IV over at least 15–30 minutes, repeated every 4–6 hours or continuous infusion (e.g., 500 mg/hour) depending on severity and local protocols. Must be given as early as possible in organophosphate poisoning. Pediatric: 20–50 mg/kg IV over 15–30 minutes, repeated as needed under specialist guidance.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Atropine – always used together in significant organophosphate poisoning. Atropine treats muscarinic symptoms (secretions, bronchospasm, bradycardia), while Pralidoxime treats nicotinic neuromuscular weakness; inadequate atropinization risks death from secretions even when pralidoxime given. CRITICAL-INTRA: Neostigmine – pralidoxime antagonizes therapeutic anticholinesterase effects and may provoke myasthenic symptoms or loss of neuromuscular reversal; avoid unless treating genuine poisoning. MODERATE-INTRA: Other drugs causing tachycardia or hypertension (Atropine, beta-agonists like Salbutamol, vasopressors like Adrenaline, Dopamine, Dobutamine) – additive cardiovascular stimulation; monitor BP/ECG.	Use with caution; elderly may be more susceptible to BP/HR changes; monitor hemodynamics closely, especially with repeated dosing or infusion.	Use is life-saving in pediatric organophosphate poisoning; weight-based dosing; monitor for hypertension, tachycardia, and neuromuscular response; ensure concurrent appropriate Atropine dosing and supportive care.	Use only for life-threatening organophosphate poisoning in pregnancy; benefit usually outweighs risk of untreated poisoning for mother and fetus; fetal monitoring recommended when feasible.	High doses can cause muscle weakness, respiratory depression, tachycardia, hypertension, vision disturbances; management is supportive with airway and cardiovascular monitoring, dose reduction or interruption; no specific antidote.	Store below 25°C, protect from light; reconstitute/dilute according to manufacturer’s instructions; use solution soon after preparation; discard unused portion.	
Glycopyrronium bromide	Injection 500mcg/ml	Quaternary ammonium antimuscarinic agent with minimal CNS penetration used to reduce secretions and block muscarinic side effects of anticholinesterases.	Competitive antagonist at peripheral muscarinic receptors, especially M1 and M3 in salivary glands, airways, and GI tract; limited ability to cross blood–brain barrier, so central anticholinergic effects are reduced compared with Atropine.	Pre-anesthetic reduction of salivary and respiratory secretions; prevention/treatment of bradycardia during anesthesia; reversal of muscarinic side effects during neuromuscular blockade reversal with Neostigmine; adjunct in peptic ulcer disease and sialorrhea (other formulations).	Hypersensitivity; glaucoma (especially narrow-angle); urinary retention; severe ulcerative colitis or toxic megacolon; obstructive uropathy; caution in coronary artery disease and arrhythmias.	Dry mouth, reduced sweating, tachycardia, blurred vision, urinary retention, constipation; less CNS toxicity than Atropine but confusion can still occur, particularly in frail elderly; potential for bronchial mucus inspissation.	Pre-anesthetic: adults 0.2–0.4 mg IV/IM 30–60 minutes before induction. With Neostigmine for neuromuscular blockade reversal: commonly 0.2 mg glycopyrronium per 1 mg Neostigmine IV, given concurrently. Doses adjusted per local anesthesia protocols; pediatric dosing is weight-based (e.g., 4–9 mcg/kg).	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Neostigmine – routinely co-administered to blunt Neostigmine-induced bradycardia and secretions during reversal of atracurium, cisatracurium, rocuronium, suxamethonium residual block; inappropriate imbalance can cause bradycardia (too little glycopyrronium) or excessive tachycardia (too much). MODERATE-INTRA: Other anticholinergics (Atropine, Hyoscine butyl bromide, Bezhexol, Procyclidine, Promethazine, Cyclizine) – additive peripheral anticholinergic burden (urinary retention, ileus, tachycardia). MODERATE-INTRA: Drugs causing tachycardia (Adrenaline, Dopamine, Dobutamine, Salbutamol, beta-blocker withdrawal) – synergistic HR increase; monitor hemodynamics.	Safer than Atropine regarding CNS effects, but elderly still at increased risk for urinary retention, constipation, and confusion; use lowest effective dose and monitor.	Commonly used IV/IM as premedication and during neuromuscular reversal; weight-based dosing; monitor for tachycardia, urinary retention, and constipation.	Generally avoid routine use in pregnancy unless required for anesthesia or neuromuscular reversal; limited systemic exposure with single doses; benefits in peri-operative setting often outweigh risks.	Excessive dosing may cause pronounced anticholinergic syndrome (tachycardia, urinary retention, ileus, hyperthermia, confusion); management supportive with monitoring; physostigmine rarely considered if severe central signs develop, under specialist guidance.	Store at room temperature (<25°C), protected from light; do not freeze; keep in original carton until use.	
N-acetylcysteine	Solution 20%	Acetylated cysteine derivative used as antidote for paracetamol poisoning and as a mucolytic (other formulations).	Restores hepatic glutathione by providing cysteine precursor, enhancing detoxification of the toxic paracetamol metabolite NAPQI; also has direct free-radical scavenging and vasodilatory effects. As mucolytic (inhaled/oral forms), breaks disulfide bonds in mucus glycoproteins.	Primary antidote for Paracetamol (Acetaminophen) overdose (oral and IV protocols); adjunct in some other drug-induced liver injuries and in certain critical care indications (e.g., contrast nephropathy or ARDS in some protocols, off-label).	Known hypersensitivity or prior anaphylactoid reaction (relative; may still be used with precautions); caution in asthma or bronchospasm when using inhaled forms; caution in severe volume overload (IV infusion).	IV: anaphylactoid reactions (flushing, rash, pruritus, bronchospasm, hypotension), usually early in infusion; nausea, vomiting; oral: nausea, vomiting, unpleasant odor/taste. Generally hepatoprotective rather than hepatotoxic.	Standard IV paracetamol-overdose protocols: e.g., 150 mg/kg over 60 minutes, then 50 mg/kg over 4 hours, then 100 mg/kg over 16 hours (exact regimen may vary by guideline); dose based on actual body weight. Oral regimens also exist (e.g., 140 mg/kg loading, then 70 mg/kg every 4 hours for 17 doses) where IV not feasible.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Paracetamol (oral and injection) – N-acetylcysteine is the specific antidote for overdose; timing of NAC relative to ingestion is critical (best within 8–10 hours). MODERATE-INTRA: Divalproex/Sodium valproate and other hepatotoxic drugs (e.g., Methotrexate, some anti-TB such as Isoniazid, Rifampicin, Pyrazinamide; some antineoplastics like Doxorubicin, Cyclophosphamide) – NAC may offer hepatoprotective benefit in overdose or high-risk situations but also adds fluid load when given IV; balance needed in heart failure patients. OUT: Activated charcoal (if given orally together) – charcoal can adsorb oral NAC and reduce its bioavailability; separate doses.	Generally safe; dose is the same but monitor more closely for volume overload and anaphylactoid reactions; adjust infusion rate or dilute more if cardiovascular compromise present.	Same weight-based protocols; important in pediatric paracetamol overdose due to narrow margin for error; monitor for anaphylactoid reactions especially with rapid IV infusion; consider slower infusion rate if reactions occur.	Considered safe and life-saving in pregnancy for paracetamol overdose; use full weight-based dosing; early administration prevents maternal and fetal liver injury; benefits clearly outweigh risks.	NAC itself is rarely “overdosed” in a toxic sense; excessive dosing may cause fluid overload, hyponatremia, and more intense anaphylactoid reactions; management is supportive, including slowing or stopping the infusion, antihistamines, bronchodilators, and vasopressors if needed.	Store at controlled room temperature; protect from light; once opened or diluted, use within manufacturer-specified period; inhalation/oral preparations have similar storage requirements and should be kept tightly closed.	
Protamine sulphate	Injection 10mg/ml	Strongly basic peptide antidote used to neutralize heparin (and partially low-molecular-weight heparins).	Forms stable ion pairs with acidic heparin molecules, creating inactive complexes that lack anticoagulant activity; neutralizes unfractionated heparin almost completely and low-molecular-weight heparins (e.g., Enoxaparin) partially.	Reversal of heparin anticoagulation after surgery or in cases of bleeding; partial reversal of low-molecular-weight heparin (Enoxaparin) effect; management of heparin overdose.	History of hypersensitivity to protamine; previous exposure to protamine (e.g., prior protamine, NPH insulin), fish allergy, or vasectomy (higher risk of anaphylactoid reactions); severe hypotension or pulmonary hypertension risk with rapid IV administration.	Hypotension, bradycardia, flushing, dyspnea, pulmonary hypertension, bronchospasm, anaphylactoid reactions; in excessive doses, can exert anticoagulant or antiplatelet effects and paradoxically increase bleeding.	Heparin reversal: approximately 1 mg protamine neutralizes 100 units of unfractionated heparin remaining in circulation (heparin dose in last 2 hours). Maximum single dose often limited (e.g., 50 mg) and given slowly IV over 10 minutes. For Enoxaparin, ~1 mg protamine per 1 mg Enoxaparin given within previous 8 hours neutralizes about 60–80% of activity; follow local protocol.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Heparin (Unfractionated) – direct antidote; dose based on heparin amount and timing. CRITICAL-INTRA: Enoxaparin – partial neutralization only; some anticoagulant effect may persist; monitor anti-Xa level or clinical bleeding. MODERATE-INTRA: Other anticoagulants (Warfarin, Rivaroxaban, Dabigatran) and antiplatelet agents (Aspirin) – protamine does not reverse their effects; combined therapy requires broader hemostatic strategy (vitamin K, PCCs, etc.). OUT: Vasodilators or agents causing hypotension (e.g., high-dose anesthetics, Dopamine/Dobutamine shifts, calcium channel blockers) – additive hypotension when protamine infused rapidly; careful BP/ECG monitoring needed.	Elderly cardiac/vascular patients are at higher risk of hemodynamic instability and anaphylactoid reactions; administer in monitored setting, slow infusion, and minimal effective dose.	Used in pediatric cardiac and surgical patients according to weight and heparin dose; careful hemodynamic and coagulation monitoring essential.	Use when necessary to reverse heparin after cardiopulmonary bypass or other procedures in pregnant patients; benefit usually outweighs risk; administer in a fully monitored environment due to risk of severe reactions.	Excess protamine can itself lead to anticoagulant effects, bleeding, and hemodynamic collapse; there is no specific antidote; management is supportive with fluids, vasopressors, oxygenation, and blood products as needed.	Store at 2–25°C as per manufacturer; do not freeze; protect from excessive heat and light; use opened vials promptly and discard unused portion.	
                                                        
Medicine	Presentation	Description	Mechanism Of Action	Indications	Contraindications	Adverse Effects	Dosage And Administration	Drug Interactions	Geriatric Use	Pediatric Use	Pregnancy	Overdose	Storage	
Ergotamine Tartrate	Tablet 1mg	Potent ergot alkaloid used in the acute treatment of migraine attacks, with strong vasoconstrictive and adrenergic effects on cranial blood vessels. It has historic utility but is largely superseded by safer triptans in many centers. Still included in formularies where cost and clinical familiarity favor continued use.	Ergotamine is a partial agonist and antagonist at various serotonin (5-HT1B/1D), adrenergic (alpha), and dopaminergic receptors. It causes constriction of intracranial blood vessels and inhibition of trigeminal neuropeptide release, reducing neurogenic inflammation associated with migraine. It also constricts peripheral vasculature through alpha-adrenergic stimulation and smooth muscle activation, which explains ischemia and end-organ risk in overdose.	Acute migraine attacks (with or without aura) when used early in the attack; occasionally used for cluster headache under specialist supervision. It is **not for prophylaxis**.	Severe peripheral vascular disease, coronary artery disease, uncontrolled hypertension, severe hepatic or renal impairment, sepsis, pregnancy or breastfeeding, hemiplegic or basilar migraine, hypersensitivity to ergot alkaloids, and concurrent strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (table or non-table) due to risk of prolonged vasospasm. Avoid in patients with severe infection or compromised circulation due to risk of gangrene.	Nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, paresthesia, muscle cramps, weakness, transient hypertension, bradycardia or tachycardia. Serious adverse effects include vasospasm, digital ischemia, Raynaud-like symptoms, myocardial or cerebral ischemia, infarction, gangrene, and ergotism with chronic exposure (vascular insufficiency, hallucinations, convulsions).	Administer at earliest onset of migraine. Typical oral regimen: 1–2 mg initially, then 1 mg every 30 minutes as needed. **Maximum dose**: 6 mg per attack or 10 mg per week (varies by guideline). Do not exceed recommended limits due to cumulative vasoconstrictive toxicity. Use antiemetic (e.g., Metoclopramide) if nausea severe. **Not for daily continuous use**.	CRITICAL-INTRA (TABLE): Beta-blockers (Propranolol, Atenolol, Carvedilol, Metoprolol, Labetalol) – ergotamine + beta-blockers increases risk of **severe peripheral vasoconstriction and digital ischemia**, because beta-blockers block compensatory vasodilation and unmask alpha-mediated vasospasm. Strictly avoid routine co-administration in migraine patients. CRITICAL-INTRA: Calcium channel blockers used for angina/hypertension (Verapamil, Nifedipine, Amlodipine) – theoretically reduce vasospastic risk, but in overdose settings may mask early ischemia and allow more ergotamine exposure before symptoms manifest; monitor closely. MODERATE-INTRA: Tramadol and SSRIs/SNRIs (Fluoxetine, Sertraline, Citalopram, Escitalopram, Mirtazapine, Amitriptyline, Imipramine, Clomipramine) – serotonergic overlap increases theoretical serotonin-syndrome risk if used repeatedly; risk is **low**, but clinicians should differentiate arterial vasospasm from serotonin toxicity. MODERATE-INTRA: Opioids (Morphine, Pethidine, Fentanyl, Tramadol) + ergotamine – additive nausea, vomiting, respiratory depression risk, and risk of aspiration during severe migraine. OUT-OF-TABLE: Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (macrolides not in your dataset such as erythromycin high-dose or clarithromycin, azoles like itraconazole, HIV protease inhibitors, or verapamil/diltiazem externally) – **absolute contraindication** due to prolonged vasospasm, risk of gangrene and fatal ischemia; if these drugs are added to the database, tag as CRITICAL.	Use caution. Elderly have higher risk of peripheral ischemia, coronary vasospasm, confusion, reduced hepatic clearance and drug accumulation. Maximum dose should be lower, and therapy should be **exceptional rather than routine**.	Not recommended. Higher risk of vasospasm and unpredictable toxicity. If used, strict specialist supervision with vascular monitoring is mandatory.	"ABSOL
"

Use caution. Elderly have higher risk of peripheral ischemia, coronary vasospasm, confusion, reduced hepatic clearance and drug accumulation. Maximum dose should be lower, and therapy should be **exceptional rather than routine**.	Not recommended. Higher risk of vasospasm and unpredictable toxicity. If used, strict specialist supervision with vascular monitoring is mandatory.	ABSOLUTE CONTRAINDICATION – ergotamine is uterotonic and vasoconstrictive, associated with miscarriage, fetal growth restriction, uterine tetany, placental ischemia and neonatal toxicity. Avoid while breastfeeding because ergotamine suppresses lactation and can cause infant cyanosis.	Toxicity presents with severe vasospasm, limb ischemia, gangrene, abdominal ischemia, myocardial or cerebral infarction, hallucinations, seizures and hypertension. Immediate treatment: discontinue drug, hospitalize urgently, initiate IV vasodilators (nitroprusside, nitroglycerin, nifedipine), aggressive limb perfusion support, anticoagulation when appropriate, vascular surgery consult for severe digital ischemia or infarction.	Store at controlled room temperature, protected from excessive light and humidity. Avoid heat degradation. Keep tablets in blister until use to preserve stability; do not store in bathroom or high-moisture areas.
